#5813. The Truth About Renewable Electricity: Your Questions, Answered

Reliable, affordable electricity is essential for many of the products and services that underpin the modern economy. But the sources of that electricity—and their respective merits and risks—are increasingly the subject of debate and polarization.

A flood of narratives and counternarratives is rising in the media and public conversation about the reliability and affordability of renewables, as well as their impacts on the economy, jobs, and communities. These mixed messages can make it hard to get the accurate information people need as they face important decisions about how they live, spend, or vote.

Here, we answer some common questions about renewable electricity, using the latest evidence, data, and research.

Renewable electricity is power generated from naturally replenishing energy sources, including solar, wind, hydropower, geothermal, and biomass.

Solar and wind are considered “variable,” in that their output depends on weather, time of day, and season. Hydropower, geothermal, and biomass are considered “firm” in that their output is reliably available on demand (although no power plant, whether it uses fossil fuels or renewables, is always available). Experience and modeling show that electricity grids can be reliable, affordable, and clean (low-carbon) by combining high volumes of variable and firm renewables with energy storage and other low-carbon generating sources.

Renewables accounted for around 34% of global electricity generation in 2025, with 15% from hydropower, 8% from wind, and 7% from solar. Wind and solar have grown significantly since 2015, when their shares in global electricity generation were only 3.4% and 1%, respectively.

Still, having been in use for more than a century, fossil fuels remain the dominant electricity generation source globally. They account for nearly 60% of the world’s electricity, primarily from coal (35%) and natural gas (22%).

Renewable electricity is not just growing—it’s skyrocketing. Solar and wind deployment in particular are expanding faster than any other electricity sources.

The growth trajectory is most evident in new capacity additions. In 2025, over 85% of new global electricity generation capacity came from renewables. The total global renewable electricity generation capacity has grown nearly 7-fold in the past 25 years, from 764 gigawatts (GW) in 2000 to 5,149 GW by 2025. Growth in 2025 set a record, with renewable capacity growing by 15.5%, adding an additional 629 GW within a single year.

Beyond installed capacity, it is important to understand how much electricity is actually generated. Electricity generated from solar and wind has increased more than 11-fold and more than 3-fold, respectively, between 2015 and 2025. In comparison, electricity generation from all fossil fuels increased only 14% during the same period.

Notably, 2025 marked the first year in over a century that electricity generated from renewable sources exceeded that from coal, the largest fossil fuel source. This signals a structural shift in the global electricity mix.

Solar has been the fastest-growing electricity source over the past eight years, roughly doubling every three years. In 2025, it posted a record increase of 600 terrawatt-hours (TWh), bringing total solar electricity generation to nearly 2,700 TWh. Together, solar and wind met 90% of global electricity demand growth in 2025, with solar accounting for 75% of that increase. Meanwhile, total electricity generation from fossil fuels declined slightly for the first time in 2025, by 0.2%.

Battery storage capacity has also grown exponentially. New capacity additions in 2025 were more than 150 times higher than they were a decade ago. Batteries allow solar and wind to deliver more value to grids, since they can store and provide energy for times when the wind isn’t blowing and sun isn’t shining.

Solar and wind are likely to continue growing fast and are expected to account for almost all new renewable capacity additions through 2030, according to the International Energy Agency (IEA). IEA estimates that solar and wind will account for 30% of global generation in 2030, nearly double their current share.

The three main drivers are cost decreases, enabling government policies and corporate purchasing.

The cost of electricity from some renewable sources has fallen dramatically over the past decade. One way of assessing energy costs is to look at the levelized cost of electricity (LCOE), which, roughly speaking, is the average cost of electricity from a power plant over its lifetime. According to the International Renewable Energy Agency (IRENA), the LCOE for utility-scale solar power and onshore wind dropped 90% and 70%, respectively between 2010 and 2024.

This dramatic decline in solar and wind costs has been reinforced by rapid improvements and falling costs in battery storage technologies. Battery storage is a key integration strategy to bring the variable output of solar and wind into the electric grid while maintaining reliability (more on this later). BloombergNEF’s data shows that the global LCOE for four-hour battery storage—meaning the cost of a battery that can hold and discharge four hours of electricity—is $78 per megawatt-hour (MWh), having declined 90% from 2012 to 2025.

Altogether, the combination of maturing technologies, larger project scales and reduced operating expenses has made renewable electricity a low-cost option for many regions of the world, while offering greater price stability compared to fossil fuel generation since solar and wind have no fuel costs.

Government policies are another driver of growth in renewable electricity. IEA reports that more than 130 governments have policies that encourage the deployment of solar, wind, and other renewables. These range from subsidies and other financial incentives, to purchasing targets (such as Renewable Portfolio Standards) to carbon pricing policies that raise the cost of fossil generation.

These policies help to overcome lock-in by fossil fuels, many of which have benefited from decades of supportive policies. The International Monetary Fund estimated that in 2024, governments provided over $700 billion in explicit subsidies for fossil fuels, or 0.6% of global GDP. While many subsidies are directly for consumers for things like transportation or cooking fuels, governments also subsidize the coal, oil and gas industries directly. For example, the U.S. government has recognized 16 tax measures benefiting upstream fossil fuels, with the largest exceeding $1 billion annually for oil and gas. These have been in the U.S. tax code for over 35 years, with one coal tax benefit dating back to 1918.

Corporations are increasingly motivated by the cost-saving benefits of renewable electricity, as well as their own voluntary commitments to decarbonize. Hundreds of firms have pledged and achieved the goal of using 100% renewable electricity through alliances such as RE100.  Large buyers such as Google, Meta, Amazon, and Microsoft are driving roughly half of corporate purchases of renewables, accounting for about 27 GW of new solar and wind capacity in 2025.

Cost decreases, supportive policies, and corporate interest are likely to spur strong growth in renewable generation for years to come. IEA projects that renewable electricity will grow by about 1,000 TWh annually through 2030, or 8% per year.

Solar and wind generation are variable, driven by daily and seasonal weather patterns. However, power systems have successfully integrated large volumes of solar and wind generation into reliable, affordable grids. Grids can incorporate an increasing share of variable generation through “integration strategies” such as:

Electricity prices in any specific country reflect a wide range of factors, including existing investments in generation, transmission, and distribution; the cost and availability of fuels; and a wide array of policies (regulatory, tax, market design, etc.). The affordability of electricity has become a central issue across the globe, especially as energy demand grows for data centers, transport, cooling and other uses.

Renewable electricity can help keep power bills affordable because solar and wind, often paired with storage, are the lowest-cost sources of new supply in many regions of the world. They have much lower operating costs than fossil fuel alternatives because they have no fuel cost and can be deployed quickly. This cost advantage is one of the key drivers behind IEA projections that solar and wind generation will grow faster than conventional generation from now to 2030.

Renewable electricity sources have far lower GHG emissions over their entire lifecycle than fossil fuel power plants. While fossil generators produce most of their climate impact during fuel combustion — and contribute additional emissions through raw material extraction, fuel processing, power plant construction, operation and decommissioning — renewables avoid the vast majority of these carbon‑intensive steps. As a result, sources like wind power carry only a small fraction of the lifecycle carbon footprint associated with coal or natural gas, making them much cleaner options for electricity generation overall.

According to a 2021 assessment by the National Laboratory of the Rockies, the median amount of greenhouse gases emitted per kilowatt-hour of electricity generated by wind is 13 grams (g CO2e/kWh) and solar PV is 43 g CO2e/kWh. Meanwhile, coal is about 1,001 g CO2e/kWh and natural gas is 486 g CO2/kWh. The lifecycle emissions of wind and solar are almost entirely attributable to emissions associated with their raw materials and manufacturing, which will likely decline further as industrial processes and transportation decarbonize.

There are lots of ways renewable electricity offers advantages over fossil fuels beyond just costs and climate impact, including:

The current energy crisis spurred by the war on Iran underscores the risks of relying on globally traded and unevenly distributed fossil resources. Fossil fuels are heavily concentrated in a few countries, whereas every nation on Earth has sunlight and wind, and many have hydro and geothermal resources. Expanding renewable electricity deployment can therefore strengthen national energy security and decrease exposure to global fossil fuel price volatility.

Some countries are already realizing this benefit. For example, following the Russian invasion of Ukraine in early 2022, the European Union recognized the security risk of its reliance on Russian fossil fuels. Russia supplied 45% of the E.U.’s natural gas, 27% of its crude oil, and 50% of its coal before the Ukraine war. In response, the E.U. launched its REPowerEU plan to rapidly reduce dependence on Russian fossil fuels by accelerating its renewable energy deployment, energy efficiency and energy supply diversification.

Conventional thermal power plants such as coal, natural gas and nuclear facilities require large volumes of water for cooling. Studies from the U.S. Energy Information Administration estimate that coal-fired power plants in the U.S. need to withdraw more than 21,000 gallons of water to generate 1 MWh of electricity, while natural gas-fired generation needs 2,793 gallons per MWh.

Meanwhile, solar panels and wind turbines require little to no water for cooling and maintenance. For example, the Solar Energy Industries Association estimated that solar panels use approximately 20 gallons per MWh for cleaning. Using solar instead of fossil fuels can therefore significantly reduce water use in regions experiencing high levels of water stress.

Research published in 2023 in the British Medical Journal showed that an estimated 5.13 million excess deaths per year globally are attributable to ambient air pollution from fossil fuel use. Renewable electricity can help reduce air pollutants such as particulate matter (PM2.5), sulfur dioxide (SO2) and nitrogen oxides (NOx) to improve public health.

At a time when electricity demand is skyrocketing from sources such as data centers, cooling and manufacturing, solar and wind can often be deployed faster than fossil fuel power plants. Among all the electricity generation options, solar stands out as the fastest to deploy, often within two years. Even under favorable permitting conditions, non-solar options generally take 3-15 years to get online.

Meeting new data center-driven electricity demand is a complex and rapidly evolving topic. IEA analysis projects that this demand is likely to be met by more fossil generation than renewables through 2030 in the two largest markets (United States and China), though at the global level, renewables are expected to take the lead. Large users of energy are also turning to nuclear power, pursuing existing and new operations, as well as restarting decommissioned plants.

This regional increase in fossil-based power is being driven by data center developers’ and hyperscalers’ unique demands. A big one is “speed-to-power”: getting generation online fast, since data centers can be built far faster than new generation can connect to the grid. While a solar or wind farm can actually be built faster than a natural gas plant, in practice, variable renewables can be harder to deploy quickly through the grid, due to poor policy environments, locational constraints, permitting delays, and extensive grid study and building timelines.

Even large-scale gas plants are coming online slower than data center demand — a problem compounded by a natural gas turbine shortage — so building on-site is often the fastest option. Here, fossil generation has an advantage: Though solar has more siting flexibility than wind, it still needs large land areas that major data center hubs often lack. The siting flexibility and lower land needs of fossil-based generation often make it the preferred choice for “behind-the-meter” or co-located generation.

The second pressure data centers face is reliability. These gigawatt-scale loads demand highly reliable, dispatchable power around the clock. The grid itself, drawing on many balanced sources of generation, is typically the most reliable option. But where grid capacity is unavailable or can’t keep up yet, developers are turning to fossil generation to fill the gap. While on-site solar and wind paired with storage can technically serve the same load at a similar reliability, meeting those demands would require significantly more land than is typically available near many data center sites. Even when grid connection is possible, fossil power, including diesel generators, still typically serves as backup power.

Making renewables and storage the default for data centers will require faster grid modernization, along with solutions tailored to data centers’ unique characteristics. Critically, much of the disadvantage faced by renewables is policy-driven rather than inherent in the technology itself. Streamlining permitting and interconnection processes would let solar and wind deploy far faster, making them a more competitive option for meeting new data center demand.

Jobs in electricity generation from solar and wind are already outpacing many other generation sources, particularly fossil fuels. In 2024, around 14 million people worked in the electricity generation sector globally, with solar and wind generation accounting for 48% of the entire electricity workforce. Fossil fuel-based jobs comprised only 26%. Solar energy is the largest electricity subsector for creating new jobs. According to the IEA, solar PV created nearly 4 times more jobs than power generation from oil and gas worldwide in 2024. Jobs in coal power generation declined in the same year.

If we zoom in on the United States, the same trends apply. According to the 2025 U.S. Energy & Employment Report from the U.S. Department of Energy, solar and wind comprised 54% of all jobs in the electricity generation sector in 2024; fossil fuels accounted for 21%. Solar not only leads in employment but is also projected to grow faster than other sectors — at approximately 9.8%, vs. 6.8% for natural gas and 4.1% for coal.

Locally, however, the transition from fossil fuels to renewable energy can be a source of worry for employees and local businesses. In the U.S., jobs in oil and gas tend to pay higher than those in renewables, although both are higher-than-average wages. These are legitimate concerns that require proactive, thoughtful engagement to create economic opportunities in impacted communities. Some governments and businesses are using workforce strategies to ease the transition from fossil fuels to clean energy, including training and reskilling for fossil fuel workers. In Grimsby, England, for example, an offshore wind boom has revived its economy, which had suffered from a decline in the fishing and oil and gas sectors. Spain’s national Just Transition Plan is retraining workers from the coal industry for new jobs in the wind sector.

The impact on birds from wind energy is extremely small compared to other causes, such as buildings and vehicles. According to the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service, onshore wind turbines are responsible for 0.007% of human-caused bird deaths annually.

The impact is also low compared to other sources of electricity. For example, one study on the U.S. and Europe showed that fossil-fueled power stations pose a much greater threat to birds than wind and nuclear technologies, due to associated air and water pollution. Another study from the University of Geneva in Switzerland concluded that the onset of shale oil and gas production reduces bird population counts by approximately 15% in the U.S., while wind turbines do not have any measurable impact on bird counts.

There are also several effective ways for wind farms to reduce the risk to birds. For example, new technologies allow blades to turn more slowly, while wind farms can prioritize siting away from bird habitats and migratory pathways.

Modernizing electric grids, building energy storage capacity, and simplifying permitting are challenges many countries face as they try to scale up clean electricity.

Regardless of the source of electricity, grids need to expand quickly to increase reliability. Driving higher performance by modernizing and optimizing existing grid infrastructure is as important as building more. Investment in electricity grids is growing worldwide, but even more is needed, especially for high-voltage transmission. More reliable grids can support integrating higher volumes of renewable electricity.

When it comes to where that infrastructure should be placed, siting and permitting need to improve to speed the transition to the new energy economy while safeguarding local communities and nature. Streamlined administrative processes, such as setting adequate timelines for approvals and improving coordination within and between agencies, are essential. So are community benefits agreements, which can address stakeholder concerns and ensure clean energy projects produce local benefits such as jobs and infrastructure improvements.

Adequate supply of critical minerals is also a challenge for expanding clean electricity supplies. While necessary to meet surging energy demand, mining and processing these minerals can produce emissions, pollute the environment and bring risks to nearby communities, such as water contamination from mine tailings. Mining and processing are also concentrated in a small handful of countries, which can lead to geopolitical risk. Reusing critical minerals discarded technology can help minimize demand, while responsible mining can reduce risks related to environmental, social, supply chain and other concerns.

As governments scale up clean electricity technologies, they should work with businesses and industries as implementation partners and align markets to help those businesses compete in global markets. Public and private large buyers can play a major role in scaling electric vehicle fleets, increasing home and building efficiency, and boosting low-carbon industrial products, while helping to expand clean electricity to power these end-uses. Large buyers, as well as groups of smaller buyers pooling their demand together, can drive widespread adoption of clean electricity and low-carbon products through their procurement decisions.

Research shows that keeping some of the worst climate change impacts at bay requires global greenhouse gas emissions to drop to “net-zero” by mid-century. At the global level, credible modeling by organizations including IEA, IRENA and BloombergNEF show that reaching net-zero emissions by 2050 is feasible and affordable by.

The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) reached a similar conclusion.

In a world facing numerous geopolitical risks, the security and affordability of energy supplies are top-of-mind for countries, businesses and households. An economy dependent on fossil fuels is a structural economic vulnerability, leaving countries exposed to price and supply shocks whenever the next conflict hits. An economy built on home-grown clean energy protects against future shocks, provides economic resilience and ensures lower bills for consumers. Renewable electricity puts countries in control: it provides energy price stability, independence and long-term security.

#5811. BAAJA gives federal regulators ‘bigger hammer’ to break transmission blocks

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“I think the cost-sharing elements are by far probably the most important part of this bill,” Rep. Josh Harder, D-Calif., told Utility Dive.

BAAJA’s motion to proceed is scheduled to receive a cloture vote in the Senate on Nov. 9, according to the Bipartisan Policy Center. It increases FERC’s power by eliminating the federal right of first refusal for incumbent utilities, stripping their automatic right to build new regional lines, and requiring FERC to issue a rule requiring each transmission planning region to file a consolidated interconnection and regional transmission planning process.

The legislation also states that FERC can require public utilities to make cost-effective grid upgrades and determine a penalty for utilities that fail to comply within a timeline decided by FERC — including a potential reduction of their return on equity.

FERC must establish “rate treatments for regional and interregional transmission planning and cost allocation,” the bill says, and require tariffs that allocate the cost of doing so to customers that benefit.

“I think the cost-sharing elements are by far probably the most important part of this bill, and my hope is that can unlock a lot more collaboration as opposed to what we have now, which is kind of folks pointing the finger at each other,” Harder said. “We have seen, at least at the FERC level, essentially no new interregional transmission built in a long, long time.”

“That's unacceptable,” he added, “and it's just not doing the job to lower prices for folks. So I think it's going to make some folks uncomfortable, but it's absolutely necessary to incentivize transmission getting built.”

Chirag Lala, vice president of research and chief economist at the nonprofit Center for Public Enterprise, and co-author of a recent paper that proposed a federally coordinated macrogrid, said in an October brief about BAAJA that the legislation “gets transmission right” but noted that the interregional planning mechanism lacks a “‘forcing function’ in the event regions fail to submit a planning process — only a six-month extension, after which the outcome is unclear if no process is submitted.”

Advait Arun, senior associate for energy finance at the Center for Public Enterprise and co-author of the macrogrid paper, told Utility Dive in an email that in addition to including a “forcing function,” BAAJA could potentially be amended to add “more firepower” to the federal government’s transmission finance programs, such as the U.S. Department of Energy’s $2.5 billion revolving Transmission Facilitation Program fund.

Arun also suggested an alteration to the “federal support restriction,” which currently blocks DOE’s Office of Energy Dominance Financing — formerly known as the Loan Programs Office — from financing projects which have already received some other kind of non-tax credit federal commitment.

“One worry I have here is that there is no money in any of these proposals,” he said about the Build More Agenda. “Ultimately, a lot of the private investment that these suggestions aim to catalyze through targeted streamlining of regulations will still be at the mercy of a temperamental macroeconomic picture.”

However, Harder noted that the U.S. is facing a “lack of fiscal headroom that we're going to have at a national level over the next couple of years,” adding, “I think that a lot of policymakers on both sides of the aisle are going to be looking for pro-growth policies that don't demand enormous new spending."

When asked if BAAJA might be amended in the House, Harder said, “This is a pretty delicate negotiation that's gone on for over two years, and anytime you have something of this magnitude, it's certainly a little bit balanced on a knife.”

Harder acknowledged that tribal organizations have voiced opposition to the changes the bill makes to the National Historic Preservation Act, and there is “some disagreement about what they would actually mean in practice, so I think that is something that might end up being clarified. But … the vast majority of this bill, I think, will probably remain as-is.”

The Build More Agenda suggests expanding on programs like DOE’s Utah Frontier Observatory for Research in Geothermal Energy, which “has helped demonstrate a commercial pathway for enhanced geothermal,” and streamlining regulations such as requirements for nuclear plant developers to “use specialized nuclear-grade materials … in non-safety structures of a plant where using commercial-grade materials would be just as safe.”

In regard to public perception of the safety of nuclear energy, Harder said, “I think we've come a long way for sure, and I think one of the real pressures is that people see the cost of electricity has skyrocketed. And if we really want to electrify our economy and move it towards 100% clean energy, then nuclear — and geothermal as well, that clean, firm power — have got to be a really important bedrock.”

Some moves from the Trump administration to encourage nuclear development, Harder said, “frankly would have been better in legislation.” He said he would like a “2.0” of the Accelerating Deployment of Versatile, Advanced Nuclear for Clean Energy Act of 2024, to “incorporate some of the changes that the Trump administration has made at the [Nuclear Regulatory Commission].”

“But the way that I would characterize it is, move it from a zero-risk approach — where any new nuclear project presents more of a risk than the status quo — to a real risk-based approach,” he said. “You can't have any regulatory agency that doesn't want to build anything at all ... And that's the way that unfortunately the NRC has been for decades.”

#5808. Waymo for Business | Ramp

Today, we’re announcing that Waymo for Business is integrated with Ramp, making it easier for organizations and their employees to manage expenses when using our fully autonomous ride-hailing service.

For over a year, Waymo for Business has offered 24/7 reliability and convenience directly to organizations embracing Waymo for their transportation needs. Now, our integration with Ramp makes the experience even more seamless for businesses and their employees by reducing manual expense reporting.

When an employee rides with Waymo, their ride receipt is automatically connected to Ramp and matched with the corresponding charge, eliminating the need to find, upload and attach the receipt manually. For businesses already using Ramp, that means an easier way to incorporate Waymo into work travel while simplifying the expense process for employees.

To learn more about Waymo for Business or get in touch with our team to activate the Ramp integration, contact us here.

#5807. GE Vernova in $9B deal to buy high-voltage manufacturer as gas turbine orders slow

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The acquisition of Trench Group will help GE Vernova capitalize on rising electricity demand, company executives said.

GE Vernova delivered “strong financial results” in the third quarter, despite a slowdown in gas turbine orders and slot reservation agreements, CEO Scott Strazik told investors during a Wednesday earnings call.

Total gas turbine gigawatts under contract rose to 122 GW from 116 GW last quarter – an increase of just 6 GW, compared with double-digit GW quarterly increases earlier in 2026.

The company expects its companywide order backlog to exceed $200 billion by next quarter and to have at least 130 GW of gas power equipment under contract by year-end.

Strazik said GE Vernova expected solid growth in turbine orders and reservations in 2027, if not at the breakneck pace of the first half of 2026.

“We certainly continue to see real growth in our gigawatts on contract throughout 2027, but that maybe the quarterly profiles in 2027 are more likely to feel like 3Q and 4Q” of 2026 than like the first half of the year, which saw aggressive growth, he said.

Strazik touted GE Vernova’s planned acquisition of Trench Group in a $9 billion deal the parties expect to close in late 2027. Trench Group has more than 800 utility and hyperscaler customers for its bushings, instrument transformers and coils, according to GE Vernova.

The deal includes approximately $5 billion in backlog and framework agreements, some of which reach into the next decade, Strazik said.

The transaction is poised to boost GE Vernova’s fast-growing Electrification segment, which makes and markets a range of medium- and high-voltage equipment to utilities, independent power producers, infrastructure owners and heavy power consumers.

Trench already supplies GE Vernova with high-voltage direct-current equipment, and the acquisition will allow the larger company to offer “better integrated solutions” while compressing delivery timelines and “enhanc[ing] supply chain certainty for both businesses,” Strazik said.

“Lead times across the industry are increasingly important,” he said. “By bringing Trench's manufacturing footprint and capabilities inside Vernova, we expect greater visibility into production planning and improved ability to align this component supply with our customers' project schedules.”

Trench’s new North Carolina factory will be the first U.S. manufacturer of bushings rated up to 765 kV, “giving us an important capability within our U.S. footprint,” Strazik added.

GE Vernova expects to see continued growth in its gas power order book in 2027, Strazik said.

The company is now taking reservations for delivery in 2032 amid continued strong demand from a mix of about 80% “traditional customers” and 20% data centers, he added.

A favorable mix of higher-priced equipment and a greater proportion of aeroderivative turbines in the order book pushed third-quarter pricing more than 20% higher than in the fourth quarter of 2025 and kept full-year 2026 order pricing on track to rise 50% compared to last year, Strazik said.

But per-kilowatt pricing will likely dip next quarter due to fewer aeroderivatives and more 50-hertz units — which are primarily sold in Europe, Africa and Asia — in the sales mix, he added.

In a Wednesday note, analysts for William Blair noted the lower slot reservation agreements and softer pricing per kW, but said that agreements decreased directly due to conversions and pricing is lower due to “expected revenue mix.”

“While the reversal in trend was initially concerning, management noted no slot exits,” they said. “We believe investors should focus on the pace of new signings … we currently view this [pace] as normalization, not a peak.”

Julien Dumoulin-Smith, a senior energy-sector analyst for Jefferies, said Wednesday that the impacts of GE Vernova’s “soft guidance” for 2027 turbine activity could be blunted if the company continues to deploy cash in transactions like the Trench Group acquisition, which it expects to boost its longer-term fortunes.

GE Vernova’s struggling Wind segment turned a profit in the third quarter thanks to a 16% increase in orders, mostly driven by demand for onshore wind equipment and services in North America.

Offshore wind deliveries also increased in the quarter as GE Vernova nears the finish line on its U.S. offshore wind obligations. In September, the company and the developers of Vineyard Wind 1 announced that they had settled a lawsuit that threatened final delivery of the 806-MW installation off the coast of Massachusetts.

But Strazik and Kenneth Parks, GE Vernova’s chief financial officer, cautioned against reading too much into the quarter’s results.

“We expect to continue to perform very well in the North America onshore wind market, but it's a very small market right now. And although our orders grew year-over-year, that's off of a very small base,” Strazik said. “I'd like to see where the U.S. market goes over the next year and continue to evaluate how we invest in this business for value and/or continue to evaluate how we create value in this business.”

U.S. wind turbine orders neared 3 GW in the second quarter as developers raced to meet construction-start deadlines to qualify for the federal investment tax credit, according to data from Wood Mackenzie.

That credit is now mostly closed to projects that have not begun substantial construction, and U.S. wind projects must be placed in service by the end of 2030 to retain eligibility. Wood Mackenzie expects 39 GW of onshore wind capacity to come online by then.

Still, GE Vernova remains cautious, Parks said.

“We keep saying it's hard to call the inflection in this U.S. market based upon all the dynamics at play, but we will be ready to actually benefit from that based upon all the work that the team is doing today,” he said.

#5804. RE+ will host another clean energy job fair this year

Job seekers hoping to launch or advance a career in clean energy can connect with employers and explore new opportunities at the RE+ Clean Energy Job Fair on Nov. 18, at the Las Vegas Convention Center. Hosted by GRID Alternatives, the Women of Renewable Industries and Sustainable Energy (WRISE) and Green Jobs Board, the fair is free for job seekers, and everyone who registers will receive a complimentary pass to the RE+ Expo, North America’s largest clean energy industry event.

The job fair will run from 11 a.m. to 2 p.m. and attendees can connect with hiring companies. The fair is open to people of all skill levels.

“The future of clean energy is about building sustainable careers. When we connect job seekers with employers, we bridge the gap between opportunity and talent, strengthen the workforce, and ensure that the people who need access to these careers have a seat at the table.” said Lyric Darru, grants manager of workforce development at GRID Alternatives. “By connecting job seekers directly with employers and opening the door to the broader RE+ expo at no cost, we’re helping more people explore pathways into clean energy careers and ensuring the benefits of this growing industry reach more communities. We thank our partners and sponsors for their support in bringing this event to Las Vegas.”

In 2025, more than 37,000 people attended RE+, an event with more than 1,300 exhibitors from businesses in solar, energy storage, electric vehicles, grid technologies and other sectors. This year’s conference will take place November 16–19 at the Las Vegas Convention Center.

“WRISE is excited to co-host this important event with GRID again this year,” said Lauren Friedman, senior manager of programs and events at WRISE. “The continued success of the job fair year over year speaks to the lasting strength and enthusiasm for our industry. We’re glad to make these opportunities available to the WRISE membership and our larger clean energy community.”

Register for the RE+ Clean Energy Job Fair here.

#5803. Trump Rolling Back Methane Pollution Safeguards

The Trump administration has already delayed implementation of the critical standards for year+

Washington, DC — Last night during a rally in Texas, Donald Trump announced that the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency would roll back safeguards finalized in 2024 to limit methane pollution from the oil and gas sector. This dangerous reversal—revealed earlier this month by Administrator Lee Zeldin—would allow bad-actor corporations to release more climate-disrupting compounds and other pollutants into our atmosphere, jeopardizing the health and wellbeing of our families and communities.

In 2024, the Biden Administration issued landmark methane standards for the oil and gas sector that significantly reduce these and other pollutants from both new and existing equipment. These safeguards are based on low-cost, commonsense practices and technologies that have been available to oil and gas operators for years or even decades. For instance, the standards require operators to capture or use gas at oil wells rather than flare it; to conduct regular inspections to identify and fix leaking equipment at well sites and compressor stations; and to use zero-emitting electric pumps and process controllers rather than gas-powered devices.

Soon after taking office in 2025, the Trump EPA suspended the implementation of key provisions from the Biden-era standards, delaying their enforcement by up to 18 months despite no widespread evidence of compliance difficulties. Now, the EPA is preparing a proposal that would permanently eliminate some of the rule’s most important safeguards, including its ban on routine flaring at oil wells and its program to leverage third-party monitoring data to identify and stop particularly large methane releases known as super-emitter events.

The proposal would also shield low-producing wells from leak monitoring requirements, despite consistent evidence showing that such wells do not pollute less than higher-producing wells. Nearly one in ten people in the U.S. live in a county with over a thousand low-producing wells. These sources represent nearly half of the sector’s methane emissions and are primarily owned by large, well-resourced and highly profitable corporations.

In reaction, Sierra Club released the following statements:

Cyrus Reed, Legislative and Conservation Director of Sierra Club’s Lone Star Chapter, said: “No state produces more oil and gas than Texas, and no state has more emissions of methane and associated gases than Texas, and yet the state has no state-level rules to protect us from its dangers. This proposed rollback is an affront to the thousands of Texans who have depended on and called on the EPA to protect us, and yet this proposal is designed to let so-called low-producing oil and gas wells off the hook, even though nearly half of all methane emissions come from these low-producing oil and gas wells.”

Sarah Corcoran, Deputy Director of the Sierra Club Pennsylvania Chapter, said: “Policies that reduce methane waste are commonsense and cost effective. The Trump Administration is not doing anyone any favors by reducing these regulations, not the industry, not the environment and not the American people who will feel the health impacts of these decisions for years to come. Companies have repeatedly made promises to reduce methane pollution – but voluntary measures are often empty promises. We need strict, enforceable regulations in order to hold companies accountable.”

Antoinette Reyes, Sierra Club Rio Grande Chapter Organizer, said: “New Mexico cannot afford a federal retreat on methane. The 2024 EPA safeguards were designed to do basic, practical things: find leaks, stop routine flaring, catch super-emitter events, and require operators to use cleaner equipment that already exists. Rolling those protections back—including protections for low-producing wells that make up a majority of active wells in both New Mexico and Texas—would create a sweeping industry loophole. Oil and gas pollution is like secondhand smoke from the industry: communities are exposed without consent, and the people closest to the source carry the heaviest burden. With the Permian spanning both Texas and New Mexico, pollution knows no boundaries, which is exactly why New Mexico and other states must codify these methane protections into state law and make them strong enough to withstand political rollbacks in Washington.”

Ramesh Bhatt, Chair of Colorado Sierra Club’s Conservation Committee, said: “At a time when Americans are struggling with energy prices, the Trump administration is going out of its way to allow industry to pollute with zero safeguards or commonsense methods to curb dangerous emissions. Methane and associated pollution from oil and gas operators will add billions more to our health costs while causing more climate chaos. Fortunately, the state of Colorado has recognized this folly and has become the first state in the nation to fully adopt the provisions of the 2024 federal methane rules. The state is acting to help protect the people and environment of Colorado while also reducing costs, but these federal standards are so important because pollution does not stop at the state’s border.”

Mahyar Sorour, Sierra Club Director of Beyond Fossil Fuels Policy, said: “It took many years and carefully considered input from health experts, community advocates, and even the oil and gas industry itself to craft the achievable, common-sense methane standards. Yet, the Trump administration, with pockets lined by the biggest polluters, has unceremoniously worked to roll back this and other standards meant to protect our health and safety. This giveaway to fossil fuel executives is foolish and short-sighted, and reveals who the Trump administration really works for—its political donors.”

Methane is a potent greenhouse gas that has more than 80 times the power of carbon dioxide over a 20-year period, driving approximately one-third of the warming our planet has experienced to date. Each year, the U.S. oil and gas sector emits large amounts of methane into our atmosphere. Methane is emitted from oil and gas sources alongside other damaging pollutants, such as smog- and soot-forming volatile organic compounds and air toxins like benzene and formaldehyde, human carcinogens.

According to EPA’s analysis, the 2024 standards are projected to avoid 58 million tons of methane through 2038, as well as 16 million tons of volatile organic compounds and 590,000 tons of air toxins.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5801. Xcel Energy rolls out $70B, 5-year capital plan focused on renewables

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“We believe that we can, in fact, protect the planet” while serving growing demand, CEO Bob Frenzel said. The spending plan calls for adding 9.2 GW of renewables and 2.3 GW of storage.

Renewable energy will account for nearly three-fourths of new generation built under Xcel Energy's five-year, $70 billion capital plan that the company unveiled Thursday during its third-quarter earnings call.

The new plan — up 17% from its previous $60 billion spending plan — calls for building 9.2 GW of renewable generation, 2.3 GW of energy storage and 3.3 GW of natural gas generation. Xcel expects the renewable portion of the buildout would cost about $21.7 billion. The plan also includes 2,400 miles of new transmission and $4 billion for wildfire mitigation.

“These investments are critical to serve growing electric demand, to keep customer bills low, to deliver clean energy, and to ensure system safety and reliability,” Chairman, President and CEO Bob Frenzel said, later adding that Xcel “continue[s] to lead a clean energy transition” and believes its commitment to clean energy will attract additional data centers to its service territory.

About $7 billion of the capital plan will go to data center projects already under contract at Xcel. Xcel could spend an additional $10 billion for projects not included in its spending plan, according to Frenzel. That spending would cover the more than 15 GW that is included in pending utility requests for proposals, high-voltage transmission projects and infrastructure needed to serve the 4 GW of new data centers the company expects to contract before the end of next year, he said.

“As we think about future data center growth, our contracting principles are based on our longstanding commitment to ensure that the needs of our customers and our communities are met,” Frenzel said. “We believe in responsible development. We believe that existing customers should not subsidize new large loads.”

To finance the updated capital plan, Xcel will take on $26 billion in debt and issue $8 billion in equity, about 40% of which is already contracted, CFO Brian Van Abel said. The company also agreed earlier this month to sell gas and electric assets in Michigan — including 9,000 electric customers and 6,000 gas customers — valued at $150 million to Upper Peninsula Power Co. and SEMCO ENERGY Gas Co. Van Abel said the sale should close by the end of 2027 and that the price was confidential.

Xcel’s net income jumped 83% to $960 million in the third quarter, up from $524 million a year ago, the company said. Favorable weather, increasing electric sales and this year’s “heavy rate case calendar” all helped Xcel increase its third-quarter earnings, Van Abel said.

A July order by the Minnesota Public Utilities Commission approved a 2.9% annual rate increase expected to generate $211 million over two years. Xcel filed a rate stability plan in Minnesota on Oct. 9 that would freeze rates at this level in 2027 and 2028, as an alternative to the rate case it is due to file in 2027. In Colorado, a September decision by the CPUC approved a 1.7% annual rate that will bring in $184 million. The company expects a final decision on a rate case in New Mexico by the end of this year.

Xcel cannot yet estimate its total liability for the 2024 Smokehouse Creek fire pending litigation initiated by the Texas Attorney General's office that is expected to continue into 2027, according to the company's quarterly U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission filing. The company has so far settled 358 claims related to the fire for $410 million; claims by 141 plaintiffs remain unresolved. The Smokehouse Creek Fire burned more than 1 million acres in the Texas Panhandle. Liabilities associated with the fire could exceed Xcel's $525 million insurance policy, according to the SEC filing.

#5799. Nearly Half of EU Cities Do Not Yet Meet New Clean Air Standards: Report Shows Collaboration Is Key

BRUSSELS — Almost half of EU cities exceed air pollution limits set by a landmark EU clean air law, according to a new report from ClientEarth and World Resources Institute (WRI). The report raises concerns about the level of preparedness in cities across the bloc and outlines the changes needed across all levels of government to meet the new air quality standards, protect people’s health, and strengthen local economies.

The EU’s Ambient Air Quality Directive (AAQD) entered into force in December 2024, with stricter pollution limits to be met by Member States by 2030. According to the latest validated data from 2024, 181 of 394 cities analyzed (46%) exceeded the new limits.

ClientEarth lawyer, Emma Bud, said: “The new EU air pollution laws are a once-in-a-generation opportunity to protect Europeans from toxic air, but with almost half of cities falling short of the standards, alarm bells are ringing. We particularly need to transform the transport system in cities, reducing dependence on cars.  Urban authorities can lead the way on so many of these policies, but they need strong support to protect people, including children and the most vulnerable.”

The report explains that meeting the new standards—particularly on tackling dangerous small particle (PM2.5) pollution—will require systemic changes to how governments work together and address the root causes of air pollution across sectors, including transport, agriculture, energy, and urban planning. The key to this deeper level of change, the report argues, is strong collaboration between all levels of government, as well as across business and industry sectors. To achieve compliance, authorities will need to implement measures suggested by the AAQD, including:

Using case studies from across Europe, the report shows how strong support for city leaders is key to tackling urban air pollution:

Steven Bland, Strategic Lead, City Systems Europe at WRI, said: “Air pollution remains a major public health and economic risk in European cities—one that is compounded by heatwaves. Making real progress requires tackling pollution across major emitting sectors, embedding air quality outcomes into investment frameworks, and coordinated action from local, regional, and national authorities. The EU now has an opportunity to show that cleaner air isn’t just an environmental goal—it’s an investment in people and a stronger, more resilient European economy.”

Download the full report: New clean air standards for Europe: Implications for cities.

WRI is a global research organization that spans more than 50 countries, with offices in Brazil, China, Europe, India, Indonesia, and the United States. Our more than 450 experts and staff work closely with leaders to turn big ideas into action to sustain our natural resources—the foundation of economic opportunity and human well-being. Find out more at www.wri.org

#5797. States must take a critical eye to gas plants proposed over clean energy

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The key question for regulators is no longer whether utilities can simply find a reason to install another gas turbine, write experts from Environmental Defense Fund and Minnesota’s CURE.

Will Scott is the North Carolina policy director of the Environmental Defense Fund. Hudson Kingston is the legal director at CURE, a nonprofit rural environmental and energy democracy organization in Minnesota.

The data center boom has become a cash cow for investor-owned utilities across the country. Many utility companies are seeking approval for costly new gas plants and other fossil fuel infrastructure based on uncertain projections of future demand for data centers and other large customers. If those projections fall short, ratepayers are left with 30 years of payments for projects that are increasingly expensive to build and slow to develop. We can’t let phantom projections for new data centers leave regular ratepayers with the bill.

Historically, state regulators have given utilities significant latitude to pursue new generation. Yet, as heating and cooling bills become painfully unaffordable for families and local businesses, utility regulators are increasingly skeptical of proposals that could leave them paying for decades of dirty and expensive fossil fuel infrastructure, when clean, renewable energy and batteries provide a reliable, homegrown and affordable alternative. Indeed, a recent study found that batteries are now cheaper than gas peaker plants, globally.

While customers are forced to pay increasingly higher energy bills, utility shareholders are guaranteed profits on centralized fossil energy projects. The bigger and more costly the construction, the more money energy corporations make. Once new energy infrastructure is approved by state regulators, utilities can start recovering the costs through customer rates even before the project is built and in use. Because utilities make profits off the infrastructure they build, and not the energy they sell, executives have a financial incentive to keep building to benefit shareholders.

To be clear, getting ahead of potential demand is not inherently problematic. Investing in clean and affordable energy generation to meet everyday customers’ future needs for things like home heating and cooling and electric vehicle charging would lower prices and improve reliability. The issue is that utilities are using that incentive and uncertain future demand to justify spending customers’ money on new fossil fuel infrastructure that will raise prices and pollution.

Amid this surge in data center proposals, regulators must be proactive and diligent in protecting customers from imprudent utility spending on fossil fuels, particularly when clean energy offers an affordable alternative.

In the Midwest, Minnesota Power proposed large amounts of new gas generation as part of its plan to meet future electricity demand. Instead of granting the utility carte blanche to build more gas infrastructure, as is common in these proceedings, Minnesota’s regulators have directed the utility to explore less costly alternatives, including a scenario that adds wind, solar and storage instead of expensive and polluting gas power plants that would run afoul of the state’s carbon-free standard.

Wind and solar have been cheaper than gas plants for nearly a decade in the U.S. Clean energy keeps getting more affordable while the cost of constructing new gas plants has increased by 66%, and the time required to bring them online grew by 23% between 2023 and 2025. Despite that, in 2025 utilities reported plans to build 24 GW of gas plant capacity, representing a 570% increase over 2023. Utilities justify this build out of gas plants on the basis of projected data-center demand, rather than the current needs of households and local businesses. Not all regulators are buying it.

In North Carolina, regulators rejected a Duke Energy $584 million gas turbine that the company argued is needed to meet the energy needs of anticipated large customers, particularly data centers. Regulators found that Duke’s anticipated load projections were not justification enough to saddle customers with another expensive gas project, that the project put too much risk on residential ratepayers and that the utility had not adequately examined potentially cheaper alternatives, like batteries.

With profits driving spending decisions rather than what’s in the best interest of customers, utilities wielding monopoly power often deliberately squeeze out more affordable energy sources, such as wind and solar — which are also faster to bring online — when forced to split ownership of those facilities with project developers.

In fact, some utilities are actively working against cheaper energy options. Duke Energy was found to have artificially capped energy storage and solar energy in its modeling to justify the need for new, lucrative gas plants.

In Minnesota and North Carolina, we’ve seen what happens when state regulators use their authority to stand up to utilities and protect residents from unnecessarily high energy costs. Both states offer examples of how regulators can push back against costly gas projects and put the interests of ratepayers first. Speculative demand should not be the burden of customers, and customers shouldn’t have to pay for gas when solar and wind are cheaper.

The question for regulators is no longer simply whether utilities can find a reason to build another gas plant. It is whether utilities can prove that customers actually need it, whether clean energy and batteries could do a better, more cost-efficient job, and whether families and businesses should unnecessarily shoulder decades of debt risk to cover short term demand projections driven by data center developers.

#5794. Lack of wildfire reform is having ‘dire and escalating impacts’: SCE

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“While we remain confident that California policymakers will ultimately take action, delay into 2027 will have significant and costly consequences,” said Edison International CEO Pedro Pizarro.

Wildfire liability reform is key to the financial health of Southern California Edison, ideally in the form of a long-term solution that “includes a clear and durable liquidity source for funding claims resolution, and a true shareholder liability cap that is not connected to the Wildfire Fund,” said Pedro Pizarro, president and CEO of Southern California Edison’s parent company Edison International, on a Thursday earnings call.

Pizarro voiced many of the same concerns — such as credit rating stability and potential impacts to customers — expressed by his fellow California utility CEO, PG&E Corp.’s Patti Poppe, during PG&E’s earnings call last week.

“We estimate that the currently elevated credit spreads could cost customers an incremental $300 million over the life of the bonds we expect SCE will issue in 2027 alone,” Edison International’s Chief Financial Officer Aaron Moss said during the call.

In September, the California Legislature opted not to advance Gov. Gavin Newsom’s proposal to amend a wildfire reform bill to limit the liability of utilities. The news that lawmakers rejected Newsom’s proposal caused Edison International’s stock to drop by 23%. The session ended without a final vote on the legislation, but Assembly Speaker Robert Rivas said work on the issue would continue.

Pizarro said that while Edison International is focused on items like the shareholder liability cap, “we recognize that other pieces may be needed to develop a package that gains support from stakeholders and ultimately the Senate, the Assembly, and the governor in time to send a bill to the governor by the constitutional deadline of November 15.”

“If we do not succeed in 2026, we must continue the effort with the new governor and legislature in 2027,” he added. “While we remain confident that California policymakers will ultimately take action, given the dire and escalating impacts on customers and the economy, delay into 2027 will have significant and costly consequences.”

SCE and Edison International are named in around 2,000 lawsuits from 38,000 plaintiffs concerning damages from the 2025 Eaton Fire, according to Edison International’s third quarter U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission filing. Those plaintiffs include individual plaintiffs as well as lawsuits by subrogation and public entity plaintiffs.

“As of September 30, 2026, SCE had recorded $1.8 billion in losses” related to Eaton Fire settlements, said the filing. “SCE had also recorded expected recoveries from customer-funded self-insurance of $917 million, from the Wildfire Fund of $859 million, and through FERC electric rates of $70 million related to the settlements.”

Despite wildfire headwinds, Edison International had a “strong” year-to-date performance and has a strong outlook for the remainder of the year, said Moss.

On paper, the company’s third-quarter core earnings declined year-over-year from $901 million to $738 million, but 2025’s third-quarter core earnings “reflect a 55-cent true-up resulting from the receipt of SCE's general rate case final decision,” Moss said. “Looking through that item, the quarter reflected continued stability in our core operations.”

When examined year to date, Edison International’s core earnings for 2026 are $1.88 billion compared to 2025’s $1.8 billion.

According to the company’s presentation slides, “SCE forecasts investing $38–41 billion from 2026 to 2030 to support SCE’s wildfire mitigation strategy and clean energy transformation in California.”

Edison International’s 2026–2030 capital plan devotes 22% each to load growth/electrification and infrastructure replacement, 14% to maintenance and inspections, and 12% to wildfire management.

In response to an analyst question about the potential for Edison International to reduce its investment if durable wildfire reform is not realized, Moss said the company would “prioritize spending in safety, reliability, and compliance-related areas. And that just means, by definition, we'd have less for load growth and clean energy policy.”

#5792. SnapNrack increases domestic manufacturing with new Texas factory

Solar mounting and racking manufacturer SnapNrack, a subsidiary of Sunrun, will host a ribbon-cutting ceremony on Nov. 4 to celebrate the opening of its new facility in El Paso, Texas. The new operational site has onshored SnapNrack’s assembly and distribution operations from its former facility in Juarez, Mexico.

By bringing assembly and distribution directly into Texas, SnapNrack eliminates international customs friction and cross-border transfer delays. The El Paso facility leverages the I-10 and I-25 interstate corridors alongside the region’s “southern gateway” rail network to distribute products across the country.

Manufacturing and assembling racking solutions within the United States directly supports solar installers and project developers in meeting domestic content requirements.

#5791. Construction Materials Stand Out At Greenbuild 2026

Last week, I attended Greenbuild International Conference + Expo 2026 in the North Hall of the Javits Center here in NYC.

After being overwhelmed by shows like CES and InterSolar Europe, the smaller scale of Greenbuild was a welcome break. At the show, there were a few heat pumps and Copper brought their battery-backed induction range, but less emphasis was placed on renewable energy storage and energy efficiency than other events. Many of the people I talked to had not heard the emissions reduction requirements of New York City’s LL97, which was a major motivator of Build Expo in the Spring. I also did not see much media presence at the show.

However, what stood out at Greenbuild was an emphasis on green materials. Although some of these companies had appeared at other shows, I had more opportunity to discuss and really get into the details at Greenbuild. A few exhibitors stood out.

Durra Panel was the first product to really attract my attention. Throughout history, straw has gotten a bad rap in parables. In contrast to the “Three Little Pigs” folktale, Durra Panel is using straw to create a building material that is not only more sustainable but, in many ways, better than traditional materials.

The standard boards are 96% compressed straw and 4% recycled craft paper. The process uses heat and pressure to bond the straw together with naturally occurring lignin, with no added binders needed. In the process, the straw panel gains a 1-hour (60-minute) Fire Resistance Level (FRL). The panels are 99.9% VOC-free, but the resulting panel, which incorporates biochar, actually absorb VOCs from the environment where they are installed. Standard panels are 50 or 58 mm thick (~2”).

Due to the mechanical strength of the panel, it can replace a partition wall or form a walkable roof without additional reinforcement or studs. Compared to a stud wall, the embodied energy drops from 153 MJ/m2 to 12.6 MJ/m2. It simply needs a header and footer to be mounted as a wall. According to Durra Panel, these walls provide an 84% time savings and 21% cost savings versus traditional building materials.

In a place like NYC, where rented commercial real estate and offices frequently have partition walls added and removed with high labor costs, those savings could add up quickly. In addition, the walls can be dismantled and re-used, further reducing cost and environmental impact.

The technology was developed in Australia decades ago, but it is expanding globally. Due to the acoustic performance, it is often used to build recording studios. Durra Panel recently selected Brocton, Massachusetts for their first US manufacturing facility.

Roughly 40% of global CO2 emissions come from the built environment, more than all transportation combined. In addition, a large portion of agricultural straw is burned as waste, putting more CO2 back into the atmosphere than all commercial air travel, according to Durra Panel. As much as 3.7 Gt of CO2 could be sequestered if agricultural straw could be used in construction. Carbon is locked away for the life of the building. At the end of life, the panels can be recycled or shredded and composted.

There were also several companies touting timber-related products. However, even if forestry is managed properly, utilizing waste is still preferable to felling trees. Using agricultural waste for construction makes you wonder what other waste streams could potentially be put to use.

Up to 8% of global emissions come from concrete manufacturing. That is more than air travel and global shipping combined. However, concrete has clear mechanical advantages as a durable, long lasting building material.

ZeroTwelve is incorporating biochar as a replacement for a portion of cement. In the process, the company claims more carbon can be sequestered than emitted, making the concrete carbon negative. At the same time, the concrete can become stronger and more resilient, depending on the mix. In addition, the biochar gives the concrete a slate-like dark gray appearance, making it well suited for decorative applications like floors or poured concrete countertops.

Nearby Pozzotive showed another solution, replacing a portion of cement with powdered glass, or pozzolan, from post-consumer recycled glass in what they call “urban mining.” Currently, much of the glass that gets put in recycling bins ends up in landfills. By eliminating the need to sort by color and size, more glass can be recycled. And it can be manufactured locally with low energy requirements, close to where the waste glass is generated. In the process, 94% of CO2 emissions are eliminated vs. the cement being replaced. The resulting pozzolan powdered glass can replace up to 50% of cement. The resulting concrete is stronger and more chemical resistant. In addition, the powdered glass makes the concrete more reflective, potentially reducing heat island effect.

Beyond the cement itself, Glavel showed off their foamed glass aggregate. Made from recycled glass using renewable energy, the aggregate both replaces crushed stone in the concrete and provides a level of insulation to replace separate foam insulation. It also makes the concrete structure lighter while maintaining high compressive strength.

There were many more options for building materials at the show. Something like a composite may have lower initial carbon footprint and durability but create challenges at end of life. Some natural materials may have degradability benefits but durability challenges. Each has its tradeoffs, and what works for one application may not be ideal in another. However, there is clear potential for the different materials to fit different applications and priorities..

While at the show, I briefly met with “legend” David Gottfried, co-founder of the US Green Building Council and founder of the World Green Building Council. He was promoting his new book, Regen360, which is positioned as “An Operating System For A Regenerative Earth.” While I have just started the book, it poses frameworks used to ask the right questions and align what we do with what we value.

I will not say that any material that I saw at the show is the answer. But if we change the way we think about building and start asking the right questions, green materials have the potential to contribute to a net positive impact.

Larry lives in Queens, NY, with his wife and cats. While he has spent much of his professional career leading global marketing efforts, his passions focus on clean technology and the automotive industry. He believes in Green Free Trade and the potential for the application of engineering talent to create solutions to the world’s most challenging problems.

#5789. What Europe Can Learn From China If It Wants To Save Its Industry

Something I learned from my trip to China: you can be fast followers if you’re willing to learn from the best.

Sometimes a bit of distance can improve your perspective.

Walking down the silent streets of Beijing I listened to the future of mobility. Statistics can’t convey the silence of the streets. When an old — usually German or Japanese combustion engine drove by, I heard and smelled China’s smoggy past.

As I checked my phone, together with your ID — your most precious possession in China — I learned France and Germany had agreed a deal to remake Europe’s auto industry.

Brussels-bubble-me might have thought this deal — and the slightly worse European Parliament copy of the deal a week later — was more or less what was to be expected. Seen through a rosy lens the deal maintains a degree of pressure on carmakers to electrify whilst introducing elements of ‘technological neutrality’ the political right so desired.

Perhaps lowering the pressure on carmakers while using local content rules to bar Chinese carmakers like BYD from the company car market could be a clever ploy? It might just buy European industry some time.

But seen from China the car deal is simply terrible and displays exactly the lack of leadership and consistency that Chinese policymakers so deride in western politicians. One of my interlocutors said, “you have politicians, we have statesmen”. I pushed back, spoke up for Europe, but painful as it may be, these days, those words have a ring of truth to them.

In China two-thirds of all new cars are electric. Its car factories churn out the most amazing vehicles. Software defined, developed in record time, with outstanding infotainment and AI features, and electric of course.

Trucks aren’t going electric in some distant future as truck manufacturer bosses in Europe like to say. 40% of sales in China are electric already, with battery swapping not just some utopia but a market reality with trucks queuing at stations waiting for a six minute swap.

Oil demand from road transport is in freefall. Urban pollution caused by vehicles is a thing of the past. And as a result of its clear eyed policies and generous support, as well as a little help by Trump and Putin’s oil shocks, Chinese carmakers are now conquering the world.

Underpinning China’s EV might is a world class battery industry. I was in the world’s battery capital, Ningde, once a small fishing town, now a city of nearly 3 million people. The city produces 300gWh worth of battery capacity — more than the whole of Europe. That factory boom town could have been in Germany or Spain, I mused.

The way the China EV story is often told this was all inevitable. But it never was.

China once too had a legacy, combustion engine and oil industry that was saying “electric will never work”. Yes, some of those companies were foreign but they all had joint ventures and were locally embedded just like Toyota and Ford are in Europe. Yes, some visionaries were thinking of leapfrogging, but they were a minority for a long time.

China looked at its interests and set out its course. Pollution, oil dependency, climate and industrial strategy all merged into one coherent strategy. Now it is reaping the rewards.

What a contrast with the stitch up back in Europe, which one Financial Times reporter labelled ‘self-destructive.’ The deal means Europe will produce fewer electric cars — just 10% extra compared to today’s sales share by 2030, and it all but abandons the idea of a European battery industry (10% EU batteries required in 2028). Instead it imposes local content rules to protect European producers of seats and wheels.

In an obscene example of political disorientation, the deal creates a new category of “zero emission” vehicles powered exclusively by synthetic e-fuels and biofuels. I asked my Chinese partners why they didn’t do biofuels. “We don’t like solutions that compete with food” was the simple answer.

China is all in on electrification. CATL is building up its presence in Europe with three sites in Germany, Spain and Hungary. They are even branching out into shipping and even aviation as it seeks to disrupt another industry. Soon medium sized containerships with range big enough to cover the Mediterranean will hit the water. Made in … China, of course.

When I noted to CATL engineers that the technical challenges involved sounded hard, the reply was, “yes, but we’re only getting started”. If only this would have been the attitude of European industry.

No-one I met here dismissed Europe or its auto industry. I accept that it’s hard for incumbents — companies or continents — to be ‘first’ or ‘disruptive’. But if we can learn one thing from China it’s that you can be fast followers if you’re willing to learn from the best.

China and its EV industry aren’t invincible. Its economy is heavily indebted (300% debt-to-GDP), stagnant and relies on exports for growth. The auto price war means almost no-one makes any money. Nor is its industry a monolithic bloc. Some make very average cars, others make excellent cars, CATL doesn’t make cars at all. The Chinese export model will work only as long as its trade partners accept it.

What is crystal clear though is European industry can’t meet Chinese competition with old gas guzzlers. Those days are over. The future is for those who master battery tech and software, including self driving. The race doesn’t have to be over. Just remember, five years ago China was nowhere.

But Europe’s leaders will need to display a lot more vision and consistency if they want their industry, or indeed Europe itself, to be at the table rather than on the menu in the years ahead.

By William Todts, Executive Director, T&E

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.

#5787. Hitachi’s nuclear revenue rises as SMR delivery date approaches

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The bulk of the Japanese conglomerate’s Energy segment business is in electrical grid equipment. Sales are strong, but inflation and long delivery timelines bear watching, company executives said.

Power grid equipment continued to make up the vast majority of Hitachi, Ltd.’s Energy segment revenues in the most recent quarter, but its nuclear division saw a 48% year-over-year increase as the company prepares to deliver the first of four small modular reactors to Ontario Power Generation in 2030.

The 300-MW BWRX-300 small modular reactors are being developed in partnership with GE Vernova.

In the United States, the Tennessee Valley Authority has tapped the joint venture to build at least one BWRX-300 in its territory, with delivery set for 2032.

“With the start of the construction of the first [SMR] in Canada as a foothold, we will advance the deployment of the SMR business in North America, Poland and other parts of Europe,” Hitachi said in its 2025 annual report.

However, it also spoke generally about the potential risks the company faces as it takes on ambitious projects around the world, without mentioning its nuclear investment specifically.

“High-risk projects, such as long-term projects or projects involving new technologies … may result in significant changes to the assumptions underlying the estimates related to those contracts … or their termination,” the company said.

The Japanese conglomerate’s Energy segment is divided into Nuclear Energy and Power Grids, which manufactures and markets high-voltage components, power conversion products, energy storage systems and other electrical equipment for commercial, industrial and utility customers.

Its Power Grid division saw revenues of ¥893.7 billion, or about $5.7 billion, in the most recent quarter — a 27% increase over the same period last year, the company said this week. Nuclear Energy took in ¥65.3 billion, or about $415 million, over the same period.

In total, Hitachi’s Energy segment saw a 28% increase in revenue and a nearly 50% increase in orders, the company said. Significant drivers included large-scale power infrastructure projects and demand from data center customers, with “notable strength” in North America.

Tomomi Kato, Hitachi’s chief financial officer, told investors and stock analysts on Wednesday that the company has seen broad-based order growth thanks to the data center boom. Those orders will be reflected in company revenues beginning next year, he said.

The Energy segment reported second-quarter orders of ¥1,566.2 billion, or nearly $10 billion, with particular strength from transmission and data center customers.

Speaking in Japanese, Tomomi said the conglomerate has seen no signs of a pullback in AI-related investment.

“So far, we are not seeing any delays or any payment issues,” he said through a translator.

Some of the company’s other segments are also increasing their focus on the energy sector.

Anand Birje, CEO of Hitachi’s newly consolidated, California-based digital services business, said the conglomerate was using its proprietary technology platform to improve performance and productivity for its energy customers.

The company says HMAX Energy, its energy-oriented digital solution, can help its customers reduce revenue loss from equipment downtime by up to 60%.

“There’s massive transformation happening [in] everything from transmission to distribution to pricing to load management using data, cloud and AI, along with our ecosystem partners,” Birje said in English.

One of those partners is chipmaking giant NVIDIA. Last October, Hitachi said it would provide “grid-to-rack architecture” to the 800-volt data center architecture NVIDIA and its customers expect to become the industry standard for high-performance computing facilities.

With its order backlog swelling thanks to AI and general electrification demand, Tomomi said Hitachi was doing its best to meet delivery timelines — and manage cost pressures in an inflationary environment.

On long-lead contracts, “we use indexing” to ensure Hitachi can pass along cost increases during production and fulfillment, he said.

“Otherwise, we cannot accept a long-term contract …. I think we are doing a good job managing the process,” Tomomi said.
