#5785. Rethinking the outage map for a multi-hazard world

For many, fall means sweater weather and all things pumpkin spice. For utilities, these months have other, less festive sides. Hurricane season, wildfire season and the start of the heating season all converge in the same 8-12-week window.

Hurricane season may be quieter this year, thanks to El Niño, but data shows climate change has intensified Atlantic hurricanes. Meanwhile, fall is peak wildfire season, particularly in California, when Santa Ana winds kick up. And the northern half of the country is switching furnaces back on after months of inactivity. Individually, these are known risks utilities plan for. Layered together, they're a bigger threat.

Because of this, utilities are starting to ask more of their outage maps, moving beyond "when will my power be back on?" to "what's happening around me?" Maps are becoming less of a status tracker and more of a situational awareness tool.

For decades, utility outage maps told customers when their power would be back on, along with repair status and outage cause. That’s still valuable, but outages aren’t the only thing causing sleepless nights. Hazards now stack and planned maintenance often lands in the same week and region as storms, wildfires and seasonal gas-related risk.

Historically, customers pieced this information together from disconnected sources like text alerts, county web pages, weather apps and wildfire trackers. Now, hazard layers bring it together in one place, drawing on GIS, OMS and weather feeds.

This shift isn’t just about the growing frequency and overlap of hazards. It also reflects rising customer expectations for self-service, with the map as their first stop before the call center.

During a crisis, utility customers want access to whichever channel responds fastest and most clearly. If the utility’s map can’t deliver, their next stop is usually the already strained call center.

Utilities say they want the same thing. Chartwell's 2024 benchmark survey found that 77% named reducing call center and crew impacts as a strategic objective and 93% named satisfying customers as a strategic objective. The stakes are real. Annual call center cost is $20-40 million for a typical two-million-customer utility fielding two to three million calls a year.

What does this look like in practice? During a major winter storm, Rappahannock Electric Cooperative added richer hazard context to its outage map, adding outage polygons to outline affected areas and damage photos to explain long restoration times. The result: 500,000+ views in two days and an average session time of approximately 12 minutes, well above the global benchmark of 2.5 minutes. This shows customers used the map as a primary source, not a quick check-in.

It wasn’t an outlier. During a major multi-state storm, outage maps across affected utilities drew 9.78 million views in three days. Mobile traffic climbed from 75% to 86%, as customers checked and rechecked maps from their phones. Every customer who self-serves on the map is one less person tying up phone lines.

Visibility upgrades only matter if everyone can benefit. Language, disability and access barriers mean some customers still get left behind in a crisis. Addressing these challenges means making map information clear and in actionable language across all channels.

Text, email and voice reach different populations in different ways. During Hurricane Idalia, opt-in data to alerts showed just that: text and email led at 45.3% and 40.3%, respectively, with voice trailing at 14.4%, underscoring the value of offering multiple channels, delivered in language customers can actually act on.

Channel reach is only half the equation; plain language is the other. Most utilities are still catching up. Very few have fully simplified their messaging or removed jargon. The message needs to be clear to work.

Uptime and map refresh rates still matter, but next-gen outage maps will be judged on more than restoration times. As storms, wildfires and gas risks pile up alongside planned maintenance, utilities need to keep every customer informed, not just report on uptime. That means investing in multiple communication channels. As the Idalia data shows, each channel reaches a different slice of the customer base and no single channel is enough alone.

Treating hazard visibility, communication equity and channel diversity as one problem, not three, is what builds trust.

#5783. Scaling European Battery Recycling via the Circular Economy Act

Ahead of the Circular Economy Act, a joint letter from T&E, Recharge, Clean Tech for Europe and EIES calls for concrete measures to support the effective scaling of Europe’s material recovery and recycling capacity.

Recovering materials from end-of-life batteries is a key means to secure much needed critical minerals (CRMs) resiliently and sustainably. With increased recycling, Europe can reduce imports of CRMs, in turn reducing external dependencies. However, Europe’s battery recycling industry is struggling to scale, with over half of Europe’s battery recycling capacity currently at risk. The Circular Economy Act must introduce measures to help scale Europe’s battery recycling capacity.

European battery recyclers lack the needed feedstock. Europe currently preprocesses most battery waste domestically but exports the majority of black mass — CRM rich concentrate from shredded lithium-ion batteries — for final refining abroad. Analysis shows at least 60% of Europe’s black mass is leaving the EU, with this likely only the tip of the iceberg, and lots more black mass leaving the EU, hidden in different customs codes, and unable to be traced. This structural leakage undermines EU strategic autonomy, shifts high-value jobs abroad, weakens circularity and environmental protection.

Faced with financial pressures and higher energy costs, European recyclers also need strong political and demand signals in Europe, in order to encourage investment in EU refining capacity. This will be key to ensuring we can process and keep valuable materials in Europe.

As a result, the upcoming Circular Economy Act must urgently address Europe’s black mass leakage problem by:

The CEA must also support the scaling of European recycling capacity, encouraging investment in EU refining capacity with:

The Circular Economy Act can achieve a shift in mindset, and deliver much needed tools to support Europe’s battery recycling industry, securing effective material recovery capacity in Europe.

#5781. SEG Solar plans HJT cell plant next to existing Texas facilities

SEG Solar, a global solar manufacturer, has signed a land agreement for a planned $950 million, 4-GW heterojunction (HJT) solar cell plant at the Tomball Industrial Campus in the greater Houston area.

The facility is expected to create more than 700 local jobs and is planned to come online in two 2 GW phases scheduled for 2028 and 2029. SEG reportedly plans to manufacture HJT cells, modules and other key components are made in one place.

The facility will be located adjacent to SEG’s existing 4 GW Tomball #1 module manufacturing facility and its 4.6 GW Tomball #2 module manufacturing facility, which is currently under construction. The company hopes to produce modules with HJT technology to help solar projects meet domestic content requirments.

“Bringing HJT cell manufacturing to the U.S. will give customers a more domestic supply chain with clearer sourcing and a single company standing behind the product,” said Jim Wood, CEO of SEG Solar. “As the only U.S. company dedicated to integrated domestic vertical manufacturing, SEG will continue to strengthen our U.S. operations and innovation. American-made cells, American-made modules, one company accountable.”

SEG opened its second Texas plant in August, and began assembling HJT modules from it the following month.

#5780. Utilities need to become project developers. Are they ready?

Load growth is no longer an abstract number in an electricity market report or forecast; it’s a reality. According to the U.S. Energy Information Administration (EIA), U.S. electricity load will increase nearly 2 percent in 2026 and 2.5 percent next year, driven by large loads like data centers and manufacturing as well as increased electrification of transportation and building heating and cooling. To put that number in perspective, annual load growth between 2005 and 2019 was about 0.1 percent, meaning the next two years will see nearly 50 times the growth that the industry experienced in the last 15 years.

Rapid load growth changes nearly everything for utilities, from how they plan and invest to how they engage and communicate with customers. Load growth is also compelling many utilities to do something they haven't done at this scale and pace before: develop their own generation. “Utilities are used to designing and building a gas plant once every five or 10 years, and then they would contract via PPAs (power purchase agreement) everything else,” said Alex Pope, Manager of Power Development Portfolios 1898 & Co. “But the systems utilities have in place to self-develop generation projects were never built to handle the volume necessary to meet the demand and timelines of today’s customer needs.”

As PPAs and BTAs (Build-Transfer Agreement) alone become insufficient to close the generation and load gap, many utilities that recognize the need to build more projects themselves face an important question: how do we start? One avenue is to focus inward and develop the internal expertise and resources needed to move projects from concept to commercialization—everything from land acquisition and permitting to financing, regulatory approvals, procurement strategies, and construction.

While this approach gives utilities complete control over the development of new generation, it also has meaningful challenges. One significant hurdle is the need to either augment the teams responsible for each phase of a successful new project or completely build from scratch. “You may have to quadruple your internal team of environmental specialists because you need to be pushing multiple projects forward,” Pope said. “The challenge is that everyone is trying to hire for these project-related roles and competition is stiff.”

Beyond adding personnel, what utilities also find is that the underlying internal processes and coordination needed to efficiently develop a large pipeline of generation projects simply don’t exist. Creating that necessary foundation involves hard choices about responsibilities and collaboration that inevitably take time that utilities don’t have if they are to meet this historic moment of load growth.

There’s another option, one that leverages existing utility expertise without requiring a vast expansion of staff. That option is to bring in a partner like 1898 & Co. to work alongside utility staff, increase their impact across projects, provide industry lessons and best practices from other utility peers, and act as the connective tissue between departments that need to collaborate for generation projects to move forward efficiently.

The rationale for the partnership is simple: utilities already have exceptionally capable people to handle environmental review, land rights, interconnection, regulatory approvals, and other aspects of project development. What utilities often lack, however, is a foundational infrastructure to ensure their internal teams work together rather than at cross-purposes across a large portfolio of projects in simultaneous development. This is necessary when utilities are trying to expand their self-development capacity from one or two projects per decade to a large and growing portfolio. “Their groups are industry best at what they do,” said Pope, who works directly with major utilities across the country to bolster the utility’s self-development capabilities. “But what they did was suited for one thermal project every five years. It was never set up to handle the throughput of what they’re being asked to do right now.”

1898 & Co.’s partnership approach is intentionally flexible, designed to supplement a utility’s existing self-development capacity and expand it to align with its goals and strategies. For example, Pope says some utilities start with development transfers, in which a third-party developer brings a shovel-ready project, and the utility completes due diligence before moving forward. This offers a lower-risk way to better understand the demands of self-development before attempting a larger portfolio of self-originated projects.

Other utilities have embraced full self-development, choosing to outsource only the capital-intensive step of land acquisition while keeping the rest of project management in-house. Utilities like Xcel Energy and DTE Energy, with support from 1898 & Co, leverage a blend development-transfer, self-development, PPA, and BTA structures to avoid putting all their eggs in one basket. No matter what model a utility chooses, Pope says 1898 & Co. provides a “single source of truth” across a portfolio of projects. This is possible because 1898 & Co. screens potential sites, manages due diligence, helps steer overall strategy, tracks deliverables, and coordinates across environmental, permitting, interconnection, and land rights teams. That combination of coordination and analysis ensures that a utility’s internal specialists, consulting partners, and selected EPC firms can all work together as one cohesive self-development team.

The financial rationale for pursuing a partnership that enhances a utility’s self-development is also compelling. 1898 & Co.’s services not only help investor-owned utilities keep up with the demands of their customers but also create strong shareholder value in the process.

Ultimately, though, what matters is whether a partnership approach allows utilities to function as high-volume generation project developers. In a recent all-source request for proposals (ASRFP), 1898 & Co. supported over 60 gigawatts of generation projects for its utility clients. Of those, 22 gigawatts were then bid in competitive RFPs. The result: 10 gigawatts won.

#5778. Sure Looks Like Trump Loves EVs After All

Jaws dropped to the floor last year when the US Department of Energy handed US automaker Rivian a $4.5 billion loan guarantee to build an EV factory Georgia. So much for the President’s heroic efforts to stop the vehicle electrification movement in its tracks. To ice the decarbonization cake, federal laboratories have just brewed up a vast, scientific conspiracy to help private sector partners develop next-gen batteries that will help make EVs more affordable for the masses. Hold on to your hats…

If Georgia seems an unlikely spot for an EV ecosystem to arise, guess again. Despite his red-state status, Georgia Governor Brian Kemp has embraced the vehicle electrification movement as a critical economic development strategy for his state.

“Georgia leads the nation in EV battery and vehicle manufacturing investments, totaling over $31 billion and creating over 38,000 jobs since 2015 — outpacing even Michigan by over $10 billion in investments over the same decade,” noted the Georgia Network for Electric Mobility at the University of Georgia in a report last year.

Georgia’s electrification ambitions earned a fresh wave of publicity during the last months of the Biden administration, when Rivian nailed down plans to build a new factory in the state with a $6.57 conditional loan guarantee in its pocket from the Loan Programs Office of the US Department of Energy.

After Trump took office he promptly shut down the LPO, allowing all but a scarce few decarbonization projects to move ahead (here’s one example). Somehow the Rivian factory loan also survived the Trump chopper, though at the lesser amount of $4.5 billion, and a groundbreaking ceremony took place last year.

If all goes according to plan, Rivian will soon join electric bus maker Bluebird on Georgia’s roster of EV makers. Another potential opportunity surfaced last Saturday, when Audi CEO Gernot Döllner took to the campaign trail with US President Donald Trump.

That was not a typo. Last Saturday, Döllner appeared on stage at a campaign rally in Georgia with US President Donald Trump, during which the two of them announced the culmination of a years-long plan to build Audi’s first ever factory in the US, a $4 billion facility to be constructed in Peach County, Georgia.

The new factory announcement was somewhat premature, to put it kindly. The supervisory board of parent company Volkswagen has yet to sign off on the deal, and the news blew up in Audi’s hometown, Germany, where Volkswagen is laying off workers.

Audi has yet to release details about its model plans for the new factory, but over the summer  company Döllner articulated a plug-in hybrid strategy for the US market. So, there is Trump on the campaign trail, making the case for an automaker that aims to help electrify the US mobility business, if not in full then in part. Go figure.

In the meantime, a fresh wave of next-generation EV batteries is also moving ahead under Trump’s watch, in the form of BattMan,* which is catchy shorthand for the newly hatched Battery Manufacturing Accelerator under the umbrella of the Energy Department.

“DOE’s Strategy for American Leadership in Batteries establishes the Department’s priorities to build a fully integrated domestic battery supply chain by 2030, achieve U.S. leadership in battery technology and manufacturing by 2035,” the Energy Department explained in a press statement on October 29.

BattMan is a technology accelerator that builds on a long list of previous DOE efforts to push the envelope on next generation energy systems, going back to its roots in the birth of the nuclear era. “The accelerator will focus on lowering manufacturing costs, speeding the transition of novel battery technologies from laboratory discovery to manufacturing deployment, and boosting U.S. leadership in energy storage manufacturing,” DOE explains.

That’s…interesting! Ford and General Motors are among the EV stakeholders that pivoted into stationary energy storage after the market for EVs took a swan dive in the US last year, but DOE has more than stationary applications on its mind. Mobile applications  also make an appearance.

“From transportation and power grids to medical devices and consumer electronics, batteries are essential to modern life,” notes Assistant Secretary of Energy Audrey Robertson.

“U.S. battery demand across transportation and energy-storage applications is projected to nearly quadruple, increasing from 179 gigawatt-hours (GWh) in 2025 to 707 GWh in 2035,” DOE emphasizes.

The BattMan consortium launched with support from the DOE Office of Critical Minerals and Energy Innovation under the leadership of DOE’s Pacific Northwest National Laboratory. Coincidentally or not, PNNL also launched the DOE “Innovation Center for Battery500 Consortium” in 2016, an effort that specialized specifically in advanced batteries for EVs.

Other founding members in the new BattMan effort also claim experience in the EV battery field. In all, the list includes Argonne National Laboratory, Oak Ridge National Laboratory, the University of Washington, Binghamton University, University of Alabama, Arizona State University, and University of Michigan-Dearborn.

The National Laboratory of the Rockies (formerly the National Renewable Energy Laboratory) is another partner in the BattMan effort. NLR expects to contribute its extensive experience in materials assessment and characterization to bear, along with its track record in electrode design.

That’s no small potatoes. Validating battery materials for durability and performance is a time consuming, expensive, and somewhat nerveracking process, with innovators having to re-invent the wheel with each new development. NRL has the know-how and the equipment to speed up the process.

“NLR researchers will establish physics-based machine learning models to analyze how specific defects and heterogeneities might impact cell performance years into the future, generating reliable data earlier in the development cycle,” the lab explains.

“The consortium will also work closely with domestic companies across the battery and materials sector to test and validate manufacturing processes for commercially viable production,” NRL elaborated, calling attention to the technology accelerator part of the program.

“Ultimately, the consortium aims to develop self-optimizing manufacturing systems using real-time data, automated feedback, and digital twin models that link virtual and real systems to identify opportunities to improve performance,” NRL added.

If all goes according to plan, NRL will produce statistically accurate models based on microscopic measurements, making it possible for battery manufacturers to move forward without investing in specialized equipment.

AI will play a prominent role in NRL’s contribution to BattMan, though the lab uses Trump’s preferred “SI” terminology. Either way, Trump himself all but guaranteed an EV sales recovery in the US when he decided to bomb Iran last February. When that comes around, the next generation of EV batteries will be queued up and ready to roll.

*Not to be confused with the Danish stationary energy storage specialist BattMan Energy.

#5777. Exelon eyes $8B in energy storage, partly to address PJM supply crunch

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

President and CEO Calvin Butler said the grid operator isn’t adding generation fast enough, but the utility’s plan to invest in energy storage and transmission can help address the issue.

Exelon utilities could spend $3 billion to $7 billion building 1.5 GW to 3.5 GW of regulated energy storage, not including a $1 billion, 500-MW project already proposed for New Jersey, company officials said Friday during an earnings call.

“With regulated storage, we have certainty that projects get built, and control over when and how they're deployed,” Robert Kleczynski, Exelon’s CFO, said during the call. “We view utility-owned storage as one option alongside competitive development, not a replacement for it.”

Customers would benefit from the projects through lower capacity prices and the flow-through of investment tax credits, which would ease the assets’ effect on electricity rates, he said.

Three states where Exelon utilities operate — New Jersey, Maryland and Delaware — allow them to own energy storage facilities to bolster grid resiliency, Kleczynski noted.

Atlantic City Electric’s 500-MW project, developed with Invenergy, would be the largest battery installation in the PJM Interconnection and is expected to provide more than $700 million in net benefits to customers, he said. Exelon expects the New Jersey Board of Public Utilities to make a decision on the proposal late in the first quarter next year, according to Kleczynski.

PJM isn’t moving fast enough to add generation onto its system, which is the biggest issue facing consumers in the grid operator’s Mid-Atlantic and Midwest footprint, according to Calvin Butler, Exelon president and CEO.

Two key PJM initiatives — its backstop capacity procurement plan and its Interim Resource Adequacy Service proposal — have been delayed by only partial approvals by the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission.

“Neither proposal would have solved the reliability and affordability challenges facing our customers, but both represented important steps forward,” Butler said.

However, adding energy storage and transmission can help address the issue, according to Butler. Exelon’s current capital expenditure plan calls for investing $16.3 billion on transmission through 2029, and the Chicago-based company sees opportunity for $12 billion to $17 billion in transmission spending beyond that plan, including on infrastructure for large loads.

Exelon’s “high-probability” large load additions remained flat at 11 GW — with 9 GW in Commonwealth Edison’s service area in northern Illinois and 2 GW in the Mid-Atlantic region, the company said.

Its future pipeline slipped to 23 GW — 14 GW with ComEd and 9 GW in the Mid-Atlantic — from 25 GW last quarter.

On the financial front, Exelon’s third-quarter income fell to $729 million, down from $875 million in the same period last year, partly due to higher writeoffs related to a rate case, and higher storm and interest expenses. Third-quarter revenue jumped to $7.3 billion from $6.7 billion in the year-ago period.

Not including one-time factors, third-quarter adjusted income increased to 87 cents/share from 86 cents/share in the third quarter in 2025.

In a note, Jefferies equity analyst on Friday said the earnings results and the potential spending on storage and transmission, in addition to Exelon’s $41.7 billion capital expenditure plan, were “favorable.”

However, the analysts warned that regulatory uncertainty in 2027 dampens the outlook and may offset any incremental earnings from new capital expenditures.

Exelon has close to 11 million customers through its six utility subsidiaries —  Atlantic City Electric,  Baltimore Gas and Electric,  ComEd,  Delmarva Power &  Light,  PECO Energy Co.  and Potomac Electric Power Co.

#5776. Ireland’s Grid Flexibility Assessment Shows Why Location Matters

Ireland’s electricity regulator has put some useful numbers on the country’s future need for grid flexibility. Its first national assessment identifies about 234 MW and 58 GWh of potential upward flexibility requirements on distribution networks in 2028. Batteries, managed EV charging and industrial demand response could all contribute, but the figures become more interesting at the level of individual substations.

The Commission for Regulation of Utilities’ Flexibility Needs Assessment identifies a potential requirement of 0.6 MW and 3 MWh at the Naas 38 kV/10 kV substation in 2028, concentrated in January. Reinforcement is planned by 2030, when the assessment identifies no corresponding flexibility requirement. This describes a modeled network need, without a commitment to purchase a service or install a battery.

A battery elsewhere in Ireland could provide useful balancing services, help integrate renewable generation and earn revenue in electricity markets while contributing nothing to the particular constraint at Naas. The electrical connection matters. A resource has to change power flows across the affected equipment, in the required direction, during the periods when that equipment is constrained.

Flexibility must affect the constrained electrical equipment at the right time.

The distinction applies equally to demand response. A large fleet of electric vehicles might represent considerable flexible electricity demand nationally, but only vehicles connected in electrically useful locations can relieve a particular transformer. Their charging must also be adjustable without compromising the journeys they need to make. The same questions apply to refrigeration, heating and other industrial or commercial loads.

Timing adds another complication. Naas’s modeled 0.6 MW and 3 MWh figures describe different aspects of the expected requirement. Dividing one by the other does not establish that the network needs a battery discharging continuously for five hours. The duration of individual events, recovery between them and customer operating requirements all affect which resources could help.

There’s an important reality check in Britain. UK Power Networks’ March 2026 network assessment reports that the utility stopped seeking flexibility tenders for certain single-transformer substations whose constraints typically lasted 24 hours. The available flexibility market could not address those requirements effectively on technical and commercial grounds. In those circumstances, reinforcement or another engineering intervention may be more appropriate.

As distribution networks accommodate more electric vehicles, heat pumps, batteries and distributed generation, assessing flexibility at the right electrical location becomes increasingly important. National capacity estimates provide useful context, but investment and procurement decisions need much more specific evidence. The Naas example starts the first article in a series connected to The Flexibility Mesh, an open 99-page strategy report on how physical resources, permissions, operating capabilities and commercial arrangements fit together.

My full TFIE Strategy Briefing analysis examines the Naas case, the alternatives available to utilities and the conditions that turn flexible capacity into a deliverable network service. It also links to the interactive Flexibility Mesh architecture.

#5775. US to install 36% more wind turbines in 2025 than previous year

The United States is expected to add more than 7 GW of wind installations in 2025, a 36% increase over the previous year, and the five-year outlook remains unchanged quarter-over-quarter from previous forecast, according to the “U.S. Wind Energy Monitor” report released by Wood Mackenzie and the American Clean Power Association (ACP).

Amid a challenging market environment, the report shows that the United States is on track to add 46 GW of new wind capacity from 2025 to 2029, with total projected volumes unchanged quarter-on-quarter from previous forecasts. However, timing has shifted, as 2026 and 2027 will deliver significant gains, at 10.7 GW and 12.7 GW, respectively, as more assets advance through the development pipeline.

While Q3 installations came in 23% below forecast at 932 MW, the market has shown progress with 3.8 GW queued for Q4 2025 — representing 52% of the year’s total expected capacity. This back-loaded installation pattern is consistent with typical project-commissioning timelines.

U.S. turbine order intake has rebounded to pre-One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) levels quarter-over-quarter, supported by 2+ GW of firm commitments in Q3, the biggest intake in the region in the last nine months and a 79% quarter-over-quarter increase. However, true visibility remains limited as OEMs increasingly withhold project details and much qualifying “start-of-construction” activity occurs through off-site component manufacturing.

The market will then see a noticeable drop off, as 2029 weakens meaningfully quarter-over-quarter following project cancellations and inactive designations for late-decade capacity, driven by permitting and broader development challenges.

“The U.S. power market is facing mounting strain after a decade of flat demand, with utilities committing to 160 GW of large-load additions. This represents a significant opportunity for wind energy, which benefits from strengthened economic fundamentals and a compelling business case driven by its competitively low LCOE,” said Leila Garcia da Fonseca, director of research at Wood Mackenzie. “However, turbine costs remain elevated due to tariffs and mid-term wind growth will depend on resolving permitting and policy uncertainty.”

Power demand growth through 2029 is expected to average around 3% compared to just 0.7% over the previous decade, with data centers accounting for approximately 59 GW of the 90 GW total peak demand growth. This surge in baseload demand positions wind as a natural fit to meet rising power needs.

The five-year capacity outlook remains unchanged at 39.8 GW of added capacity quarter-over-quarter. The 2025-2027 pipeline is fully committed with all projects having turbine orders in place. More than 60% of the three-year capacity outlook has been commissioned or is under construction.

Activity is led by western states, such as Wyoming, New Mexico and others, that will account for 34% of activity in this time period. Major projects driving the outlook include Pattern’s 3.5-GW SunZia project in New Mexico, which will position the developer as 2026’s top installer, and Invenergy’s 998-MW Towner Energy Center in Colorado, the largest single project expected in 2027.

The market continues expanding geographically, with Arkansas bringing its first utility-scale onshore wind project online through Cordelio’s Crossover Wind.

The repowering market remains strong, as Wood Mackenzie projects that 18 projects will drive 2.5 GW of capacity additions in the next three years.

Wood Mackenzie expects the offshore installation pace to slow in Q4 2025 due to harsh winter weather conditions, pushing remaining capacity into 2026. Despite these near-term adjustments, Vineyard Wind has demonstrated strong execution, connecting 15 turbines in Q3 and delivering 200 GWh through nine months.

“U.S. offshore wind shows diverging momentum,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Projects under construction with COD estimated for 2026 continue to hit key milestones, but post-2027 developments face potential delays amid constrained wind turbine installation vessel capacity, driving delays and contract terminations.”

The offshore sector is also experiencing significant financial pressure.

The report highlights that uncertainty around tariffs is a threat to projections. Wood Mackenzie projects that tariffs will drive up turbine costs in 2026, before moderating in subsequent years. In total, U.S. onshore wind capex is projected to increase by 5% through 2029.

“U.S. wind turbine pricing is experiencing unprecedented uncertainty as conflicting market and regulatory forces interact,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Domestic manufacturing overcapacity relative to permitted project volumes, particularly after 2028, would normally place downward pressure on prices. Despite this, onshore wind costs are expected to continue rising due to tariff exposure on raw material inputs and subcomponents.”

#5774. Mitsubishi Electric seeks to expand North American grid foothold

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

In August, the Japanese conglomerate said it would spend $1.4 billion to buy PCI Energy Solutions, whose platform manages about 60% of U.S. power generation.

On Friday, Mitsubishi Electric reported a 20% year-over-year revenue increase in its utility-facing business segment as it prepares to launch an electrical components joint venture with fellow industrial firm ABB and acquire an energy management software provider to boost its exposure to North American wholesale power markets.

On Aug. 20, the Japanese conglomerate said it would spend $1.4 billion to buy PCI Energy Solutions. PCI supports utilities, independent power producers and other wholesale market participants in bulk power trading and settlement, generation and transmission operations, supply-demand planning and risk management, Mitsubishi Electric said.

About 60% of U.S. power generation is managed by PCI’s platform, according to Mitsubishi Electric.

The transaction, which is expected to close by the end of 2026, will help the combined company manage a North American bulk power system transformed by rising renewable energy generation, expanded use of distributed energy resources, greater power market volatility and a broad increase in electricity demand, Mitsubishi Electric said.

“These developments are increasing demand for advanced energy management and optimization solutions capable of supporting integrated forecasting, scheduling, trading, risk management, settlement and operational decision-making,” the company said.

Mitsubishi Electric’s Infrastructure segment houses most of the firm’s work for utilities and heavy power consumers. It took in 379.7 billion yen in the second quarter of the 2027 fiscal year, up from ¥316.4 billion in the second quarter of fiscal 2026, according to financial figures released Friday.

The company’s fiscal year runs from April 1 to March 31.

Both of the Infrastructure units serving utilities and large power consumers increased their revenue by double-digit percentages, with Public Utility Systems rising by 13% and Energy Systems by 18%. Together, they comprise about 70% of Infrastructure revenues, with the non-utility Defense and Space Systems unit accounting for the remainder.

Public Utility Systems manufactures and markets water treatment controls, communications systems and power supply equipment for large utility users. Energy Systems makes and sells power plant controls, substation equipment, electric transmission and distribution components, power electronics and power supply equipment.

The Public Utility Systems unit drew strength from “large-scale projects in the uninterruptible power supply business outside Japan,” while Energy Systems “saw robust demand …. from the expanded use of renewable energy and increased investments in data centers,” Mitsubishi Electric said.

Transmission and distribution equipment orders were especially robust, the company added.

Mitsubishi Electric’s just-announced partnership with ABB would create a jointly owned subsidiary to manufacture and distribute circuit breakers, electromagnetic switches, controls and uninterrupted power supply systems rated for 5 kilovolt-amperes or less, and to sell “certain high-voltage power distribution control equipment” and smart meters, the Japanese conglomerate said on Oct. 20.

“ABB holds a leading position in the power distribution and control field, and develops power distribution and control solutions by leveraging its technologies and global sales network developed over many years,” Mitsubishi Electric said, adding that rising electricity demand outside Japan necessitates a partner with deeper exposure to international markets.

Mitsubishi Electric expects the ABB transaction to close by October 2027.

In the first half of its 2027 fiscal year, Mitsubishi Electric earned 45% of its revenue in its home market of Japan, according to its second-quarter earnings presentation. Most of the remainder came from China, other parts of Asia, Europe and North America, with roughly equal shares for each, according to a chart provided by the company.

North America, home of Mitsubishi Electric’s Pennsylvania-based electric power products subsidiary, is the conglomerate’s slowest-growing major overseas market so far this fiscal year, according to company figures. North American revenue is pacing about 5% ahead of the same period in fiscal 2026, compared with about 19% for Europe and 34% for China. Japanese revenue is up about 10% so far in fiscal 2027.

#5773. PJM’s Emergency Order Should Be a Wake-Up Call for Michigan: Build More Power Before the Next Crisis

By Terence Parker, Michigan resident and Solar Mounts LLC team member

The U.S. Department of Energy’s October 12, 2026, emergency order directing PJM Interconnection to use additional generating resources should be a wake-up call for Michigan and the entire PJM region. Effective October 13–16, the order authorizes specified power plants to operate as needed for reliability and allows backup generators at large facilities to help reduce grid demand during emergencies.

This does not mean blackouts are inevitable. However, it highlights the serious challenges facing our electricity system and the need to add capacity before emergency measures become routine.

As a Michigan-based company, Solar Mounts LLC, a designer and manufacturer of commercial and utility-scale solar mounting systems, recognizes the potential implications for electricity prices, air quality, local businesses, and families across the state.

Rising Costs and the Consequences of Delayed Generation

PJM’s capacity auctions have exposed the difficulty of securing enough electricity for future demand. Prices reached $329.17 per megawatt-day for the 2026–2027 delivery year and $333.44 for 2027–2028, with the latter auction falling short of PJM’s stated reliability requirement.

Source: Vicinity Energy’s Analysis of PJM Capacity Auctions

Capacity costs are only one component of electricity bills, but rising prices signal growing pressure on consumers. We need more power before shortages force increasingly expensive emergency responses.

The October 2026 order permits designated generating units to exceed specified operating limits when necessary for reliability. It also authorizes PJM to direct backup generation at data centers and other large facilities, potentially including diesel generators as a last resort.

Running fossil-fuel plants more frequently can increase emissions, depending on the units involved. Operating diesel generators will worsen local noise pollution and expose operators to high diesel prices. If these measures become more common, we should ask why cleaner generation and storage are not coming online faster.

Weather Uncertainty Makes Planning Urgent

NOAA’s October 15, 2026, winter outlook anticipates warmer-than-average conditions across much of the northern United States, including the Midwest, influenced by a strong El Niño.

Source: NOAA’s 2026–2027 U.S. Winter Outlook

Seasonal forecasts cannot predict every period of extreme demand. Aging infrastructure, maintenance, changing weather, and growing electricity consumption reinforce the need for advance planning. Temporary authority to run additional fossil-fuel resources cannot replace long-term investment in generation.

Solar, Wind, and Batteries Can Help Close the Gap

Utility-scale solar, wind, and battery storage can help meet demand on timelines that many conventional generation projects cannot match. Suitable solar projects may be built within 18 months, depending on permitting, equipment, and interconnection. New nuclear facilities generally require substantially longer development periods.

Solar and wind will reduce emissions when they displace electricity from fossil-fuel generators. Batteries store surplus electricity for periods of higher demand. Note: actual emissions benefits depend on which generators are displaced and when electricity is delivered.

Faster interconnection reviews are also essential. Qualified solar projects shouldn’t remain in queues for years because of avoidable administrative delays. Better coordination among PJM, utilities, regulators, developers, and state governments, alongside necessary transmission upgrades, will bring capacity online sooner.

Solar Development and Agriculture Can Coexist

At Solar Mounts LLC, we witness concerns about installing solar on productive farmland here in rural Calhoun County (home to Solar Mounts LLC). However, well-designed projects can include restoration plans that allow agricultural use to resume when leases end. Solar leases can also provide farmers with supplemental income when crop prices fail to keep pace with production costs.

Agrivoltaic systems combine electricity generation with compatible crops or livestock. Thoughtful design and community engagement can balance renewable energy development with agricultural productivity, rural traditions, and farmland preservation.

Opportunities extend beyond prime farmland. Parking lots, commercial properties, brownfields, landfills, and other underutilized sites can accommodate solar generation. Solar carports provide electricity, shade, and weather protection, while community solar expands access for people unable or unwilling to install their own panels.

Solar Mounts LLC supports practical, site-appropriate solutions that expand generation while respecting farmers, landowners, and rural communities.

Hyperscale Data Centers Must Help Provide Their Own Power

Growing data center demand, particularly in Virginia and other parts of PJM’s territory, is adding pressure to an already constrained grid. Consequently, developers of new hyperscale facilities should demonstrate how they will secure adequate electricity and pay their fair share of supporting infrastructure. Dedicated generation, renewable energy contracts, batteries, flexible demand, and grid upgrades can all contribute.

Requiring every facility to build a gas-fired peaker plant is not necessarily the best answer. Gas turbines face equipment and supply-chain constraints, while additional fossil-fuel generation carries emissions and operating costs. A combination of solutions tailored to each location is more practical.

Existing facilities should also reduce peak demand where possible. Energy efficiency remains one of the most immediate ways businesses can limit electricity consumption.

Solar Mounts LLC sees the opportunity to expand commercial and utility-scale solar infrastructure across Michigan and the country. We encourage this expansion by supporting faster approvals, better grid planning, and a serious commitment to adding power generation and energy storage before shortages worsen.

Reliable electricity underpins our economy, jobs, and daily lives. Repeated emergency orders, rising capacity costs, and greater reliance on high-emission generation are not sound long-term strategies.

We shouldn’t wait for the next crisis to build the power system we already know we need.

#5772. XPENG L05 Offers A Bit More

XPENG recently launched sales of the new L05 in China and began shipments to global markets. Having seen a camouflaged prototype in Munich a few months ago, I had the impression that it was basically a longer L03. Looking at the production model, other than a few added features and details, that initial impression seems to have been an accurate assessment.

Unlike some of XPENG’s models targeting more premium segments, the L03 and L05 are based on 400V architecture. 3C charging rate goes from 10-80% in around 20 minutes. Overall charge times will come out slightly ahead of a Tesla Model Y (also 400V), but not as fast as other models in China on higher voltage architectures.

In terms of length, the L03 measures 4650 mm, while the L05 increases to 4870 mm. That puts a Model Y in the middle at 4791 mm. The increase in size adds roughly 50 kg to the weight of the L05 across trim levels.

The 58.2 kWh and 71.1 kWh battery sizes in the Chinese L05 are essentially the same as the L03, but the range of the larger battery in RWD drops from 650 km CLTC to 635 km due to the larger size and weight. At this time, the L05 is not being offered with AWD in China, although that might change in export markets. The 183 kW (~245 hp) motor is also the same while the 0-100 km/h time increases from 6.6 s in the RWD L03 to 6.8 s in the larger L05. Both are a little quicker than the European Model Y Standard at 7.2 s. We’ll have to see how the range works out under WLTP.

While the L03 impressed with a larger passenger compartment than the Model Y in a smaller footprint, it did have a somewhat smaller rear cargo area. Of course, because Tesla uses a non-standard measurement for cargo volume, comparing manufacturer numbers is pointless. However, the L05 does have a larger cargo volume than the L03 under standard measurements, which would be even more if measured to the top of the squared-off roof, rather than to the top of the seatbacks. XPENG has shown it holding 9 carryon suitcases with the rear seats up, which should be more than enough for normal people. Overall, the L05 has 41 storage locations throughout the vehicle, including under-seat storage.

In addition, the L05 expands the passenger compartment even more over the Model Y. The rear seats now also have folding rear tray tables and a 17.3” entertainment screen folds from the ceiling. In addition, there are sunshades that deploy to cover the sunroof. Other than the center touchscreen increasing from 15.6” to 17.3” and a few new color options, the interior is essentially the same up front. That includes heated, cooled and massaging seats and an easily visible HUD (European models will likely also get a driver display). Ultra trims include XPENG’s excellent NGP intelligent driving system with VLA 2.0.

List prices for the L05 range from 144,800 RMB ($21,722 USD at current exchange rates) for the base 560 Plus up to 176,800 RMB ($26,522 USD) for the top 635 Ultra SE trim. An EREV version with 1310 km of combined range comes in at 154,800 RMB ($23,222) up to 166,800 RMB ($25,022 USD). However, XPENG is offering a launch discount of 5000 RMB ($750). For comparison, the L03 EV currently ranges from 123,800 RMB ($18,586 USD) to 156,800 RMB ($23,540 USD) in China.

However, a G6 comes in at the same 176,800 RMB list price as the L05 635 Ultra SE. That premium vehicle has more power and faster charging on an 800V platform with close to the same range. And it isn’t lacking in technology. At 4758 mm long, it only gives up a small amount of space compared to the L05.

Of course, those are Chinese prices. XPENG has recently been rolling its latest models with the latest technology out globally. When adding ~20% VAT, 20.7% EV countervailing duty, 10% base tariff, shipping, misc. fees… EU prices would be higher. If the L03 starts at €35,600 in Germany, the L05 could start around €38,000. For comparison, the Model Y starts at €39,900 in Germany (intelligent driving is a €99/month subscription). A G6 starts at €43,600 in Germany.

Personally, I would stick with the slightly smaller and sportier L03 if given the option. For me, the space is sufficient. In some ways, the L03 is a better Model Y competitor because it is not trying to be more than a Model Y. I could also see potential updates coming soon to the G6 to position it as more premium and provide added differentiation. However, If you like the L03 but want a bit more room, the L05 could be a good option.

Larry lives in Queens, NY, with his wife and cats. While he has spent much of his professional career leading global marketing efforts, his passions focus on clean technology and the automotive industry. He believes in Green Free Trade and the potential for the application of engineering talent to create solutions to the world’s most challenging problems.

#5771. Obamacare Premiums to Rise by Double Digits in 2027

— Healthcare experts predict less coverage and higher prices

People heading into the Affordable Care Act (ACA) open enrollment period with plans to sign up for insurance should expect a variety of changes in 2027, including double-digit increases in premiums and fewer coverage options.

The sign-up window for the marketplace, also known as Obamacare, started Nov. 1 in most states and runs through Jan. 15.

Big changes to the exchange include expiring financial assistance, rising rates, potential delays in working with a broker, new restrictions on immigrants, and stricter enrollment checks.

Financial help, including premium tax credits and cost-sharing reductions, is now restricted for many groups of people who were previously eligible.

Across the board, healthcare experts predict less coverage and higher prices, with the average price of health insurance purchased through the marketplaces expected to rise by 13% in 2027, according to analysis of new CMS data by the healthcare research nonprofit KFF.

Cynthia Cox, a vice president and director of the ACA program at KFF, said the increase is primarily driven by rising healthcare costs, hospital market concentration, and potential factors such as the No Surprises Act, which took effect in 2022 and bans surprise billing, or the rising availability of GLP-1 drugs.

The people who will be most affected by the new increases are those who do not qualify for a subsidy.

"They may or may not be able to afford to continue to keep their coverage into next year," Cox said, adding that "a lot of them have already moved into bronze plans, which are the cheapest plans."

A CMS fact sheet about open enrollment that was distributed Friday says "Americans using HealthCare.gov will have robust access to low-cost plans."

The price of ACA insurance has skyrocketed for many people during Trump's second term after Republicans opposed extending COVID-era subsidies that had helped offset the costs of health insurance for most enrollees during Democratic President Joe Biden's administration.

That resulted in 1.2 million fewer people signing up for coverage in the exchanges.

Nearly 20 Million People in the U.S. Are Actively Enrolled in ACA Plans

As of February, roughly 19.2 million people in the U.S. were actively enrolled in ACA marketplace health plans, according to the Department of Health and Human Services website. The number of enrollees grew during the COVID-19 pandemic when enhanced subsidies became available through the American Rescue Plan Act and Inflation Reduction Act, both signed into law by Biden.

New regulations starting in 2027 will also limit tax credits for certain lawfully present immigrants, which could result in an additional 1 million uninsured individuals over time.

Meanwhile, the Trump administration has been removing ACA enrollees from the program as part of the ongoing campaign against what it characterizes as waste, fraud, and abuse.

In September, Vice President JD Vance and other officials in the Republican administration announced plans to remove 760,000 ACA enrollees from public healthcare exchanges, alleging the individuals were fraudulently enrolled in the program or simply did not exist.

Vance said the discovery and canceled subsidy payments for hundreds of thousands of people from the exchanges, part of an administration-wide crackdown on fraud, would result in $2.2 billion in cost savings.

It's unclear how many more people will drop off of ACA coverage in 2027, Cox said. But with higher premiums, immigration eligibility changes, and the anti-fraud efforts, "I think there's going to be fewer people enrolled in this market, but it's going to be harder to understand why."

#5770. Ford Wants Utilities To Help Make Bidirectional EV Charging Cheaper For Everyone

Earlier this week, the Ford Motor Company dropped word that anyone with a home EV charger who buys its new Ford Fathom electric pickup truck will not have to shell out cash for an external, wall-mounted inverter for bidirectional charging. Ford is also slashing the cost of its home EV charging and power management equipment. That’s not all. The automaker is also on a mission to convince all 3,000 utilities in the US and Canada to help accelerate the uptake of bidirectional charging systems.

With bidirectional charging, EVs can do something that gasmobile can. They can feed electricity back into household wiring, transforming themselves into emergency backup generators without the noise, pollution, and fuel cost of gas or diesel generators.

EVs equipped with bidirectional charging can also provide ratepayers with more control over their utility bills. With a home EV charger in hand, ratepayers can recharge their batteries during off-peak periods to take advantage of off-peak discounts, and they can use those discounted kilowatts to power their home during peak periods when rates are higher.

Drivers can also recharge at off-site charging stations that offers discounted rates, and ferry those kilowatts back for use at home.

Either way, the home needs to be equipped with an inverter to convert the DC current of an EV battery into the AC current used in buildings.

Or not, as the case may be. As explained by Ford earlier this week, the forthcoming Fathom pickup will come with its own built-in inverter. By eliminating the expense of an external inverter, Ford aims to sweeten the EV affordability pot, and the company has tapped Fathom to showcase the strategy at an MSRP under $30,000.

The missing link is the availability of utility-sponsored incentive programs that reward ratepayers for participating in managed demand programs. That includes rooftop solar, home energy storage systems, and even hot water heaters, with EV batteries being the icing on the cake.

“The opportunity is significant for utilities. But there are more than 3,000 utilities across the U.S. and Canada with largely unique interconnection processes,” Ford notes, drawing attention to another bottleneck limiting rapid uptake.

The problem is that interconnection processes are typically designed around rooftop solar and stationary batteries. “Bidirectional EVs bring new considerations because they are vehicles first, not stationary equipment,” Ford emphasizes.

Rooftop solar and home batteries are commonplace, but bidirectional EV charging has yet to scale up. To help push things along, last summer Ford began strategizing on a scalable interconnection system with the leading utilities American Electric Power, BC Hydro, Consumers Energy, Exelon, and Pacific Gas and Electric Co.

The aim is to get a process in place before the Fathom starts production next year, with a focus on collaboration between utilities, regulators, and OEMs. Accounting for periodic changes in bidirectional technology is another goal

As described by Ford the working group has already settled on the idea that interconnection should be required only for EV drivers who plan to deploy bidirectional EV charging in concert with the grid. Drivers who only charge up, or who only use their battery for backup power as needed, would not need to go through the interconnection process.

If you’re wondering why Ford is the only US automaker to offer bi-directional EV charging with a built-in inverter, that’s a good question. One answer is Auto Motive Power, the California-based power management specialist that Ford acquired in November of 2023.

“As part of the deal, Ford is acquiring AMP’s technology, its team of nearly 150 employees, as well as the company’s facility in Santa Fe Springs, California,” Wards Auto reported in November of 2023.

“Ford and AMP determined the advantage of their technology is best realized with deep integration into Ford’s EV products, which will allow for optimizing both cost and performance while improving experiences for our EV customers,” Ford elaborated in an email message.

You can say that again. As described by Ward’s, AMP had three systems in hand leading to the Ford acquisition. One is a Tesla NACS-compliant onboard charger that supports bi-directional charging through integrated DC fast charge contactors. “It can also be used to tap into an EV’s reserve battery power when the vehicle is not in use,” Ward’s observed.

Another system involves a V-to-X (vehicle-to-everything) power management system that supports grid connections. Rounding out the three is “ampEMU,” an energy management tool that integrates an onboard charger and power distribution system, along with fast charging for up to 500 amps.

By the time of the 2023 acquisition by Ford, AMP was already on its way to carving out an A-list slice of the bidirectional EV charging market. In 2020 the startup announced four new members on its advisory board, including former battery expert Kurt Keltey, who served an 11-year stint in strategic positions at Tesla before moving on to become VP of Automotive at Sila Nanotechnologies.

Sila CEO and co-founder Gene Berdichevsky also joined the AMP advisory board in 2020 along with former Canoo exec Phil Weicker and another Tesla alumnus, Troy Nergaard.

Ford is playing its AMP technology close to the vest, but a poster with the “ampforenergy” handle on the SpeakEV forum in August of 2023 provided some details about the EMU unit:

“The ampEMU is a comprehensive energy management system that manages the complete energy supply chain in an electric vehicle, from the vehicle’s charging infrastructure to battery management. It is backed by ampLOGIC for automotive big data analytics preventing battery degradation and enabling optimizations. ampEMU is compliant with the CCS, Tesla NACS, and CHAdeMO.”

“AMP’s EMU utilizes the latest SiC technology from Wolfspeed to drastically reduce the power electronics packaging volume,” ampedforenergy continued.

“This extremely compact unit will save space and reduce the vehicle’s overall weight, thereby improving range. EMU has the entire AMP Ecosystem in one unit offering cloud connectivity (ampLOGIC), battery management, power distribution unit, and charging solutions highly integrated with each other,” the poster added.

Speaking of Tesla, what happens next will be interesting. Tesla CEO Elon Musk’s pet project, the Cybertruck, was marketed as a pickup truck and it flopped epically, leaving the pickup truck field wide open for other EV makers to take a crack.

Meanwhile, last year Ford suspended its Lightning F-150 full sized electric pickup truck, evidently to focus its marketing program on the mid-sized Fathom, which is entering the market at a more affordable MSRP starting at $28,350.

Interested? Sign up here for an invitation to pre-order.

#5769. Trump admin ‘pauses’ leases for five offshore wind projects already under construction

The U.S. Dept. of the Interior has immediately paused the leases for all large-scale offshore wind projects under construction in the United States due to “national security risks identified by the Dept. of War in recently completed classified reports.”

“The prime duty of the United States government is to protect the American people,” said Secretary of the Interior Doug Burgum. “Today’s action addresses emerging national security risks, including the rapid evolution of the relevant adversary technologies, and the vulnerabilities created by large-scale offshore wind projects with proximity near our East Coast population centers. The Trump administration will always prioritize the security of the American people.”

The Dept. of the Interior claims that the blade movement and reflective towers create “radar interference called ‘clutter.'” Allegedly, this is a national security risk.

Dominion Energy released the following statement in response to the pause issued for the Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind project:

“The Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind Project (CVOW) is essential for American national security and meeting Virginia’s dramatically growing energy needs, the fastest growth in America. This growth is driven by the need to provide reliable power to many of America’s most important war fighting installations, the world’s largest warship manufacturer, and the largest concentration of data centers on the planet as well as the leading edge of the AI revolution.

Stopping CVOW for any length of time will threaten grid reliability for some of the nation’s most important war fighting, AI and civilian assets. It will also lead to energy inflation and threaten thousands of jobs.

CVOW is American-owned and benefits all of our Virginia customers. Our customers are paying for the project after a careful review of project costs and benefits by Virginia state regulators in 2022. These same state regulators, along with numerous federal agencies, oversee our cyber and physical security program, which is among the strongest in the energy industry.

The project has been more than 10 years in the works, involved close coordination with the military, and is located 27 to 44 miles offshore, so far offshore it does not raise visual impact concerns. The project’s two pilot turbines have been operating for five years without causing any impacts to national security.

CVOW enjoys bipartisan support and is within months of generating a massive 2,600 MW to support the fastest growing part of America’s energy grid. This growth serves the largest concentration of critical infrastructure in the world.

Virginia’s All-American, All-Of-The-Above-Energy Plan requires a range of power generation assets, including natural gas, advanced nuclear, and renewables. Virginia needs every electron we can get as our demand for electricity doubles. These electrons will power the data centers that will win the AI race, support our war fighters, and build the nuclear warships needed to maintain our maritime supremacy. Virginia’s grid needs addition of electrons, not subtraction.

We stand ready to do what is necessary to get these vital electrons flowing as quickly as possible.”

#5768. Volkswagen Q3 Loss Largest Since Dieselgate

The Volkswagen Group on Thursday posted its worst quarterly numbers in more than a decade. According to German news source DW, the company reported an operating loss of €5.86 billion for the period July 1 through September 30, 2027. A massive write down of its stake in Porsche was responsible for much of the loss, while its sales in China continued to decline at an alarming rate.

“The nine month results make one thing clear: We cannot afford to lose any time. We must act quickly and decisively,” chief financial officer Arno Antlitz said in a video statement. DW was quick to point out that the cockamamie tariffs on cars imported into the US from Germany were another significant factory in the dismal financial picture for Volkswagen. The US is Porsche’s largest market and those tariffs have had a devastating effect on its sales in America.

For the third quarter, Volkswagen reported an operating loss of €5.86 billion — four times greater then the loss is posted last year in the third quarter. That marks the company’s worst performance since the Dieselgate crisis in 2015. Revenue from sales fell by 6.3 percent year on year to €75.3 billion. Deliveries to customers, unit sales, and unit production numbers all dipped by roughly 15 percent as the company reduced production levels in an attempt to adjust factory output to match lower demand.

For 2026 so far, the company has had gross sales of €233.4 billion, which was virtually the same as last year, but sales volume was off 11 percent compared to 2025, so it is selling fewer cars but at higher prices.  Operating profit was just €0.1 billion, compared with €5.4 billion a year ago. But so-called “special items” totaling €8.7 billion — including a non-cash write down of €6.4 billion on Porsche assets —  led to that nearly negligible operating profit.

For the year, Volkswagen now expects its profit margin to be just 1 percent — far less than the 5 percent it projected at the start of the year. The only bright spot in the financial picture for Volkswagen is that sales of battery electric cars are up 39 percent year on year.

Volkswagen’s first joint venture in China was with SAIC in 1984. At that time, China knew next to nothing about manufacturing high quality automobiles. Now more then 40 years later, the tables have turned and China is making what are perhaps the highest quality automobiles in the world. Volkswagen, which was once the teacher, is now the student. If it is to survive, it may be because of the contributions of its Chinese partners — SAIC, FAW, Anhui, and Xpeng.

Together, they have created Vega, a new technology package comprised of software, electronics, and a dedicated EV platform, making Volkswagen the only non-Chinese automaker with a locally developed technology foundation represented in the Chinese market. While the package will first be used for cars made and sold exclusively in China, the company hints it may become part of an export campaign. Sharp eyed readers will immediately spot the irony of a company that is being hammered by Chinese imports in its home market becoming a Chinese import itself, at least in some markets.

The China Main Platform (CMP) is designed for battery-electric and plug-in hybrid models in the A-segment. It is expected to achieve a CLTC range of over 600 km, with an efficient energy consumption of around 11 kWh per 100 km. Charging from 30 to 80 per cent will take 25 minutes. For the premium segment, the China Scalable Platform (CSP) features 850 volt architecture, which makes charging from 10 to 80 per cent in just 13 minutes possible. Models built on the CSP architecture are expected to have a range of 800 km or more, with energy consumption below 13 kWh per 100 km.

Autonomous driver assistance systems are now critically important to new car sales in  China, and so Volkswagen is putting a priority on providing cutting edge ADAS technology to its customers in China. Volkswagen’s software subsidiary, now called Carizon, has developed its first in-house L2++ package known as HyperSense 8. This system will enter series production in seven new electrified models from the Chinese joint ventures FAW-Volkswagen, SAIC-Volkswagen and VW-Anhui before the end of this year.

HS 8 utilizes a powerful onboard computer that supports navigation assisted driving both on highways and in urban traffic. Automated parking assistance for all everyday and parking scenarios is integrated into the new system. Thanks to a robust training system the can simulate up to 10 million kilometers of driving a day, the ADAS system will be able to handle even rare and unexpected extreme situations safely, the company says.

Level 3 systems, which allow drivers to take their hands off the steering wheel permanently under certain conditions, are scheduled to reach series production by 2027. Volkswagen has already obtained an official licence for real world road testing in Hefei. Volkswagen intends to have Level 4 autonomy in its cars for the Chinese market by 2029, thanks to the third generation of its CEA electronic architecture (CEA 3.0) and a Volkswagen-developed proprietary control chip known as  C7H.

Four years ago, Volkswagen Group launched its strategic transformation under the motto “In China, for China.” Now, it is making a strategic pivot that is more globally oriented known as “In China, Beyond China.” China is transitioning from a sales market into a central research hub and an export node for the company’s worldwide activities..

In fact, the deployment of China-developed electronics in Europe may soon be on the horizon. Today, models from Volkswagen produced in China are being exported to the Middle East, Uzbekistan, Kazakhstan, and parts of Sub-Saharan Africa.

By the end of 2026, exports are set to expand to markets such as Mexico, Egypt and Vietnam, followed by more countries in the ASEAN region and South America. Beyond vehicle exports, the combination of the CMP platform and CEA electronic architecture serves as the technical basis for upcoming model ranges throughout the Global South, particularly South America.

German mechanical engineering has always been the standard of the world, but Volkswagen Group stumbled badly out of the gate when it attempted to integrate computer technology into its cars, starting with the ID. 3 in 2020. It taught China how to build cars and then China taught it how to master digital technology. Maybe the combination will allow Volkswagen to become profitable and thrive in the future? Or not…..

#5767. She Had a Life-Threatening Reaction After Sex

— Partner's antibiotic use likely triggered anaphylaxis after intercourse

A young woman with a known allergy to penicillin had a severe allergic reaction immediately after intercourse with her husband, who was taking amoxicillin.

The case underscores the fact that sexual contact can lead to mucosal exposure to beta-lactams in bodily fluids, and trigger anaphylaxis in those with allergies, Cassim Akhoon, MBChB, of King's College Hospital in London, and colleagues wrote in BMJ Case Reports.

The lesson is that unprotected sexual intercourse can be a route of transmission and cause significant allergic reactions in a partner, Akhoon told MedPage Today. "At the moment, including allergy specialists I've spoken to in the U.K., this is not something people are mindful of, nor counselling people on when they're being given a diagnosis of a drug allergy."

After this case, he counsels patients with allergies of the risk for transfer if their partner is taking penicillin, "and it's better to use protection or abstain during those times."

It's unclear whether other drugs or even foods might be transmitted in the same way, but reactions have been reported to another antibiotic, and in one case, walnuts. "We don't know enough to be able to give precise answers about which ones can, but it's enough evidence to say that some can, and it is something we need to start getting data on," he said.

Reports of anaphylaxis related to beta-lactam transfer during sexual contact are rare, the authors wrote. The case reported here is "an episode of anaphylaxis, tightly linked to a partner taking amoxicillin, supported by subsequent positive skin prick testing to amoxicillin, and a clinical course that argues strongly against seminal plasma hypersensitivity."

In this case, paramedics were called when a young woman in her 20s had sudden onset of generalized pruritic or itching, widespread urticaria or hives, and angioedema, swelling affecting her throat and tongue. Onset occurred 5 minutes after ejaculation by her partner during vaginal intercourse.

She had a prior history of penicillin allergy after having a systemic reaction to treatment with amoxicillin/clavulanic acid 5 years earlier. That reaction started only 10 minutes after her first dose, with generalized urticaria, lip angioedema, and hypotension that caused a loss of consciousness, requiring treatment in the emergency department.

Another time, she developed urticaria while crushing amoxicillin to give to her dog. Although she had not had formal allergy testing, she was prescribed a 300-μg epinephrine autoinjector due to the high risk of exposure to beta-lactams through her occupation as a student nurse.

So, in the current case, she was able to self-administer epinephrine prior to arrival of the paramedics. Once they arrived, she was found to be hypotensive, with a blood pressure of 88/56 mm Hg, and tachycardic, with a heart rate of 115 beats per minute, and have an audible wheeze on chest auscultation. They administered an additional 500 µg of intramuscular epinephrine, and transported her to the emergency department, where she was treated for anaphylaxis.

That day, she hadn't knowingly ingested beta-lactam antibiotics, and reported no new foods or medications. Her partner, however, had started taking oral amoxicillin in a dose of 500 mg, three times per day, and had taken the last dose approximately 6 hours before intercourse.

"The temporal relationship to ejaculation during the partner's antibiotic course, together with subsequent tolerance of antibiotics, led clinicians to suspect seminal transfer as the most likely exposure route," they wrote.

After observation for a minimum of 6 hours, the patient was discharged with additional autoinjectors, and a referral to specialist allergy services, the authors noted. One year later, serum-specific IgE to penicillin determinants and amoxicillin was negative, but skin prick testing showed sensitization to amoxicillin, they added.

The report also includes the perspective of the patient, who expressed concern about the reaction of the paramedics, nurse, and physician at the emergency department to the idea she had not herself taken the amoxicillin, when she had a known penicillin allergy. "This was incredibly frustrating to have to explain multiple times and not feeling believed," she said.
