#5766. It’s Not Enough To Electrify More. We Must Electrify Better.

EVs and other electric products are emerging not only as a climate strategy but also as a question of affordability, economic competitiveness, public health, consumer experience, energy security, and resilience to volatile fuel markets.

At 5 a.m., an electric bus glides out of its depot after charging overnight, ready to carry children to school without spewing diesel fumes. As the city stirs, heat pumps have already brought homes and offices to a comfortable temperature, using far less energy than their gas boiler counterparts. By midday, solar power charges delivery vehicles, cools warehouses, and helps factories run their most energy-intensive processes. Then, as millions of people return home and plug in their cars, smart chargers quietly schedule charging for later, when electricity is cheaper and the grid less busy.

Scenes like these are beginning to emerge across the globe: electric buses in Delhi, electric heat pumps warming homes through Norwegian winters, electric trucks entering Chinese freight and construction fleets. The shift to electrification has begun.

Most people will experience this transition not as an energy-system revolution, but as a quieter journey, a more comfortable home, a lower bill, or cleaner air. But taken together, these everyday changes mark an epochal shift in the global economy: from one built on burning fossil fuels to one driven by clean electricity, efficient technologies, and flexible demand.

But what exactly is electrification? How can using more electricity benefit people and the planet? And what happens when millions of new electric vehicles, appliances, buildings, and factories connect to a power system already under strain?

Put simply, electrification means replacing technologies that burn fossil fuels directly—gasoline-fueled cars, diesel buses, gas boilers, industrial furnaces, etc.—with alternatives powered by electricity. (Ideally clean electricity, but more on that later).

This shift is moving center stage not only as a climate strategy but also as a question of affordability, economic competitiveness, public health, consumer experience, energy security, and resilience to volatile fuel markets.

Yet the case for electrification is more compelling—and more interesting—than simply switching fuels. To understand its potential, we first need to look at how far electrification has progressed, why electric technologies use less energy and are cheaper to run, and what needs to happen to ensure electrification brings about societal and environmental benefits. Here, we dive deep into the current state of electrification and how to accelerate it in the right way.

The answer depends heavily on where (and what) you look at. In some technologies and markets, electrification is advancing remarkably quickly. Across the wider global economy, however, electricity still supplies only about 20% of final energy use (the energy actually consumed in homes, businesses, vehicles, and factories).

Road transport is currently the clearest example of electrification gathering pace. Global electric car sales have increased by almost 600% since 2020, rising to 20 million last year and now accounting for more than one in four new cars sold. Transport is advancing faster than buildings and heavy industry, but most vehicles already on the road continue to burn fossil fuels.

Progress also differs sharply across geographies. China, sometimes dubbed “the first electro-state,” has pulled ahead, with electricity now supplying almost 30% of final energy use, versus roughly 22% in the United States and 21% in the European Union.

Today’s pace is still far from sufficient. According to the International Renewable Energy Agency, electricity must supply more than half of all final energy use by mid-century to hold global temperature rise to 1.5 degrees C (2.7 degrees F), the limit scientists say is necessary for averting some of the worst impacts of climate change. That must come with a profound shift in how electricity is generated, with renewables providing around 90% of global power. Electrification and power sector decarbonization must therefore advance together.

Part of the explanation lies in how dispersed the transition is. Power sector decisions are concentrated among utilities, developers, regulators, and governments, while end-use electrification reaches into millions of homes, vehicles, businesses, and production lines.

Meanwhile, some activities are simply harder to electrify than others. Electric technologies can already serve road transport, buildings, and many lower-temperature industrial processes. In many cases, they are already cheaper to operate than their fossil fuel-powered counterparts and can deliver lower lifetime costs even where the upfront price remains higher. Electric cars and buses, heat pumps, induction cookers, electric water heaters and many industrial motors are increasingly available and affordable.

But for industries like steelmaking or chemical production, and for sectors such as aviation and shipping, direct electrification is harder. These activities require extremely high temperatures, use fossil fuels as a raw material in the production process, or need more energy than today’s batteries can efficiently carry. In these cases, hydrogen, synthetic fuels, or other low-carbon alternatives may be more viable alternatives than electrification, while carbon capture may be needed for residual emissions that cannot easily be avoided.

“Electrify Everything!” might be a tempting slogan, but it’s unlikely to be pragmatic—or even desirable—as an economic or industrial strategy. The more sensible (if admittedly less catchy) objective is to electrify directly in the many areas it makes overwhelming sense to do so, while reserving scarcer alternatives for the trickier-to-electrify sectors.

Electrification is an enabler, not an end goal in itself. An electric car powered by a coal-heavy grid could be worse for the climate than a car that runs on gasoline. Its benefit, in other words, depends on how the electricity is generated. But an electric car, heat pump, or industrial process can become ever-cleaner over time as the grid decarbonizes through greater use of wind, solar, nuclear, or other clean energy sources. A gas-powered engine or boiler, by contrast, remains locked into burning polluting fuels throughout its life. The full benefit of electrification therefore only comes from expanding electric end uses while also making the power supply progressively cleaner.

Additionally, one of electrification’s most counterintuitive features is that electricity consumption can rise while overall energy use falls.

The reason is efficiency. Combustion engines waste as much as 80% of their fuel’s energy (mostly as heat), converting just 20%  into useful motion. Meanwhile, electric motors convert around 80% of their electrical energy into motion. Heat pumps go even further. Rather than creating heat by burning fuel, they extract existing heat from the air or ground, typically delivering several units of heat for each unit of electricity consumed.

Replacing technologies that burn fuel directly with electric alternatives—replacing molecules with electrons—can therefore reduce the energy required to make the same trip or heat the same home.

This produces a second apparent paradox: your electricity bill may get bigger while your total energy bill gets smaller. An electrified household uses more electricity, of course; but its occupants no longer buy gas for their cars or heating systems. Because EVs and heat pumps use energy far more efficiently than combustion engines and boilers, the avoided fuel costs can more than offset the additional power consumed, even if electricity itself becomes more expensive. For example, one recent analysis estimated that fully electrifying a typical European household’s heating and transport could cut its overall energy bill by more than half.

The case for electrification cannot — and need not — rest on emissions reductions alone (although you might think that preserving a livable planet is a sufficiently persuasive starting point!).

Transitions move faster when the new technology is not merely cleaner but visibly better for people and businesses. EVs are quieter, smoother, typically cheaper to run, and more responsive than gas-powered cars, with instant acceleration from a standstill. Heat pumps can provide heating and cooling from the same system. Electric cooking improves indoor air quality and offers greater temperature control. Electric industrial equipment can make production more precise, responsive, and efficient. And all these technologies can help reduce costs by using energy more efficiently.

But a better technology is not automatically a better proposition for everyone. Upfront costs, access to infrastructure, and the way electricity is priced can determine which households and businesses benefit. A wealthy homeowner with a driveway can buy an EV upfront and install a charger; a delivery driver in Nairobi may need affordable finance, battery swapping, and reliable public charging. A well-insulated building can use a heat pump efficiently; a poorly insulated one may require costly upgrades before a heat pump makes sense.

So while technology creates the potential, it’s the infrastructure, finance, and policy that determine who can realize it. In Rwanda, for example, policies like tax incentives alongside private financing and battery-swapping networks are helping motorcycle taxi drivers switch to electric bikes. The shift cuts daily fuel and maintenance costs for drivers while also improving the city’s air quality and supporting the country’s target of electrifying 30% of its two-and three-wheelers by 2030.

Electricity demand is already soaring, driven by increased cooling needs, data centers, industrial growth, and wider access to reliable power. Add millions of electric vehicles, heat pumps, and industrial machines without planning where and when they will draw power, and today’s bottlenecks will worsen.

Electrification is therefore not only a power generation challenge. It is also a grid and coordination challenge, requiring action on three fronts:

Consider a city planning both thousands of new homes and a large electric bus fleet. If the housing authority, transport agency and utility plan separately (as they so often do), transformers may be upgraded twice, depot connections delayed and residents left waiting for both housing and cleaner transport. Plan them together — alongside local solar, batteries and bidirectional charging and controls — and the same investment can support several public priorities while simultaneously creating on-demand local energy-storage assets.

Building clean generation is pointless if customers can’t connect to it. Building new grid infrastructure is wasteful if existing capacity remains poorly used. Strategic electrification requires both expansion and much sharper coordination across power, transport, buildings, industry and urban development.

Now picture 6 p.m. in that same, increasingly electrified city I mentioned at the beginning of this article. Thousands of families arrive home and all begin cooking dinner. Cars are plugged in. Heating or cooling is still running, shops are still open, factories are still producing and data centers are still computing. The problem is not necessarily a shortage of electricity across the total system. It is that too much demand arrives in the same parts of the grid at the same moment. Networks strain, expensive backup generation switches on and billions get spent building infrastructure needed for only a few hours each year.

A car may be parked all night but need only a few hours to charge. Buildings can pre-heat or pre-cool before peak electricity demand. Water heaters, refrigerated warehouses, batteries and some industrial processes can also run a little earlier or later, shifting demand — and all without disrupting comfort or output.

None of this requires people to reorganize their evenings or become amateur electricity traders. They simply want their cars charged, their homes comfortable and their bills lower.

Smart controls can optimize energy use automatically. A customer might opt in to a program run by their energy supplier, which temporarily adjusts their thermostat, EV charger and/or water heater when electricity demand is particularly high — typically without any noticeable loss of comfort or convenience. The customer retains ultimate control and can override the change while receiving a lower bill or payment in return. Done well, this creates a triple-win: lower bills for the customer, less strain on the grid and fewer expensive investments.

At a constrained time and place, a megawatt of demand that can be shifted in time may be more valuable than a megawatt of additional supply. It can relieve a bottleneck immediately, reduce renewable curtailment (when renewable energy production is intentionally reduced because the grid doesn’t have the capacity to handle it or because there is not enough demand for it), and avoid building infrastructure used for only a handful of hours each year.

First, the electricity system needs to know what flexible assets are available and when they can respond. A grid operator cannot rely on thousands of individual EV chargers, batteries or commercial refrigerators if it cannot see where they are, how quickly they can react, or how much demand they can shift. These many smaller assets also need a simple route into the market (often by being grouped together by an “aggregator”). And they need to be paid for the value they provide.

Second, utility incentives set by regulators matter. A utility that earns more for building new substations and cables — as is often the case — will tend to build. If it is also rewarded for using batteries, smart charging and flexible demand to solve the same problem more cheaply, it is incentivized to choose the best combination.

Third, the business model has to stack up. Batteries, smart controls and flexible industrial equipment will not scale on one-off grants or short-lived pilots. Investors need predictable revenues, customers need affordable finance for the upfront costs and service providers need confidence that market rules will survive long enough to build a viable business.

The next phase of electrification is less about inventing some technological silver bullet than about governments, regulators and system operators creating the market rules, incentives and revenue streams that allow existing technologies to scale. In practice, that means treating flexibility as a real system resource — and paying for the value it provides.

Back to our city example one last time, but 15 years on.

The buses are all electric, but that is only the most visible change. Depots charge mainly when power is cheap and plentiful. Energy stored in their batteries is available for back-up power during emergencies like storms. Apartment buildings share chargers rather than reserving e-mobility for people with driveways. Homes stay comfortable through heatwaves and cold spells because efficient buildings, heat pumps, thermal storage and smart controls work together, pre-heating or pre-cooling when clean power is plentiful, easing demand when the grid is strained and helping maintain essential services during blackouts. Restaurants and schools cook without indoor combustion and the resulting air pollutants.

The industrial district has changed, too. Electric boilers, motors and heat pumps use energy more precisely, while flexible processes such as refrigeration, water heating or pumping automatically run earlier or pause briefly when the grid is under strain. New factories intentionally choose sites where clean power and network capacity are available. Data centers and other large energy users secure connections partly by bringing storage, clean on-site generation and flexibility that benefit the wider system.

For consumers, much of this is invisible. Their car is ready in the morning. Their building remains comfortable. Their energy provider shifts flexible consumption in the background and returns some of the savings. During a power disruption, batteries in buses, homes and commercial buildings help keep critical services running.

For the economy, the shift is deeper. Money once spent importing, subsidizing, and burning oil and gas is redirected into productive assets at home: clean generation, grids, efficient buildings, vehicles, storage, software and industrial equipment. Instead of continually paying for imported fuels, countries build lasting domestic capacity, strengthen industrial resilience and gain greater control over their energy future.

The prize is therefore larger than emissions reduction. It is better products and services, cleaner air, lower costs, stronger energy security and greater national control over the energy supply.

Electrification, then, is not simply about replacing one fuel with another, or adding ever-more demand to the grid. It is about moving from an energy system built around inefficient, polluting combustion to one built around clean electricity, efficient technologies and demand that can respond intelligently to the system around it.

Electrify more, yes. But above all, electrify better.

EVs and other electric products are emerging not only as a climate strategy, but as a question of affordability, economic competitiveness, public health, consumer experience, energy security and resilience to volatile fuel markets.

WRI is a global research organization that spans more than 50 countries, with offices in Brazil, China, Europe, India, Indonesia, and the United States. Our more than 450 experts and staff work closely with leaders to turn big ideas into action to sustain our natural resources—the foundation of economic opportunity and human well-being. Find out more at www.wri.org

#5765. Misconceptions about Mobility Growth

Higher speed is the main cause of mobility growth. When people go faster, they will travel further. This is the shortest summary of the history of passenger mobility.

An illustrative example is the high-speed rail connection between Paris and Lyon. After its opening in 1981, commuters were able to travel daily between these two cities. This was not possible before.

More generally, the rise in both ownership and speed of the car after the second World War, allowed for increasing distances from home to work, to school, shops, hospitals and friends.

The average distance a person travels per day tripled since 1960: from 10 to 30 (Dutch data). This growth is entirely caused by car use, being from door-to-door faster than both public transport and the bicycle.

Many empirical studies underpin that speed is the main cause of mobility growth. Total travel time is roughly constant: a person in a city or country travels on average 1.1 hour per day. When travel time is constant, higher speed inevitably leads to longer trips.

However, conventional wisdom makes us believe in other causes of mobility growth. Such as, rising income, decreasing costs of car driving, working from home or changing preferences in favour or against cars. The dominance of speed is overlooked in standard views by traffic engineers and politicians alike. Even statistics on average speed in urban and rural areas are scarce. This omission hides important consequences for transport policy. Here are three:.

Road building in congested areas does not solve congestion. Directly after opening, there is less congestion and car speed increases. However, after only a few years this gain disappears, because extra road capacity attracts extra traffic. One of the empirical studies showing this, is aptly titled “The Fundamental Law of Road Congestion”.

Secondly, when average car speed does not increase anymore, the growth in car mobility will end. This is the reality in rich countries with a mature motorway grid. Average car speed stabilized in the Netherlands since the turn of the century, and consequently car mobility per person even decreased by 3% between 2005 and 2019 (the last year before the Covid lockdowns). The UK, Spain, Belgium, Sweden and Luxemburg also show a drop in car mobility per person over the same period. Other countries will follow. Neglecting car speed as the main driver behind traffic growth results in traffic forecasts that are systematically too high.

Finally, cars in large cities ride less than half as fast as the country average. Therefore, citizens of metropolises travel half as many kilometres by car than others. Trip distances are shorter in urban areas, destinations are nearby, and the share of public transport is manyfold that in rural areas. Therefore, good accessibility can be achieved by urban density, in a mix of housing, workplaces, shops and parks. Urbanisation is the cheapest way to improve accessibility, while reducing car use, at the same time.

So, conventional wisdom on mobility growth conceals that road building will not solve congestion, that urbanisation reduces the need for mobility, and that car growth is ending. Unsurprisingly, these misconceptions favour vested interests from car and road industries. The neglect of speed as the main driving force behind mobility growth, hinders effective policies towards affordable and sustainable accessibility.

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.

#5764. Smarter Cooling, Stronger Reliability: Advancing Wind Turbine Gearbox Performance

As the wind energy industry matures, operators and service providers are increasingly focused on maximizing turbine availability while controlling long-term maintenance costs. Although major drivetrain components often receive the spotlight, one overlooked system can have an outsized impact on turbine reliability and performance: gearbox oil cooling.

Heat exchangers play a critical role in maintaining proper oil temperature and viscosity within wind turbine gearboxes. When cooling systems become clogged with airborne debris, dust, or organic material, oil temperatures rise, lubrication performance declines, and component stress increases. Over time, these conditions can contribute to reduced gearbox efficiency, elevated maintenance demands, and avoidable downtime.

“Wind operators today are balancing increasing performance expectations with the realities of aging fleets and rising maintenance costs. Technologies that improve cooling efficiency and reduce service exposure can have a meaningful impact on long-term turbine reliability and operational profitability,” according to Mike Erickson, HYDAC’s Market Manager for the Wind Industry.

Traditional “panel cut” heat exchanger designs have long been susceptible to clogging due to their tightly packed fin structures. In many wind environments — particularly agricultural, dusty, or high-pollen regions — debris accumulation can severely restrict airflow. Oil leaks can further compound the issue by trapping contaminants within the exchanger core, making cleaning more difficult and labor intensive.

This operational challenge has driven the need for more resilient cooling technologies designed specifically for real-world wind farm conditions.

HYDAC’s new “Square Wave” heat exchanger design represents a significant advancement in addressing these reliability concerns. Engineered to allow debris to pass more freely through the exchanger fins, the design minimizes clogging while improving cooling efficiency under demanding operating conditions.

The importance of effective cooling cannot be overstated. Wind turbine gearbox oil typically operates within an optimal temperature range of approximately 45°C to 65°C. Even modest temperature increases can significantly impact oil viscosity. According to field data, a 12°C rise in oil temperature can reduce viscosity by nearly 28 percent — affecting lubrication quality and accelerating wear on critical drivetrain components.

Field trials have demonstrated measurable operational benefits from the Square Wave design. During a one-year trial on GE 1.x turbines, the upgraded exchanger consistently reduced clogging and lowered maintenance requirements compared to traditional panel cut cores. Operators reported reduced up-tower cleaning time and fewer temperature-related cooling faults.

Field validation across multiple turbine platforms has further reinforced the operational advantages of the Square Wave design. In one case study involving legacy 1 MW-class turbines operating in the Pacific Northwest, a major renewable energy operator installed pilot sets of Square Wave heat exchangers as part of a cooling performance evaluation. Installation teams reported the upgraded exchangers integrated seamlessly into the existing platform with no retrofit complications.

After initial operating data demonstrated improved cooling consistency, the operator expanded the evaluation program with additional units. Long-term SCADA analysis later showed turbines equipped with Square Wave technology operate approximately 20 to 25 percent cooler than comparable turbines using conventional heat exchanger designs under similar operating conditions. The data also highlighted another important advantage: the Square Wave cores continued to maintain airflow and cooling efficiency in debris-heavy environments where traditional exchanger designs became increasingly restricted over time.

The successful deployment also generated interest from additional wind service organizations seeking retrofit-ready cooling upgrades that could improve turbine reliability while reducing maintenance burdens across aging fleets.

“What operators are increasingly recognizing is that small improvements in thermal management can create meaningful gains in turbine reliability, maintenance efficiency, and long-term asset performance,” said Erickson. “The field data we’re seeing continues to validate the value of designing cooling systems specifically for real-world wind operating environments.”

More recently, long-term SCADA data from Mitsubishi platforms further validated the technology’s performance. After two years of operation, turbines equipped with Square Wave heat exchangers operated approximately 19 to 25 percent cooler than reference turbines using standard exchanger designs under comparable operating conditions.

For wind asset owners and service providers, the implications are substantial: Improved thermal management supports healthier oil viscosity, more stable gearbox operation, and potentially longer component life. At the same time, reduced cleaning frequency and fewer cooling-related service events translate directly into lower operational expenditures and increased turbine availability.

As the industry continues to prioritize lifecycle optimization and energy production efficiency, innovative subsystem upgrades such as advanced heat exchanger technology are becoming increasingly valuable. Rather than waiting for costly failures or performance degradation, operators are seeking practical retrofit solutions that improve reliability without requiring major system redesigns.

“Effective thermal management is critical to protecting gearbox health and maintaining oil performance over time. By addressing one of the most common causes of cooling degradation — debris accumulation — operators can improve uptime while reducing unnecessary maintenance interventions,” said HYDAC’s Erickson.

For wind stakeholders evaluating ways to improve gearbox reliability, reduce maintenance exposure, and enhance turbine performance, cooling system optimization deserves greater attention. Proven retrofit technologies like advanced heat exchanger designs can offer a practical path toward improved uptime and operational efficiency without requiring major system overhauls.

As fleets age and performance expectations continue to rise, collaboration between operators, service providers, and technology partners will be essential to building more resilient wind assets for the future. Now is the time to evaluate whether existing cooling systems are supporting — or limiting — long-term turbine reliability goals.

“HYDAC’s goal is to help wind owners and service providers solve practical operational challenges in ways that create long-term value across the entire asset lifecycle. Innovations like the Square Wave heat exchanger are designed to improve reliability, simplify maintenance, and support greater energy production over time.”

Don’t let cooling system limitations impact turbine performance.

Proactive cooling optimization can help operators reduce maintenance exposure, protect critical drivetrain components, and maximize energy production across the asset lifecycle. To learn how HYDAC’s Square Wave heat exchanger technology is helping wind operators improve reliability and lower operating costs, connect with our wind energy specialists today or visit: https://www.hydac.com/en-us/industry-solutions/renewable-energy/wind-power/

#5763. The Zombie Engine: How Brussels Bureaucrats Are Breathing False Life Into Combustion

If you poured rapeseed oil into a combustion engine and declared it a “zero-emission vehicle,” you would be laughed at. But in the EU Parliament, this fiction is being drafted into law.

If you poured rapeseed oil into a combustion engine, watched toxic exhaust billow from the tailpipe, and declared it a “zero-emission vehicle,” you would be laughed at. Yet inside the corridors of the European Parliament, this exact fiction is being drafted into law.

Under heavy pressure from the fossil fuel lobby and legacy carmakers, EU lawmakers are preparing to endorse a bizarre new regulatory beast: the VEEF, or “Vehicle Exclusively running on Eligible Fuels.” In plain English, these are standard combustion-engine cars promised to run exclusively on liquid biofuels or e-fuels. In exchange for that promise, Brussels plans to grant them a legal tailpipe emission score of zero.

It is regulatory alchemy at its worst. Cloaked in the seductive language of “technological neutrality,” this proposal creates an escape hatch for an obsolete technology, all while setting up ordinary European drivers for an expensive logistical nightmare.

It is just another desperate tactic to extend the life of the combustion engine by any means possible.

To understand the absurdity of the biofuel car, look at what actually happens in the engine. It’s still the exact same combustion. A piston firing on biofuels produces the same toxic cocktail of nitrogen oxides, carbon monoxide, and fine particulates that has choked European cities for decades. Labeling these cars “zero-emission” launders the pollution through a regulatory loophole and continues to foster emission-induced pathology.

Nor does it save the climate. The average biofuel blend available today delivers a modest 20% to 40% emissions saving compared to fossil fuels. When you factor in the indirect land-use change of first-generation crop biofuels ploughing up forests to grow food for engines, crop-based biofuel cars actually emit 23% more greenhouse gases than a standard petrol car.

Biofuel cars are being sold to the public under the guise of affordability, yet running them is punishingly expensive. Hydrotreated vegetable oil (HVO) biodiesel costs around €13 per 100 kilometers to run, compared to roughly €7 for an equivalent electric vehicle. Pushing drivers toward these cars locks households into volatile, high fuel costs at the exact moment renewable electricity prices and battery costs are tumbling.

Then comes the phantom fuel problem. Europe possesses a strictly limited supply of biofuels. The continent produces only enough advanced petrol biofuel to power roughly one million cars.

The Parliament’s compromise envisions allowing food-crop fuels until 2032, before shifting new biofuels models to run strictly on advanced wastes and residues from 2033 onward. But because sales skew heavily toward petrol while advanced biofuels skew toward diesel, the arithmetic collapses immediately. If carmakers sell one million petrol VEEFs cumulatively, European biofuel supplies would run dry immediately. Millions of drivers would be left with nowhere to fill up.

How does Brussels propose to stop a driver from pulling up to a regular pump and filling their “zero-emission” car with cheaper fossil petrol?

Through a bureaucratic and technical monster. The proposals require that manufacturers install complex onboard systems to ensure fuel exclusivity: fuel sensors, tamper-proof hardware, and engine-throttling software to strand your car if the wrong liquid is detected. Pumps would require cryptographic digital handshakes with the car’s chassis number to authorize a fill-up. On top of that, it could require two separate, parallel, continent-wide pump networks; one for pre-2033 crop fuels, another for post-2033 advanced fuels.

For an EU Commission committed to deregulation and “simplification,” this is pure loophole-hungry nonsense.

Naturally, the industry knows this. The fuel-combustion lobby is already quietly pivoting to their real goal: “mass balancing.” Under this proposal, carmakers wouldn’t need to put pure biofuels into your tank at all. Instead, by using the standard blended biofuel mix of today, they could manufacture “virtual” clean cars, allowing them to keep selling dirty combustion engines while selling fewer electric vehicles.

This is not just a technical Eurocrat debate, it is an industrial fork in the road. More than €200 billion has already been committed to building Europe’s electric vehicle and battery supply chain. That capital was deployed on the promise of a clear, stable regulatory path to zero emissions by 2035.

While China dominates the global battery supply chain and electric vehicle sales surge worldwide, European policymakers are entertaining a bizarre scheme to keep 19th-century technology on life support.

The VEEF loophole solves no climate target or air pollution, helps no driver, and builds no clean industry. It is simply a regulatory Trojan horse designed to buy legacy polluters a few more years of business-as-usual. When the vote arrives, lawmakers must slam the door shut on this costly diversion.

If you are looking for a terrifying Halloween costume, a zombie combustion engine masquerading as zero-emission is about as scary as it gets.

#5761. Eugene Becomes 5th Oregon City to Adopt Home Energy Score Requirement

Energy Audits Support Emissions Reductions and Energy Efficiency Upgrades

Eugene, Oregon — In a 6-1 vote on Wednesday, October 28th, the Eugene City Council adopted the Residential Energy Performance Disclosure Requirement ordinance, a policy commonly referred to as a Home Energy Score requirement. The vote places Eugene among four other Oregon cities, Portland, Hillsboro, Milwaukie and Bend, that have similar Home Energy Score ordinances. The vote comes in the midst of a larger trend of cities adopting a variety of policies to aid community members navigating home retrofits and efficiency upgrades as support for building electrification grows in Oregon, and nationwide in the face of efforts to undermine existing climate policy at the Federal level.

“We applaud the City Council for taking this common sense step and establishing a home energy score requirement in Eugene. This policy will ensure that home buyers have the information that they need to make informed choices when making what is often the most important financial decision in their lives,” said Bethany Cotton, Conservation Director with Cascadia Wildlands. “The vote is an important step forward to tackling building emissions, reducing energy costs, and meeting the City’s Climate Recovery Ordinance goals to reduce emissions by 50% by 2030.”

The adoption of the ordinance is the first major step from the City to reduce building emissions since the Ninth Circuit’s April 2023 decision on the Berkley building electrification requirement, which invalidated the City’s February 2023 residential building electrification ordinance. The passage of this policy in Eugene also marks more than 1 million Oregonians living in a jurisdiction with a Home Energy Score disclosure policy, representing nearly 1 in 4 Oregon residents (24.4%).

“The Ninth Circuit’s disastrous decision in 2023 set Eugene, and cities across the region, back for years on climate action and energy efficiency. While this policy is just a small step, it is a critical signal that the City is ready to begin moving decisively to regaining lost time and transitioning away from polluting fossil fuels,” said Dylan Plummer, Deputy Director of the Sierra Club’s Clean Heat Campaign. “We look forward to supporting Eugene and other local governments to continue to push the ball forward on common sense policies to require building decarbonization.”

The ordinance requires that a home energy score assessment be conducted and posted when a home is listed for sale. These assessments review the average energy cost of a home, evaluating existing appliances, heating systems, windows, doors and insulation, as well as the climate emissions associated with the home, and score the house’s energy use on a scale of one to ten. The reports include recommendations for how to improve a home’s energy efficiency and reduce emissions, and include key information about existing incentives and subsidies at the local, state and federal level.

“Eugene Water and Electric Board customers can access significant incentives to support home electrification and energy efficiency including zero interest loans and cash subsidies for weatherization and heat pumps for space and water heating. Sadly, these programs are deeply underutilized because many folks are not aware they exist,” said Meredith Tufts, Coalition Coordinator with the Fossil Free Eugene Coalition. “The Home Energy Score requirement will ensure that every new home owner has the information they need to take advantage of these programs to reduce their energy bills and climate pollution.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5760. Rising Health Costs, Coverage Changes Worry Older Adults as Election Day Nears

Barbara Weigand could not have prepared more for retirement. She stayed healthy and saved diligently for years.

This year, though, a shoulder surgery and a bladder cancer diagnosis have really tested her finances. She recently withdrew $4,000 from her retirement account to cover medical expenses associated with her cancer treatment.

"On my monthly income, I would not be able to afford my new medical expenses," said Weigand, 83, who lives in North Canton, Ohio. She is currently on a Medicare Advantage plan and was searching for new coverage as Medicare's open enrollment period began in October.

For older adults in the U.S., the rising cost of healthcare is hitting where it hurts most -- their pocketbooks. With Medicare costs, prescription drug prices, and long-term care expenses weighing on household budgets, their concerns could carry significant political weight heading into Tuesday's elections.

Weigand is part of a reliable voting bloc; 83% of voters over age 50 say they're extremely motivated to vote in the midterms, according to an AARP poll conducted in early October. Their concerns over managing healthcare costs put the issue of affordability squarely in the spotlight.

Here are some of their biggest healthcare concerns -- and how Weigand sees them.

THE SITUATION: Open enrollment for Medicare plans began in mid-October, and enrollees are facing a slew of changes that will go into effect in 2027.

During the enrollment period, people are considering higher Part D out-of-pocket caps, the end of temporary federal Part D premium subsidies, and new drug price negotiations. Additionally, in some states, insurers put caps on the number of people who can enroll in Advantage plans, pulling out of markets or eliminating plans that people have come to rely on.

Juliette Cubanski, PhD, MPH, director of KFF's program on Medicare policy, told the Associated Press that the open enrollment period offers enrollees "an opportunity to evaluate the coverage they have and see if there might be better options for them in terms of accessing the care that they need, at possibly a lower cost."

While Weigand is grateful to have the mental acuity to do the research and the spiritual community that she relies on, she worries about other older people who are struggling to plan for the year ahead. "There are so many changes, and a lot of people my age who don't know where to start," she said. "What do they do?"

Worry No. 2: The Affordability of Prescription Drugs

THE SITUATION: The Trump administration has made lowering drug prices a priority in its effort to show Americans that it is tackling soaring costs. Republican President Donald Trump has struck multiple most-favored-nation drug deals with pharmaceutical companies and has launched the discount drug website TrumpRx, among other steps.

In September, his administration said all 50 states would join in on a program set to provide most-favored-nation pricing to some drugs within the Medicaid program, bringing the net price of selected drugs down to prices paid in certain other countries.

Still, the cost of prescription drugs prevents some people from filling prescriptions. A KFF survey from March found that about 4 in 10 U.S. adults say they have not taken their medication as prescribed in the past year due to costs.

Weigand is considering rationing her drugs to save money. "I've been thinking -- if I could just do this every other day rather than every day to make the medicine last longer," she said. "Isn't that sad?"

The KFF report said about 1 in 5 people surveyed had cut pills in half or skipped doses of medicine in the prior year because of the cost.

Worry No. 3: The Costs of Long-Term Support

The costs of receiving long-term care -- whether from spouses, home-care aides, or friends -- have risen steeply for retirees.

An AARP report released Wednesday that examines out-of-pocket caregiving expenses finds that family caregivers are spending nearly 60% more supporting a family member or friend than 5 years ago. They spend an average of $11,508 a year supporting a family member or friend, compared with $7,242 in 2021, according to the report.

"We're already hearing from our members about healthcare affordability -- its prescription drug costs, caregiver costs, co-pays, premiums," said Nancy LeaMond, AARP's executive vice president and chief advocacy officer. "Not only are we trying to help family caregivers with their finances, but also to get them the kind of information they need to help their loved ones."

These worries are top of mind for older people. About one-third of Americans over 50 provide regular care to an adult relative or friend with a health issue or disability, according to a national poll on healthy aging conducted in September 2025 by the University of Michigan.

Worry No. 4: The Solvency of Social Security

The Social Security Administration announced this month that the annual cost-of-living adjustment for older adults and people with disabilities will rise by 3.6% beginning in 2027. That translates to $75 extra per month for the nearly 72 million people who receive monthly Social Security benefits and about 7.3 million beneficiaries who get Supplemental Security Income.

The agency faces an approaching bankruptcy date if a funding shortfall is not addressed by Congress.

The Social Security and Medicare trustees' report from June states that Social Security's combined trust funds, which cover old age and disability recipients, will be unable to pay full benefits beginning in 2034. After that, incoming revenue would cover about 83% of scheduled benefits.

With 2034 not far away, the upcoming Congress will be on the hook to determine how to shore up the program.

Matt Hogan, a partner at the Impact Research polling firm, has this advice to candidates in the closing days of the midterm campaign: "Regardless of party, to make inroads with these voters, Social Security and Medicare should definitely be part of their messaging."

#5758. Donated solar project powers community center in unincorporated New Mexico village  Solar Power World’s Projects of Impact

A building that started as a one-room schoolhouse has through the decades expanded to become a lifeline for a New Mexico community far from any city infrastructure, and a recent solar installation has brought new resilience to this place.

Children run outside of Ojo Sarco, New Mexico’s one-room schoolhouse in a photo from January 1943. The structure still stands today as the Ojo Sarco Community Center. Credit: John Collier, Jr./Library of Congress

The structure housing the Ojo Sarco Community Center (OSCC) was founded in the early 20th century. Ojo Sarco is an unincorporated community home to about 300 people that is located about 43 miles northeast from Santa Fe.

“We have no political voice, really,” said Carol Miller, a grant writer and board member for the Ojo Sarco Community Center. “We’re pretty neglected. We’ve lost resources in the past. We were the first wave of people to lose our post office many years ago. Everybody has to commute pretty much for everything.”

That commute is about 40 minutes round trip for basic amenities, she said.

The community center has tried to fill in those resource gaps with services like a twice-monthly food bank, summer meal programs for kids, safety classes and outings for its residents, such as a night at the opera. The OSCC also recently met all the qualifications to be a state library.

PPC Solar, also known as Paradise Power Company, from Taos, built a 7.5-kW solar project paired with 10.6-kWh of LFP energy storage at the OSCC. The contractor built the array at no-cost to the community center. Every year PPC donates a solar array to a regional nonprofit organization, and after three years of applying, the OSCC was awarded the project.

CEO Daniel Weinman’s father founded Paradise Power Company in the 1970s, and the company has installed solar in off-the-grid communities since the start. Its PV donation program is built out of that tradition.

“We’ve always wanted to find a way to give back to the community,” Weinman said. “The best thing that we found — and we’ve been doing it for over a decade now — is doing an annual nonprofit donation system … We’ve had farming organizations, land conservation organizations, and it goes on and on. Really, it’s about finding the right fit. We wanted to make a good impact for this community center, and we felt resiliency was the best way to do that.”

Solar Power World spoke to both PPC Solar and organizers with the OSCC about what effect this solar project has had in the village since it was built in fall 2025.

Larry Miller, project manager and OSCC board member: I think it’s a transformative project for our small community. We like to talk about the history of the community center, and now this is part of it. If someone doesn’t notice the solar panels on the roof, we point it out to them. It’s integrated into our lives here.

Carol Miller: It’s lowered our electric costs, which is really important for us, because we’re such a shoe-string operation. We’ve had some fun talks with the community because they all know we’ll have the charging capacity. We still have internet available, even with the power out. All the kids that come to our library programs, they’re solar oriented. The benefit of this company and its staff just has ripple effects throughout our greater community.

PPC Solar donated a solar plus storage project to the Ojo Sarco Community Center. This array will keep power running in this facility that has become a haven for Ojo Sarco residents. Credit: PPC Solar

Preston Bastardo, sales and design, PPC Solar: The building itself had some challenges with mounting the array on the roof, but that just took some exploration in the attic and making sure that we could land things where we needed to. The building has two large areas, and so fo installation of batteries had some restrictions, there was a good mechanical closet that we were able to use for batteries. Then as far as backup circuits, we did a partial facility backup where we had a critical loads panel next to their main panel. I think everything went pretty smoothly.

Weinman: Well, it really goes back to our roots. In 1972, there was no technology supporting solar at the time. My dad was a pioneer in the industry and he really developed his own controls and safety protections and found used panels and used batteries and was bringing power to places where there was no power. Our house, for example, we started with kerosene lanterns. We didn’t have electricity until we had a solar power system, and it was really life changing.

Being able to build on that legacy and look at our community in that light and be able to offer systems like this to communities that need it — I think that really adds to that legacy and really is profound in a lot of ways. It feels great to be able to do this and be in a position where we can help others realize and experience the benefits of solar power. We want to continue to participate in any way we can.

Carol Miller: We’re a very old community and proud of it. And having worked with local businesses and our electrical co-op, we know it’s really the exception and not the rule because we own our power. Any technology that is shared equally in a low-income community has ripple effects beyond what I think some of us imagined a number of years ago. It’s for everyone.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5756. CT Images Facilitate PCI's 'New Era': Planning Before Catheterization

— CCTA-based upstream decision-making claims noninferiority in P4 trial

Coronary CT angiography (CCTA) made it feasible and safe to plan percutaneous coronary intervention (PCI) procedures before cardiac catheterization, as shown by the open-label randomized P4 trial.

At 12 months, a CCTA-guided PCI strategy was noninferior to intravascular ultrasound (IVUS)-guided PCI with respect to target vessel failure -- a composite of death from cardiac causes, target vessel myocardial infarction (MI), or ischemia-driven target vessel revascularization -- occurring in 4.1% versus 4.6% of patients, respectively (P<0.001 for noninferiority), reported Carlos Collet, MD, PhD, of the Cardiovascular Research Foundation in New York City.

Additionally, CT guidance resulted in shorter procedures (46 vs 63 minutes) and reduced contrast utilization (150 vs 170 mL).

"P4 supports a CT-based pathway for PCI planning and guidance in selected patients referred to the cath lab ... What we're proposing is moving the PCI planning, not to do it in the cath lab, but basically shift that upstream to read, think, process, share the decision, and then go in the cath lab and treat," Collet concluded at the Transcatheter Cardiovascular Therapeutics (TCT) annual conference hosted by the Cardiovascular Research Foundation.

The P4 manuscript was simultaneously published in the New England Journal of Medicine.

Collet noted the current reliance on making PCI decisions in real time -- everything related to lesion preparation, stent diameter and length, and more -- during the procedure.

"That generates or increases the cognitive load of our brain, when we have to use it to make very complex decisions. So, what if we were to learn from our colleagues in the structural field and start planning PCI interventions? We will be better prepared, perhaps reduce the variability in treatment care, and that will give us an opportunity for shared decision-making with our patients," he said.

Of note, the comparator in P4, IVUS-guided PCI, is associated with a lower risk of adverse clinical events than angiography-guided PCI, but still only provides information after cardiac catheterization has begun.

TCT session discussant Emmanouil Brilakis, MD, PhD, of the Minneapolis Heart Institute Foundation, called P4 a "landmark" trial.

"The study does provide the clinical evidence we need to move to this new era that Dr. Collet alluded to, which is that of planning the procedures before we come to the cath lab," he said. "But there are many steps that we need to achieve to be able to get there. Number one is to have a high-quality scan. The scans in the study were screened and they were very high quality."

"We need to learn how to actually use the CT, how to look at the CT images, and implement them for planning our practices," Brilakis added.

Collet stressed the point that interventional cardiologists should take it upon themselves to look at the CCTA images, and not rely on the reports of the radiologists. "They [radiologists] don't know the details of the techniques that we're using. They don't know the details on when to modify the calcium, bifurcation treatment, etc. So we need to get used to looking at the pictures."

During a press conference, Collet alluded to a rising prevalence of patients undergoing CT scans before a PCI. He noted that the practice is now commonplace in the U.K.; in the U.S., it still varies widely with a national site average of about 10%, he estimated.

"Let us not forget that this requires contrast and radiation for the CT scan," Brilakis cautioned. "Therefore, this needs to be taken into account, and there is also some extra time for the patient to have this procedure."

P4, or the Precise Procedural and PCI Plan trial, was an open-label noninferiority trial that enrolled at 23 centers.

Participants were patients with a clinical indication for coronary revascularization who had undergone CCTA as part of routine clinical care before enrollment in the trial. Entry criteria included having at least one epicardial lesion with a diameter stenosis >70%, a CCTA-fractional flow reserve of ≤0.80, reference vessel diameter 2.5-4.0 mm, and no more than two target vessels.

There were 1,105 individuals randomized to CCTA or IVUS guidance of PCI. To avoid leaving underexpanded stents, Collet and colleagues mandated that the CT arm utilize stent enhancement tools based on angiography.

Median age was 65 years, and 21.7% of the cohort were women. About one in five had diabetes and fewer than 3% a prior PCI. The vast majority had stable ischemic heart disease (about 98%) as opposed to acute coronary syndrome, and the extent of coronary artery disease was mostly one vessel (64%). Average SYNTAX scores were 8 for the CCTA group and 9 for IVUS.

Study authors noted that the burden of coronary calcification was substantial in this cohort, with mean coronary calcium scores of approximately 500, and over 10% of participants judged to have severe calcification.

Participants who ultimately ended up receiving PCI came out to about 93% in both arms.

The investigators reported that beyond the aforementioned primary analysis, CCTA guidance continued to have favorable results when target vessel failure was broken down to its individual components:

Additionally, a per-protocol analysis was consistent with the intention-to-treat analysis.

The noninferiority margin used for the study was an absolute risk difference of 4.5 percentage points. With that met, the investigators proceeded to test for superiority of CCTA guidance, which did not meet significance.

Safety events in P4 did not raise red flags for CCTA-guided PCI. There was no major excess of all-cause death (0.2% vs 0.5% in the IVUS-guided PCI group), bleeding of Bleeding Academic Research Consortium (BARC) type 2, 3, or 5 (1.4% vs 1.3%), or BARC type 3 or 5 bleeding (0.4% vs 0.7%).

However, the P4 trial had lower than expected event rates and is still pending a final 5-year follow-up, Collet acknowledged.

Moreover, among the trial's exclusion criteria were ST-segment elevation MI, left main coronary artery disease, previous PCI in a target vessel, previous coronary artery bypass grafting, and a severely low estimated glomerular filtration rate.

Collet reported that CT planning appeared disproportionately beneficial for patients with the most complex coronary disease. As such, a new trial, P5, is now ongoing, testing the strategy specifically in this population.

#5755. Drive Forward or Turn Back? Four Reasons to Vote for a Strong Automotive Package

The coming weeks will be crucial for the future of the EU’s car and van CO₂ standards and Clean Corporate Vehicles Regulation — a choice that will shape Europe’s car market for the next decade.

Driving forward means a competitive, EU-made EV industry and clean and affordable mobility; turning back means locking in energy dependence, industrial decline, and polluting, expensive mobility paid for by citizens and companies.

Households could save €70 a month by switching from petrol to electric. Large companies will save even more. Strong CO₂ standards mean affordable electric cars. Electrifying corporate fleets will speed up supply.

Weak CO₂ standards will delay the moment electric cars cost the same as petrol cars. Citizens are stuck in a fossil fuel trap: diesel already costs €30 extra every time they fill their tank.

Europe is going electric: 38% of new cars sold in August were fully electric. Strong CO₂ standards keep European carmakers in the race. Made-in-EU requirements mean more jobs and investment.

Weak car CO₂ standards and corporate fleet targets means 22 million electric vehicles won’t be built in EU factories (T&E based on ACEA). Clinging to the combustion engine won’t protect jobs or investments from our competitors.

Cars and vans produce 16% of the EU’s CO₂ emissions. A strong automotive package puts them on track, keeping 720 million tonnes of CO₂ out of Europe’s air by 2050.

Weaker rules on cars CO2 standards would add 1.4 billion tonnes of CO2 by 2050. After Europe’s summer of climate disasters, this rollback would threaten our climate objectives and future generations.

Electric cars saved €2.9 billion in oil imports last year alone. The EU is more independent by going electric with locally sourced renewables that strengthen our energy security. Weakening CO2 standards could mean 900 million more barrels of imported oil by 2035.

Giving combustion engines on the road longer will cost Europe €74 billion. Paid by households, straight to oil-rich countries.

#5754. Frontline Treatment in Multiple Myeloma Is Changing

— Experts discuss the broader use of four-drug regimens and whether treatment can be shortened

Ahead of the American Society of Hematology (ASH) annual meeting, frontline treatment for multiple myeloma continues to evolve, with quadruplet regimens increasingly becoming standard for both transplant-eligible and -ineligible patients.

MedPage Today brought together three expert leaders in the field: Moderator Joseph Mikhael, MD, is joined by Amrita Krishnan, MD, and Tulio Rodriguez, MD, all of the City of Hope, for a roundtable discussion. This first of five exclusive episodes focuses on the changing frontline treatment landscape, including the expanding role of quadruplet therapy and whether risk status and measurable residual disease (MRD) can help determine how long patients remain on maintenance therapy.

Following is a transcript of their remarks:

Mikhael: Hello, and welcome to this MedPage roundtable on multiple myeloma. There is so much going on in multiple myeloma, and it's my privilege today to chair this roundtable. My name is Joe Mikhael. I'm a professor at the Translational Genomics Research Institute, which is part of the City of Hope family. And on the subject of the City of Hope family, I have two very dear friends and colleagues who are joining me today and I'd love to have them introduce themselves, Drs. Krishnan and Rodriguez. Dr. Krishnan, why don't we start with you?

Krishnan: Hi, my name is Amrita Krishnan. Thank you, Joe. I'm the division chief of myeloma at the City of Hope Cancer Center and head of the Judy and Bernard Briskin Center for Myeloma at City of Hope.

Rodriguez: Thank you, Joe. My name is Tulio Rodriguez. I'm the director of the bone marrow transplant program and cellular therapy at City of Hope in Chicago. It's a pleasure being with you.

Mikhael: Wonderful. So we have three states represented. I'm from Arizona, Amrita is from California, Tulio is up in, as you mentioned, Chicago, Illinois, so we're just delighted to have everybody together. Let's dive into topic number one, which is how is frontline therapy changing in multiple myeloma?

We know that actually at every phase of myeloma, we've seen changes, frontline therapy, transplants, maintenance therapy, early relapse, late relapse, everything is changing. But we know what we do initially for patients is critical, how we initially treat them. And maybe I'll start with you, Tulio. I mean, if you were to summarize the major shift of what's been happening in frontline therapy from, let's say, 3 or 4 years ago to now, what has been that major change?

Rodriguez: I believe that for newly diagnosed patients, the quadruplets are becoming more accepted. I mean, recent data, long-term follow-up of the PERSEUS trial, for example, is showing a major benefit. For those who are considered fit and perhaps transplant-eligible, quadruplets based on the dara [daratumumab (Darzalex)] backbone is becoming the standard of care. It's being validated now.

Even for the transplant-ineligible patients, there's some data that was recently shown at the IMS [International Myeloma Society] 2026, as I'm sure you're aware of, where the BENEFIT trial gives us a long follow-up of this anti-CD38 with isatuximab (Sarclisa), but also bortezomib (Velcade) for 18 months, etc. So that is becoming also a good induction for newly diagnosed patients who are not eligible for transplantation.

Mikhael: Yeah, no, I mean, I think you've summarized it beautifully. It's quads, quads, quads, right? We have follow-up with PERSEUS data, which is now pushing us to think that progression-free survival may be over 12 years in transplant-eligible patients who receive transplant, but also from both the CEPHEUS, the benefit from IMROZ, now we have three large trials in transplant-ineligible patients either with daratumumab or isatuximab, also demonstrating this really remarkable outcome and sustained.

And Amrita, I was going to ask you, I'm sure you saw the sustained MRD data both from CEPHEUS and from BENEFIT where we're using either daratumumab or isatuximab. I mean, how is that changing the way you're treating your transplant-ineligible patients?

Krishnan: I think it's very good news for transplant ineligible, that it really does say that quadruplet therapy is sort of the standard of care for patients because you have the same goals now of achieving a deep response and sustaining that response.

Having said that, none of them really ask the question, which I thought you were going to ask, Joe, is about ENDURANCE. Of all of these trials, they still treat till disease progression. And of course that's the great unknown of, when can you stop therapy?

Mikhael: Yeah, and I'm definitely going to come to that. Talk about leading the witness. This is a great point. But just before we get to the maintenance discussion on length of therapy, my other quick comment I want to add here is what we've learned through these different trials as well is even though we think of a quad as being one phenomenon, I think how people pragmatically apply that quad in the clinic is critical. We're learning more and more that patients don't need as high a dose of an immunomodulatory drug. They can use the bortezomib once weekly and even start backing off after several months.

And please, you both know my soapbox, please use less dexamethasone. We are using way too much dexamethasone. So when someone says, oh, can I really use a quad in a transplant-ineligible patient, they have this sort of hesitation that I'm coming at the patient too strongly.

But for so many of my patients, using the CD38 antibody with a lower dose of lenalidomide [Revlimid], weekly bortezomib, maybe even soon going down to every other week bortezomib, low-dose dexamethasone, and even stopping it after the first few months really makes it tenable.

But before we leave this topic, I do want to come back to what you raised, Amrita, which is we've had this modality in frontline therapy of treat forever, treat until progression, frankly, in both the transplant-eligible and -ineligible patients, whether it's after transplant or just after induction therapy.

And I'll ask you first, Amrita, do you believe the ENDURANCE trial, which for those who aren't familiar, was a study, actually, I was a part of the study when we designed it 14 years ago at Mayo Clinic, you need ENDURANCE to wait for the results, but where we looked at 2 years of maintenance lenalidomide versus indefinite in transplant-ineligible patients, the primary outcome was overall survival and it turned out to be the same.

So whether or not people stayed on longer-term lenalidomide, it didn't contribute to their overall survival. It may have extended progression-free survival a little bit. But the quick question then to you, Amrita, is does this mean now that you're stopping maintenance in transplant-eligible and -ineligible patients after 2 or maybe 3 years, or are you approaching it differently?

Krishnan: At the risk of sounding like a politician on CNN in the morning and not answering the question directly, I do have to put the caveats in of ENDURANCE -- overall survival in part depends on the treatments you're going to get at relapse, first of all. So I think you have to take that caveat with it in terms of if you are going to stop maintenance, what do you have at time of relapse?

The second part is, because ENDURANCE didn't have MRD data, there was a progression-free survival benefit to still continuing maintenance. It was modest, I think it was 4 to 6 months, and ENDURANCE was standard-risk disease. So putting all those caveats in there, I would say I've already been stopping maintenance in patients, but I've been doing it in the context of using MRD to really help guide that decision-making in patients.

And there's already been data out there that has used MRD from the Greek data, from even Ben Derman's data from MRD to stop, where you can stop maintenance. So I do think you can. I just don't think it's as wide a swath as ENDURANCE that stop it at everyone at 2 years, right?

Mikhael: Absolutely. And in fact, you're predicting our second topic, which we'll come to in just a moment, which is MRD guidance. But Tulio, I want to give you an opportunity to comment here as well. In general, are you now thinking about shorter terms of maintenance for your at least standard-risk patients with myeloma?

Rodriguez: Right. And you're certainly right, and I agree completely with both of you that response by itself does not determine if I'm going to stop the maintenance or not. You mentioned standard risk, for example, and some people might use as staging and you come with a model with several characteristics where you can predict that will be effective interrupting maintenance, for example. But I think that MRD is going to be a strong pillar in that determination in combination with other factors.

Mikhael: Wonderful. Well, as we wrap up this first session, how things have really changed in frontline therapy, we've talked about moving towards quadruplets, both in transplant-eligible/-ineligible, and maybe even shortening the time of patients being on therapy guided by risk status and by MRD status.

Thank you very much both of you for this discussion, and we look forward to having you at future discussions as we continue to think about multiple myeloma.

#5753. The industry races into Las Vegas for RE+

The largest tradeshow focused on the U.S. solar, storage and clean energy markets is back in Las Vegas for 2026. RE+ was the 24th largest tradeshow in the United States in 2025 by attendance, and this year’s showing (Nov. 16-19) is expected to be just as large.

The Formula 1 series will race the Las Vegas Grand Prix the weekend after RE+ concludes. Photos courtesy: F1 and the Las Vegas Grand Prix

The U.S. solar and storage industries have gotten so big that it appears only the Las Vegas Convention Center can accommodate a tradeshow bringing together nearly 40,000 clean energy professionals in one place. RE+ will be held at the Las Vegas Convention Center each November for the foreseeable future.

The convention center is tucked away from the main strip of casinos, but there is still plenty in the area to see and do. The site is also easily accessible, with a stop on the Las Vegas Monorail.

Conference education programming begins on Monday and sessions continue throughout the show at various stages on the exhibit hall floor. The exhibition opens on Tuesday.

Exhibitors will be spread across three halls in the convention center — the main entrance is at West Hall which extends via an over-street walkway to North Hall, and then Central Hall is accessed beyond a lobby area. There will also be a few outdoor booths to peruse located near food trucks. Visiting outdoor exhibits in November will be more comfortable, as the average high temperature is just 70°F, unlike the late summer heat of September.

Special events at RE+ include a golf tournament on Monday and the Run with the Sun 5K on Tuesday. The “Energy in Motion” block party will be at the Convention Center on Tuesday starting at 7 p.m. in the West Hall.

Formula 1 will race the Las Vegas Grand Prix the weekend following RE+. The course takes drivers down the Las Vegas Strip and around the Sphere, and road closures to the public will begin on Thursday. The Las Vegas Convention Center is several blocks north of the racing circuit, so the conference shouldn’t experience disruptions. #5752. EVs Sales Are Rising And Another Gasmobile Bites The Dust

The automotive press is practically aflame with rumors that Ford is replacing its Ford Escape crossover SUV with an electric version, also to be named Escape. That has raised quite a ripple across the esoteric field of car-naming. Normally when a model retires, so does the name. The US auto industry is littered with has-beens, wanna-be’s, never-was, and just plain exhausted cars that retired along with their names. Hmmm….perhaps Ford is sending a not so subtle signal about the future of EV sales in the US…

By now, everyone knows that EV sales cratered last fall, after US President Donald Trump and his Republican majority in Congress engineered the “OBBA” tax bill to eliminate federal tax credits for EVs.

Still, EV sales did not actually stop in their tracks, let alone reverse course. A fresh wave of off-lease, used EVs helped keep the momentum up earlier this year, and some automakers (looking at you, Subaru) stayed on track with plans for new EVs in the US.

Plug-in hybrids also factored in as a sort of gateway drug, with Audi CEO Gernot Döllner among those looking forward to strong EV sales in that category.

EV stakeholders must smell blood in the water as Election Day 2026 approaches. With polls indicating that  Trump will lose his chokehold on federal EV policy, news about EVs has been tumbling through the Intertubes over the past several weeks. Among the examples, Slate Auto opened its first showroom in Chicago, and General Motors unveiled its gen-4 EV platform.

Somewhat bizarrely, the Trump administration also contributed to the news cycle with the launch of the new “BattMan” battery collaboration earlier this week. Under the umbrella of the US Department of Energy, BattMan hooks up federal laboratories with academic and private sector partners to push the envelope on next-gen batteries for transportation applications as well as stationary energy storage.

Of course, Tesla also weighed in with its “flying car” stunt, but that was just a stunt. Over in the world of street legal EVs, Ford has been working the publicity machine to gin up interest in the forthcoming Fathom all-electric pickup truck, which is on track for volume production early next year at an MSRP starting at the affordability benchmark of $28,350 with a built-in inverter that cuts the cost of bidirectional charging, too.

The Fathom illustrates how much blood, sweat, and tears go into naming a new car. It’s a mid-sized pickup like the popular Maverick, but Ford went off in a new direction with the Fathom, considering hundreds of options over a months-long naming journey before settling on a maritime-adjacent reference to deep understanding.

The Fathom will be the first EV to roll off Ford’s new “Universal EV” manufacturing platform, and the automaker has indicated that the next vehicle will be a compact SUV. The consensus has settled on something resembling the Escape crossover SUV, all the more so because Ford has been laying plans to retire the gasmobile…but maybe, not the name.

That’s where the ears of the automotive press have pricked up, drawing attention to the Escape landing page that depicts the rear end of a forlorn, rain-splattered Escape heading off into the dustbin of automotive history along with its dual exhaust system.

And, here’s where things get interesting. The text on the landing page simply notes that the Ford Escape is “tapping the brakes,” which has been interpreted as a pausing message, not a forever message.

However, on the same page Ford provides a good reason why the 2026 Escape will not fly. The car is not for sale in California and several other states.

“The 2026 Ford Escape® is not certified for emissions requirements with the California Air Resources Board,” Ford states in a drop-down link. “Therefore, it is not available for purchase by customers intending to register their vehicle in states that have adopted California’s certification requirements for the 2026 model year, specifically California, Massachusetts, Oregon, New York, Washington, and Vermont.”

Ford goes on to suggest two other gas-powered SUVs that are street legal in California-compliant states, the 2026 Bronco Sport SUV and the 2026 Ford Explorer SUV.

So, is Ford really banking on the Escape brand to boost its EV sales in the US? Maybe! In August, the Detroit Free Press summarized a swirl of rumors around the idea of an electric Escape, citing insider sources including a Ford dealer with access to the company’s program codes.

However, in the same report, Reuters cites an inside source who indicated that in 2029, Ford plans to introduce a new entry-level SUV in hybrid and gasoline powertrains.

“At the Las Vegas meeting, the executives, the dealer said, assured dealers that the automaker is committed to hybrid powertrains going forward,” Reuters emphasized.

Hmmmm…so much for an all-electric escape. Back in May, the Canadian Ford dealer MSAFord also raised some questions about an all-electric replacement for the Escape in a blog post (breaks added for readabilty):

“Production of the Ford Escape and Lincoln Corsair ended at the Louisville Assembly plant last December, leaving a giant hole in The Blue Oval’s lineup as it pertains to small crossovers.”

“This is particularly true of the Escape, which disappeared at a time when new vehicle shoppers are increasingly looking for more affordable options, too.

“However, back in December 2022, a report surfaced from AutoForecast Solutions, claiming that the Ford Escape would live on as an all-electric crossover, though it has thus far remained unclear if that plan is intact ever since”

Readers, what do you think? Is half an EV better than none? Full EV sales have yet to surge, but plug-in hybrids have become more popular on the heels of US President Donald Trump’s witless decision to bomb Iran last February.

EREV (extended range EVs) are another halfway-there option that will factor into an uptick in EV sales. The Scout Motors branch of Volkswagen, for example, has stated that EREVs account for the majority of its reservations so far.

Then there’s the pure EV players, including robotaxi stakeholders alongside Slate and Rivian, which is forging ahead with plans to construct a new factory in Georgia.

As for industry leader Tesla, that’s a whole ‘nother can of worms. CEO Elon Musk has earned a fair share of bad publicity in recent years, as depicted in the new “Musk” documentary. However, the film wrapped sometime last year, leaving Musk’s latest hi-jinks for the sequel.
