#5751. Spondyloarthritis Biologics 'Game-Changing' for Juvenile Disease

— Risk-benefit discussion encouraged amid unanswered questions and use of off-label drugs

Researchers continue to lay the groundwork for expanding treatment in juvenile spondyloarthritis (SpA), borrowing from advances in disease-modifying anti-rheumatic drugs (DMARDs) in adults.

Earlier this year, the interleukin (IL)-17 inhibitor ixekizumab (Taltz) was shown to be well tolerated and effective for the treatment of children with enthesitis-related arthritis (ERA) and juvenile psoriatic arthritis (PsA) in the phase III COSPIRIT-JIA trial.

At week 16, 90% and 86% of biological DMARD-naive and -experienced participants receiving ixekizumab met the prespecified definition of treatment success -- a juvenile idiopathic arthritis-American College of Rheumatology (ACR) 30 response, defined as at least 30% improvement from baseline in three of any six core outcome variables, with no more than one of the other variables worsening by more than 30% -- in the 101-person study, giving ixekizumab a potential expanded pediatric indication outside plaque psoriasis.

Meanwhile, fellow IL-17-targeting secukinumab (Cosentyx) is still the only FDA-approved biologic for ERA in children. With few options officially on the market, there is currently more off-label use of biologics and targeted synthetic DMARDs in children versus adults.

"Biologics targeting TNF [tumor necrosis factor] and IL-17A have revolutionized the way we treat SpA and are changing the long-term outcomes," Robert Colbert, MD, PhD, a pediatric rheumatologist at the National Institute of Arthritis and Musculoskeletal and Skin Diseases, told MedPage Today.

One idea behind the push to bring these therapies to children is that earlier initiation can help achieve better outcomes by preventing the progressive involvement of the axial skeleton in later-stage SpA.

"In adult patients, both biologics can slow radiographic progression, which is the formation of new bone in the axial skeleton that can lead to spinal ankylosis over many years," Colbert explained. "While excess bone formation is rarely seen in children, these biologics started early in the course of axial arthritis may have long-term benefits in preventing the need for hip joint replacements and limiting radiographic progression."

There is therefore much interest in better identification of these juvenile cases.

"Recognizing early axial disease in children continues to be a challenge and can cause delays in starting the most effective treatments," Colbert noted. "This problem should improve now that there are classification criteria for axial juvenile spondyloarthritis."

Juvenile and adult SpA are considered to exist on a continuum but do have important distinctions.

Compared with adult disease, juvenile SpA is typically marked by occasional mild to moderate flare-ups of hip or peripheral disease. ERA is considered a juvenile form of ankylosing spondylitis (AS), with juvenile PsA being the correlate to adult PsA, all falling in the SpA family of inflammatory rheumatic diseases related to arthritis of the spine and joints.

"While research suggests that the immune dysregulation leading to SpA is similar in children and adults, there are important age-related differences in how the disease manifests, particularly when it first starts," Colbert said.

With less axial or spine involvement in the early course of juvenile SpA, arthritis in the spine or sacroiliac joint can develop 5-10 years after an ERA diagnosis. Some studies have observed up to 40% of children with ERA going on to develop juvenile AS. Ultimately, up to 20% of individuals with SpA experience disease onset in childhood, sometimes as young as 5 years.

"Overall, the biologics have been game-changing in the treatment of juvenile SpA," Dax Rumsey, MD, MSc, of Stollery Children's Hospital and the University of Alberta in Edmonton, Canada, told MedPage Today. "Having said that, there are still patients who do not respond to any of the available options, so more research and development in the area is certainly needed."

The need for evidence is clear in a 2026 update to the ACR guideline for the treatment of AS and axial SpA, in which many recommendations are only deemed "conditional" on account of the "very low" level of evidence.

Moreover, while the guideline strongly recommends that affected children and adults alike receive a TNF or IL-17 inhibitor for their initial biologic DMARD following first-line therapy with nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs, there is no official preference for any single agent in these classes.

"However, it is generally recommended to choose a TNF inhibitor first, as long as there are no major contraindications to it for the patient," noted Rumsey.

Safety is another major concern when it comes to biologics in children and adolescents. Anti-TNF therapy, for example, is associated with infection-related complications and increased risks of autoimmune disease; IL-17 inhibitors have generally been linked to infections (particularly candidiasis), inflammatory bowel disease, and mental health conditions.

"No biologic is completely safe, but with proper pre-screening and monitoring, TNF and IL-17A inhibitors are generally considered safe, although IL-17A inhibitors are avoided in patients with inflammatory bowel disease like Crohn's disease and ulcerative colitis," Colbert said.

Ixekizumab reportedly showed an adult-like safety profile in children in COSPIRIT-JIA. Similarly, in the phase III JUNIPERA trial, secukinumab's safety profile in ERA and juvenile PsA was comparable to that in prior reports in adult plaque psoriasis, PsA, non-radiographic axial SpA, and AS.

"There are more long-term safety data in adults, but no particular safety signals have been found in children, suggesting that the risk-benefit ratio is quite acceptable," Colbert said. "There should always be a discussion of risks and benefits between the patient and/or parent and treating physician."

#5750. Electric Hydrofoils Save Energy, But Do They Earn Their Price?

Hydrofoils have an obvious appeal. Lift a boat’s hull clear of the water, reduce drag and move passengers faster while consuming less energy. I’ve written before about the proposed electric hydrofoil ferry between Vancouver, Bowen Island and Gibsons, where floating logs, coastal conditions and uncertain passenger demand make the economics difficult. Hydrofoils have been commercially available for decades, but their operating niche remains narrower than their technical appeal suggests.

A different opportunity emerged when Antwerp pilotage operator Brabo ordered an Artemis EF-12 electric hydrofoil pilot boat in August 2024. The vessel combines electric propulsion with submerged foils and active controls. Artemis has also demonstrated ship-contact manoeuvres using its EF-12 workboat platform, while the EF-12 Pilot design entered sea trials in 2026. The handling benefits are relevant to pilot transfers even when the boat isn’t operating at full speed.

The financial question is whether a hydrofoil can spend enough of its working life taking advantage of its design. Antwerp pilot boats work around locks, docks, ship movements and changing assignments. Reaching a high maximum speed is of limited value when the vessel spends much of its day manoeuvring or travelling through constrained waterways.

I tested that question using a Python-based operating model with synthetic assignments and three hypothetical sets of port-speed permissions. Depending on how much suitable high-speed water was available, the EF-12 spent zero, approximately 6% or approximately 13% of its underway time foilborne. These are modeled scenarios, not measurements of Brabo’s operations or actual Antwerp speed authorizations.

The resulting financial comparison was unflattering to the hydrofoil. In the central case, the EF-12’s modeled fifteen-year discounted cost was approximately €5.32 million, compared with €3.81 million for a conventional diesel tender, a premium of about 40%. Both figures include purchasing new vessels, rather than comparing a new hydrofoil with the marginal cost of keeping an existing diesel boat.

Electric propulsion still brought substantial energy benefits. The hydrofoil’s problem was that lower purchased energy did not compensate for the assumed capital cost and the limited opportunity to operate in its most efficient regime. Its active controls and potential ride-quality benefits remain relevant, but the available evidence did not justify assigning them a financial value.

An alternative electric approach also deserves consideration. Norway’s Moen Marin and Pascal Technologies have developed an air-cavity electric personnel vessel that reduces hull resistance without lifting most of the vessel above the water. It performed better financially than the hydrofoil in the exploratory comparison, although the existing craft was designed for aquaculture work and would require appropriate adaptation and certification before serving as a pilot tender.

The lesson resembles the one I drew from the Vancouver hydrofoil ferry proposal. Hydrofoils can be excellent machines, especially where sustained high-speed passages and suitable sea conditions allow them to exploit their advantages. In congested harbours, their additional complexity and capital cost need much stronger justification.

Different routes, operating permissions and vessel prices could produce different results. For commercial fleet decisions, the relevant benchmark is the work the vessel actually completes and the total cost of providing that service.

#5749. Fuel From Bamboo Costs Just $2.75 Per Gallon

At CleanTechnica, we are passionate champions of the “electrify everything” movement promoted by Tony Seba and Mark Jacobson, among others. However, being realistic, internal combustion engines will continue to play an important role in transportation by land, sea, and air for the remainder of this century and perhaps beyond.

Almost any plant that grows could potentially be turned into a biofuel with a lower emissions footprint than gasoline or diesel. Currently in the US, millions of tons of corn are used every year to make ethanol, which is then blended with gasoline. In theory, the ethanol helps lower emissions, but the reduction, if any, is modest and it decreases fuel economy, so motorist are actually using more fuel than they would if they used ordinary, unadulterated gasoline.

The other part of the equation is that the conversion process itself requires a fair amount of energy and makes its own greenhouse gas emissions, which politicians conveniently choose to ignore. Ethanol, then, is mostly a scam that rewards farmers, but has few other benefits. While the science is sound, the rewards are far less than claimed. However, farmers are an important political group and no one wants to tell them that corn for ethanol is no longer a thing.

Researchers at Worcester Polytechnic Institute think they have found a better way. In a study published September 24, 2026 in the Royal Society of Chemistry journal Sustainable Energy And Fuels, Michael Timko, the head of the chemical engineering department at WPI, and  Timothy Woodard, PhD, claim that bamboo may be the perfect source material for making biofuels. In the published study, they write:

“Corn stover, an agricultural residue widely available in the U.S., has been one of the most studied biomass sources for biofuel production.6 Yet its relatively modest yields and high ash content have created significant challenges for scaling up production. Alternative feedstocks such as switch grass have been explored, but these offer only incremental improvements in performance relative to corn stover, particularly in ash content and growth rate. These limitations highlight the importance of identifying and evaluating less conventional biomass sources that may offer superior attributes for fuel production.

“Bamboo has emerged as a strong candidate due to its rapid growth, high productivity per unit area, and low input requirements. Some bamboo species reach their full height within a few months and mature within four years. Bamboo’s deep root systems improve soil stability, promote subsurface carbon sequestration, and support water conservation. It can also be grown on marginal or degraded land, reducing competition with food crops and offering economic opportunities for rural communities.

“Critically, bamboo also possesses an exceptionally low ash content compared with most lignocellulosic feedstocks, which minimizes inorganic fouling and catalyst poisoning during thermochemical conversion and improves the quality of the resulting biocrude. Furthermore, the presence of ash complicates thermochemical conversion to products, with frequently reported adverse effects on yields.

“The characteristics of fast growth, low fertilizer and water use, and low ash content make bamboo stand out as a promising source of biomass that can achieve economic competitiveness that has not been obtained with other feedstocks. Unfortunately, despite these advantages, bamboo remains largely underexplored as a feedstock for liquid fuel production, especially in thermochemical conversion processes such as hydrothermal liquefaction (HTL).”

Here’s the best part. The researchers believe biofuel from bamboo could be commercially viable at a price of $2.75 a gallon. That’s less than gasoline at the moment and less than half the cost of diesel. What’s more, when compared to diesel, the resulting emissions are up to 26 times lower. So let’s see:  Less than half the cost and only 5 percent of the emissions? Think anyone would take that deal? Oh, you betcha!

Bamboo is a grass, and one of the fastest-growing plants on Earth. Some species grow several dozen inches in a day. Because of this fast growth, the US could make as much fuel from bamboo as it makes ethanol from corn while using only 13 percent as much land.

“I think this research is especially timely and important because fuel prices are high and the transportation sector produces more greenhouse gas emissions in the U.S. than any other,” said professor Timko in a press release. “Bamboo would use different land than we are using for food production, so there is no food versus fuel trade-off. Farmers win, consumers win.

Using bamboo for fuel can reduce greenhouse gasses in two ways, the researchers maintain. First, the process to convert it into biocrude is more efficient than it is for other feedstocks. Second, bamboo itself captures and stores large amounts of carbon both above and below ground.

Unlike annual crops used for many biofuels, bamboo is a perennial grass that can produce large amounts of biomass with relatively low agricultural inputs. Compared with other perennial grasses, bamboo has very low ash content (less than 1% by dry weight), meaning that the conversion process generates negligible waste.

The economic modeling conducted by the researchers indicates that bamboo-based fuel could sell for as little as $2.75 per gasoline equivalent under mature commercial-scale conditions, accounting for the money required to build and operate the fuel plant, suggesting strong economic competitiveness as the renewable-fuel industry expands. Considering the ability of bamboo to store large amounts of carbon in its underground rhizomes, producing fuel from bamboo would generate a greenhouse gas intensity of 3.5 grams of carbon dioxide-equivalent per megajoule of energy, the researchers report.

“The U.S. has enormous land area under cultivation for corn ethanol, a fuel which is often blended into gasoline,” said professor Timko. “Freeing up at least some of that land could allow for more food production and reduce the burden on the environment. Bamboo may eventually offer industry a way to generate low carbon liquid fuels at scale. With further research and development, bamboo could be a compelling option for decarbonization in sectors that are difficult to electrify, including heavy duty transport and aviation fuel supply chains.”

The researchers also point out that bamboo can be cultivated on former industrial land or heavily farmed properties where other crops and vegetation struggle to grow. Their research found the growth rate of bamboo (22–44 tons per hectare per year) “is at least three times that of corn stover and at least twice that of switch grass.

Based on the lowest growth rate, an idealized circular [bamboo] plantation with a 30 mile radius corresponds to an estimated 16.1 million dry tons of biomass per year. Ash content, a critical parameter for processing efficiency and waste generation, was less than 1 percent in bamboo, compared with 4.8–7.3 percent for corn stover and 2.2–11.9 percent for switch grass.

With such data in mind, expect the current US administration to impose a heavy tax on bamboo based biofuel to protect the profits of the oil industry and corn growers. Nothing must be allowed to challenge the hegemony of fossil fuels or the profits of Big Ag. And please don’t tell the head doofus that the WPI research was funded in part by Pacific Northwest National Laboratory and the National Science Foundation. If he found that out, his head might explode!

#5748. Electric Vehicle Council Celebrating 10 Years of EV Progress in Australia

The Electric Vehicle Council has been tracking the progress of electric vehicle uptake in Australia over the last ten years. Their latest report is a celebration of achievements. It is 144 pages long, and you can read the entire report here. I will just be hitting the highlights and providing some comments along the way.

In a demonstration of how fast the EV transition is moving in Australia, the EVC report projections to the end of the year are already hopelessly out of date, with a surge over the last three months looking set to continue through to the end of the year. Nevertheless, the report makes encouraging reading.

Both BEV and PHEV sales have doubled comparing H1 2025 with H2 2026. A new EV is sold in Australia every 100 seconds as Australians seek to cut costs and gain energy security. The ratio of BEV to PHEV sits at 2:1, similar to many other countries. The report warns of a possible “fragility” in Australia’s EV sales due to increasing demand for public charging and the possible introduction of a road user charge.

The report says that so far installation of public charging has kept up with demand. This is due to the fact that most Australians charge at home. There are 4268 AC and DC public charging locations available, an increase of 18% year on year and 11,600 public AC and DC connectors, a 31% increase year on year.

Political threats might also derail the electric train of progress. Australia’s right wing political parties are threatening to remove the New Vehicle Efficiency Standards and the Fringe Benefit Tax exemption should they gain power. The battle is not yet won. Intense lobbying by vested interests in the auto industry (FCAI) has led to significant misrepresentation in the media. The FUD is ever with us.

The report notes that the FBT exemption has been reviewed and extended to 2029. This may be long enough for EVs to enter the second-hand market where most Australians buy their cars. Australia has set a target of 5 million BEVs on the road by 2035 — “uptake must keep accelerating” if the country is to achieve this target. At the time of this report’s publication, there were 612,000 BEVs on Australian roads.

The top 5 best-selling plugin EVs in the Australian market over the past 12 months have been: Tesla Model Y (20,400), BYD Sealion 7 (12,500), BYD Shark 6 PHEV (9,500), Geely EX 5 (6,800), and Jaecoo J5 (6,000). I don’t expect this to change as we rush towards the end of the year. CleanTechnica readers would have been familiar with this lineup from regular reports. For the latest one, see here.

A comparison with CleanTechnica’s figures and those of the EVC will demonstrate how rapidly sales are increasing. YTD figures of each model listed in the CleanTechnica report are almost double that in the EVC report.

There is quite the disparity between the takeup of vehicle classes, with buses leading the way. Thirty two percent of new buses sold in H1 2026 were battery electric, compared to passenger vehicles 26%, vans and trucks a miserable 1%. I am very surprised at this, as small business and its need for last-mile delivery would benefit from reducing their costs for transport. According to the report, there are 16 different vans and 30 trucks to choose from. Julie Delvecchio, the CEO of EVC, calls this “the single biggest unclaimed opportunity left.” Perhaps Australia needs some low-emissions zones in our cities? Sydney, Melbourne, and Brisbane would certainly benefit from this measure.

Utes make up a sizeable portion of the Australian market. At the moment, there are only 5 plugin utes available (mainly plugin hybrids like the BYD Shark 6). When this part of the market shifts to battery electric, I expect the game will be over for diesel.

The EVC supports a federal (as opposed to state by state) road user charge, which is appropriate and enables the benefits of EVs to be shared with all Australians. In other words, “a fair go.” As an EV driver, I am not opposed to an RUC in principle.

A series of videos about EV charging to educate and build confidence for the public have been produced by the EVC — you can get a taste here.

So far in 2026, ANCAP (Australasian New Car Assessment Program) has declared that 6 out 7 of the safest cars in Australia are EVs. Despite the squealing of legacy automakers about the NVES (New Vehicle Efficiency Standards), carmakers are easily meeting their emissions reduction targets. There is now serious consideration of EVs as a part of Australia’s energy system (V2G). We are moving beyond the early adopter phase and into the early majority — how soon we start the steep ascent up the “S” curve remains to be seen. Sales will certainly be helped by reduced prices of EVs. The BYD Atto 1 (aka Dolphin Mini, Seagull) is now the cheapest car of any drivetrain — less than AU$20,000.

The EVC report tells us that the same is now happening in the van segment. Electric vans are quickly approaching price parity with diesel. Again, events have overtaken the EVC report — three new vans have just launched with prices below AU$50,000, undercutting brands from Korea and Europe. This looks like the beginning of a price war in this sector. The absolute price leader is the LDV eDeliver 5 from SAIC. Then comes the Geely Farizon V7E. The best legacy automakers can offer is the Ford Transit City. The specs are very similar.

Comparing the eDeliver 5 to similar diesel configurations, it appears that the only advantage for diesel is double the range. For city runs, the electric vehicle is the best option. With fuel insecurity, increased prices at the pump, and lower maintenance, I expect there to be a surge in this sector. Year to date up till August, 46 eDeliver 5’s and 83 Farizon V7E have been sold.

Ten years have gone by since the first EVC report (I have been driving electric for 7 of those). Sometimes it feels like it has gone quickly, and sometimes too slowly for those participating in the rEVolution. The first EVC report cited an AEMO (Australian Energy Market Operator) prediction that 25% of new vehicles bought by Australians would be battery electric by 2035. We have already achieved that, 10 years early! To quote Chau Le, chair of the EVC, “If this is the transformation over the last 10 years, then the next decade will be nothing short of electrifying.”

#5747. Residual Disease in Spondyloarthritis: What's Being Missed by DMARDs?

— It's common for symptoms to stick around even if inflammation is resolved by medication

In spondyloarthritis (SpA), the wealth of biologic and disease-modifying anti-rheumatic drug (DMARD) options still leaves some patients unsatisfied with their treatment outcomes, most likely due to residual disease.

This year, the flurry of drug development in SpA continued, including promising phase II-III trial data released for the selective Janus kinase (JAK) inhibitor filgotinib and the anti-interleukin (IL)-17A monoclonal antibody vunakizumab in axial SpA (axSpA).

Assessment of SpondyloArthritis International Society (ASAS) improvements of 40% or better (ASAS40) occurred in 35% to 40% of filgotinib recipients versus 18% to 21% of placebo recipients, while ASAS improvements of 20% or better (ASAS20) were reported for 65.6% of vunakizumab patients versus 42.5% of placebo patients.

The trials were deemed successful despite ASAS responses falling well short of 100%, in line with what is acceptable for the many medications already on the market for varying subsets of SpA.

"It is common to have persistent symptoms despite treatment of inflammation. Firstly, when you look at the results from successful clinical trials for most DMARDs approved for axSpA ... ASAS40 response is achieved by about 40% to 55% of patients," Abhijeet Danve, MD, MHS, of the Yale School of Medicine in New Haven, Connecticut, told MedPage Today. "That informs us that patients will have residual symptoms despite treating the inflammation adequately."

ASAS40 and ASAS20 are subjective measures of health recovery in terms of patient global assessment of the disease, spinal pain, function, and inflammation: ASAS40 is defined as a ≥40% improvement and an absolute improvement from baseline of two or more units in at least three of the four domains without any worsening in the remaining domain, and ASAS20 is defined as a ≥20% improvement and an absolute improvement from baseline of one or more units in at least three of the four domains and no major worsening in the remaining domain.

A major issue of tracking progress in SpA is the reliance on patient-reported outcomes and the lack of a good objective measure of disease activity.

"Frequently, there is contribution from the nociplastic pain," Danve noted. "Some of the spinal symptoms can be from concomitant degenerative disc disease or facet arthritis, and distinguishing between the pain from the degenerative and inflammatory components is difficult owing to lack of specific monitoring biomarkers."

Lianne Gensler, MD, of the University of California San Francisco, stressed that there will never be a physical exam that shows inflammation of axial joints of the spine and sacroiliac joints specifically. "It's a huge problem in our field because we don't have a physical exam ... that really clearly says, 'Oh, like rheumatoid arthritis, this patient has ongoing joint swelling.'"

"The closest you can come to that is an MRI," she told MedPage Today, "but that one alone is not specific" and "you can't do an MRI every time a patient has symptoms."

SpA is itself a heterogeneous family of diseases characterized by inflammation in the spine and/or the entheses, the joint-like connective tissues where tendons and ligaments insert into bones. AxSpA is one manifestation affecting the spine, commonly causing lower back pain and stiffness, and can be split into radiographic (i.e., ankylosing spondylitis [AS]) or non-radiographic subsets, while peripheral SpA, a sister condition, can cause limb discomfort such as heel pain and plantar fasciitis.

Generally, pharmacological treatment of these sometimes-overlapping SpA conditions means initial therapy with nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs, and in some cases glucocorticoids, before the patient resorts to the various DMARDs on the market. In a 2026 guideline update, the American College of Rheumatology endorsed tumor necrosis factor inhibitors and IL-17 blockers preferentially over JAK inhibitors for initial biologic DMARD treatment of AS and axSpA.

But what happens when patients still complain of residual symptoms after DMARD therapy?

"Many physicians who are treating patients don't recognize that these symptoms are not necessarily driven by objective inflammation, and there's a trigger response to change the treatment because a patient has residual symptoms. That's a huge problem," Gensler said.

"There's certainly some patients who can be treatment refractory, but most patients that have ongoing symptoms actually [have] other factors, whether it's damage, nociplastic pain, or comorbidities driving symptoms," such as depression and obesity, she noted.

In these cases of residual symptoms, Danve suggested physical therapy and regular exercise, as well as use of medications such as gabapentin, selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors, and muscle relaxants to help the nociplastic pain.

"Having right expectations is important to avoid rapid cycling through DMARDs," he said.

With residual disease being a major concern to researchers, several groups have recently tried to establish various terms to describe persistent symptoms and inadequate response to targeted therapies.

In 2025, ASAS released its consensus-based definition of difficult-to-manage (D2M) axial SpA: failure of at least two biologic or targeted synthetic DMARDs, suboptimal disease control, and the perception of problematic signs and symptoms by both rheumatologists and patients. Treatment-refractory disease is considered a subset within D2M that requires a history of treatment failure, the presence of objective signs of inflammatory activity, and the exclusion of non-inflammatory reasons for nonresponse.

Supporting the idea that many D2M symptoms are not coming from inflammation, Gensler cited her own group's research and estimated that treatment-refractory patients, who do have objective signs of inflammation, make up just 10% of the D2M pool.

She urged rheumatologists to "take a step back and say, 'Do I believe that the ongoing symptoms of disease activity are coming from inflammation?'"

They need to be "holistic" about assessing all the things that can drive symptoms and addressing them separately, she said, or getting patients to a clinician who will address them.

#5746. HomePower 1000 Plus v2: Portable Power Put To Use

I have been using the Jackery HomePower 1000 Plus v2 in a wide range of applications over the past few months around my apartment in Queens, NY. The flexibility and portability make it especially useful for people living in apartments. I had previously written about the Jackery HomePower 1000 Plus v2 when it launched. You can read more about the technical specifications in that article.

Overall, the battery charged in about an hour. It starts charging at a little over 1000 W and then drops to a little over 800 watts as it charges. The first thing I plugged into the battery was a fan, which used about 50 watts. Then I plugged in my laptop, which charged at another 50 watts. I was able to work comfortably for a day off the battery with capacity to spare.

Keeping on the cool side, the battery was easily able to run my 12,000 BTU window air conditioner on a warmer day. Being in a century-old pre-war building, insulation could leave something to be desired. In addition, my corner apartment with large windows and 10-foot ceilings contributes to the load. Air conditioners can be a challenge to power, as the power consumption tends to spike when the compressor kicks in, but it did not pose a challenge to the battery. On a day in the mid-80’s (Fahrenheit), the battery was able to keep the indoor temperature in the mid-70’s all day with capacity to spare. Also, as my wife likes plugging her 1750 W clothes iron into the same circuit as the air conditioner, adding the battery could reduce the potential to trip the breaker when the compressor kicks in. It would be useful to maintain comfort during a power outage.

In terms of keeping things cool, the battery tends to use a little over 3% of its capacity per hour on my high-efficiency 14.1 cubic foot Midea refrigerator during the day with the door opening periodically. With the door staying shut, it would be closer to 2% per hour. That could potentially keep food from spoiling for a couple of days during an outage. While Jackery makes the FridgeGuard specifically for this application, the Jackery HomePower 1000 Plus v2 could come in handy for added capacity during an emergency.

The battery would also come in handy for people with fish tanks to keep the pumps running during a power outage. I have an aquaponics setup with a few small fish to grow herbs and start seeds in the Spring. The battery can easily run the filters, pumps and heaters, as well as the powerful lights for the plants. However, for someone with exotic fish in a saltwater aquarium, battery backup could be essential to keeping the fish alive during a power disruption.

In addition, I was able to run my Vitamix blender with the HomePower 1000 Plus v2. The powerful motor can draw over 1000 W, but it doesn’t take long to blend up a smoothie or frozen beverage. As such, you could easily make frozen margaritas at a backyard party using the battery to power the blender.

In terms of cooking, the battery could easily handle my rice cooker and Instapot pressure cooker for extended cooking times. I was able to cook an 8 lb. pork roast in the pressure cooker. It also had enough power to run my air fryer and microwave. However, with the air fryer drawing as much as 1700 W, it depletes the capacity quickly, even though the load is within the 1800 W continuous rating of the battery. A kettle or coffee maker does not pose much of a challenge. You could also potentially run an electric grill, although I did not have the opportunity to try it yet. This could make a cookout more convenient, in addition to reducing risks associated with fire-based grills in dry areas.

After all that, I needed to clean up. I was able to run my dishwasher off the battery. Heating water is clearly one area that consumes a lot of energy, but I was able to run the dishwasher for a full cycle with room to spare. I was also able to run my portable washing machine off the battery. While the loads were not massive, the voltage fluctuates significantly while it is running. It did not create an issue for the battery. Of note, these are efficient NYC apartment-sized appliances. Many Europeans use similar-sized appliances, but middle-American McMansions tend to use more power-hungry models. Your mileage may vary.

Shifting to a work environment. I was easily able to charge my laptop, run the router, charge my phone, etc., with capacity to spare. You could easily keep an office running during a power outage. Or set up a remote office for the day, returning to charge at night.

I also used the battery to run my Bambu Labs X1-C 3D printer. Overall, the load is small enough to produce large prints that last hours using just the battery for power. However, when plugged in, the battery can ensure consistent power through multiple prints. I used a power strip to turn on and off the power to the battery on several occasions. There was not a flicker or even the slightest variance in the print layers. If you have ever had a power disruption during a multi-hour complicated print, the benefit is clear. Moving forward, I wouldn’t use the printer on a large print without the battery. In addition to serving as a high-capacity UPS, I was also able to time shift the electricity consumption of the printer.

Using the Jackery App, I was able to easily set up the battery to use a TOU utility plan. By entering the times and rates, it can even calculate the savings. In addition, you can program the battery to retain a set amount of capacity for backup, seamlessly switching to input power when that capacity level is reached.

For Con Edison small business TOU rates, peak electricity supply from 8 AM to 10 PM is $0.5443/kWh from June 1 to September 30 and $0.2680/kWh all other months. Off peak is just $0.0199. Of note, this does not include the separate service charges, delivery charges and taxes, which on my last utility bill worked out to be $0.3378/ kWh on top of the electricity supply charges. Electricity is expensive in NYC. However, many small businesses rent and do not have access to the panel to take advantage of the potential TOU savings from a larger battery at that location. Smaller batteries can be implemented at point of use, such as a workstation. As such, a battery like a HomePower 1000 Plus v2 at each workstation could save roughly $0.50 per day in the summer and $0.25 in the winter. Payback on the investment could come in a few years. If a business changed locations, they could easily take the batteries with them. However, the ability to provide UPS battery backup could also easily pay for itself quickly if work could continue uninterrupted, work in progress could be saved and sensitive equipment could be protected.

To take it a step further, the HomePower 1000 Plus v2 can directly take DC solar input. This does not feed back into the grid, but it could provide power for different uses. This could be useful when camping or even with a more permanent solar setup to power an appliance, workshop or small office. While it would not replace the capability of balcony solar, the ability to run appliances off small-scale solar that does not feed into the grid could provide some of the benefits while the balcony solar regulations are being figured out.

Overall, I found the battery to be more useful than I originally anticipated. I tended to look at the power rating of different devices and assumed that they would pull that for the entire duration and drain the battery quickly. However, many cycle on and off, making 1024 Wh of battery go much farther than I would have thought. In addition, Jackery’s power and capacity ratings seem to be conservative, letting me use it right up to the rated limits without issues. And the portability lets me conveniently try it in a wide range of applications. Living in an apartment with efficient appliances, it also fits many of my potential use cases. And it gives people living in apartments who do not have the ability to modify the panel many of the benefits of battery storage. However, if the HomePower 1000 Plus V2 turns out to be  not enough for your needs, Jackery also offers expansion batteries to increase the capacity, as well as offering larger capacity models. While travel conflicts did not give me the chance to try it out for camping yet, I hope to use it outdoors soon.

Of course, Jackery also offers much larger batteries. And they have some even smaller batteries coming. But that is for another article.

Jackery supplied the HomePower 1000 Plus v2 for review, but did not pay for the review. The thoughts here are my own.

#5745. Can One Tire Really Do It All? 8 All-Weather Tires Compete To Find Out

Generally speaking, no one can have it all, and no one thing can do it all. That's just not how life works. And for the longest time, that was true of tires. You basically had to pick summer performance, winter performance, or disappointing so-called "all-seasons." Like with engines, though, tire technology advances, and the latest crop of all-weather tires claims to pretty much be able to do it all. But can a single tire really handle every single situation well? Our friends at Tyre Reviews compared eight of them to find out.

The newest tire in the comparison is the Continental SecureContact AW, and when it comes to premium tires, a lot of times the newest one is the best. But is that the case here? Well, Tyre Reviews put it up against Michelin's legendary CrossClimate2, the Goodyear Assurance WeatherReady 2, Bridgestone's WeatherPeak, the Pirelli Cinturato WeatherActive, the Hankook Weatherflex GT, the Falken Aklimate, and the Atturo AZ810. And while this is supposedly a Continental-focused comparison, those of you who keep a sharp eye on Jalopnik's tire coverage will recognize that this is also an important comparison test for the much-improved Bridgestone WeatherPeak.

Tyre Reviews is a YouTube channel that tests tires, then scores them in a variety of categories, weights each category based on how important it is for the comparison in question, and provides some of the best unbiased tire information on the internet. The U.S. doesn't always get every single tire Tyre Reviews tests, but we definitely get the Continental, as well as the Bridgestone. So how did they do?

In the snow handling test, well, I have some bad news for Continental and Bridgestone fans. The Michelin won. That said, the Continental came in second, and the Bridgestone placed fourth. But there's more to winter performance than snow handling, and the Continental edged out the Michelin in snow traction. There's also more to all-weather tires than winter performance, and Tyre Reviews' tests include handling, braking, and performance tests in wet conditions, as well as dry, and they measure factors like noise and rolling resistance, too.

Unfortunately for the Bridgestone, it just didn't have what it took to win this particular comparison. Or beat any of the other tires in the test. Oh, well. As for the Continental, it actually tied with the Michelin ... in first place. Each tire arrived at its first-place score differently, though, so if you want to see which all-weather tire is best for you, you're going to have to watch the video.

#5744. The Share of Solar Generation with Co-located Battery Storage Is Increasing

Utility-scale solar developers are increasingly co-locating battery storage with new solar photovoltaic capacity. As of September 2026, over 16% of new utility-scale solar capacity added this year is co-located with operable battery storage, compared with 2% in 2017, according to data reported to EIA by operators.

Reported plans for solar additions scheduled to come online in the last three months of this year show that the share with co-located battery storage could increase to 19% this year. Longer-term plans indicate that this trend will continue, with the portion increasing to more than 30% in plans submitted for 2027 and 2028.

When operators co-locate battery storage with solar, they can direct solar electricity output to the grid or to the battery, depending on the wholesale prices. Wholesale prices in regions with large solar participation tend to fall when solar contributions are high and rise as they decrease. Plants with both solar and battery storage can manage the combined output of their generating resources to maximize their value in a changing wholesale price environment.

We publish unit-level generator inventory data based on our annual and monthly data collections, including information on design attributes for all utility-scale plants. Although battery storage and solar units at the same plant often enter commercial service at different times, the EIA-860 and EIA-860M data collection program tracks these developments and provides a monthly summary that can be used to identify co-located technologies.

Southwest states have higher-than-average solar capacity shares relative to their total generating capacity and also have higher levels of co-located utility-scale solar and battery capacity. As of September, 13% of the total U.S. utility-scale generating capacity is solar photovoltaic, and 18% of that is co-located with battery storage. The share of solar generation co-located with battery capacity in California, Arizona, New Mexico, and Nevada is more than double the national average.

Principal contributors: Glenn McGrath, Alex Mey

The EIA collects, analyzes, and disseminates independent and impartial energy information to promote sound policymaking, efficient markets, and public understanding of energy and its interaction with the economy and the environment.

#5743. 7 Luxury Vehicles From Throughout History That Used 4-Cylinder Engines

Luxury cars can differ massively from one another, but most of us make similar assumptions when describing the term "luxury car." We picture leather seats, 20-plus-inch chrome wheels, full-size silhouettes, and big displacement engines with a lot of cylinders. However, every once in a while, automakers mix things up a little and throw in an ingredient you might not expect. Four-cylinder engines are one such ingredient, and they found themselves in luxury cars from an assortment of automakers, spanning across what is almost the entire history of the automobile so far.

We're talking Fiats from the turn of the 20th century, British executive sedans produced by Rover in the late '60s and early '70s, hybridized BMW 5 Series sedans, Range Rover SUVs, and more. What's interesting here is that, for the most part, all of these cars were and are considered successes, with rare exceptions (Facel Vega Facellia, we're looking at you), which suggests luxury car buyers aren't actually all that hung up on the engine under the hood. Without further ado, here are our seven four-cylinder luxury cars, in chronological order.

Today, Fiat busies itself with budget-oriented and retro-themed compacts mainly, such as the seemingly evergreen 500, plus its new and immensely cute Panda Grande. Wind the clock back just over 120 years to 1905, though, and Fiat was a very different automaker. It was at this point that it debuted its 60 HP model to rival equivalent models from Mercedes. This thunderously powerful car was powered by a 647-cubic-inch inline-4 with dual-chain drive and a four-speed manual transmission. A 60-horsepower inline-4 might not sound like much at all by today's standards, but in vintage car circles, this Fiat is sometimes considered to be the original supercar.

The Fiat 60 HP had a split personality, designed to be capable both as a road-going vehicle and indeed as a racer, with removable wings and advanced cooling systems. It was also mightily expensive, commanding a $13,500 price tag in 1905, while the sole surviving example also wears a $4,000 coachbuilt touring body.

The craftsmen responsible for the body used only the finest materials available, including aluminum panels, silver solder to cover and hide fastening points, and brass mouldings to disguise the seams of the wooden framework. As for the interior, it was about as luxurious as one could expect for a car of this era, with leather seats and mohair floor mats. Remember, just owning a car in the early 20th century was a mind-boggling luxury, let alone one of this caliber.

There is much that could be said about the Facel Vega Facellia that is wholly negative, but for a car so imposing and bold in design, that seems unfair, so we shall begin with its stronger points. Facel Vega knew how to do style and opulence quite well, and although the Facellia was its entry-level model, this experience is still luxurious. It was poised as an excellent alternative to Mercedes' SL series, or indeed an equally charming Alfa Romeo of the same era. Inside, the expected amenities are all there in the form of leather seats and numerous gauges set into a hand-painted woodgrain-effect dashboard. On the outside, there is plenty of brightwork to enjoy, and the Facellia shares much in common stylistically with its bigger brother, the HK500.

However, what it sadly doesn't share with the HK500 is its hulking great Chrysler V8. Instead, the Facellia sported a notoriously unreliable French unit – a 115-hp 1.6-liter inline-4. It suffered from overheating issues, fragile internals, and troublesome chromed cylinder liners, which resulted in the majority of cars experiencing engine failure within the first six months of ownership. Facel Vega had no choice but to replace these engines free of charge, but those second engines didn't prove much better either. A final resolution came in the form of a genuinely bulletproof Volvo lump, but by this point, the firm's reputation was in the gutter, and the Facellia would ultimately prove a hurdle too high for the automaker.

This is a far more conventional offering than the other two covered so far. It replaced the aging P4 model in 1963, and production continued until 1977, so it was clearly a popular choice. With it, Rover successfully filled a notable gap in the UK's car market, where simple commuter cars generally sat at or below 1.5 liters in displacement, while large luxury models sported 3.0 liters or more. With its 2.0-liter inline-4, the P6 could jump between the two segments, offering a premium executive model that cost less than the larger luxury products.

Numerous engine options were offered throughout the model's life, including a twin-carb variant of the 2000, a 3.5-liter V8, and a 2.2-liter version of the 2000, which never made it onto U.S. shores as it arrived after Rover had withdrawn from the U.S. market in '71.

It might not have taken off well in the U.S., but with 322,302 examples sold throughout its life, the P6 was certainly anything but a failure in other markets. One key to its success was sublime handling, which was on par with sports cars of the era and at its best in the four-pot models, alongside a well-equipped cabin. Inside, plush leather seats provide impressive comfort, the steering wheel is adjustable, and faux-wood trim completes the look.

The 5 Series might not be the most opulent sedan BMW makes -– that accolade goes to the 7 Series, which also sported an inline-4 in certain global markets — but it's absolutely still a luxury car from a premium brand. Historically, the 5 Series usually sported a mix of six- and eight-cylinder engines, although a smattering of four-pots have also made their way under its hood. In fact, the first 5 Series (E12) debuted with an inline-4 way back in 1972, offering 115 and 130 hp. The U.S. never received the four-cylinder E12, though, and it would take several generations before a so-powered 5 Series found its way onto American shores.

For the 2012 model year, the F10 5 Series' naturally aspirated 3.0-liter inline-6 was replaced with a turbocharged 2.0-liter inline-4, which delivered 240 hp – identical to that of the inline-6 it replaced. However, real-world performance highlights that the smaller lump is the better performer thanks to additional turbocharged torque, earning it both a 0 to 60 mph and quarter-mile time roughly half a second quicker than the six-cylinder-powered variant.

Since then, four-cylinder engines have been a staple member of the 5 Series lineup, with such engines appearing in subsequent G30 and G60 generations, albeit alongside the usual smattering of larger mills. Those latter two generations use the B48 engine, a fuel-sipping four-cylinder that yields solid mpg, and options like these are only becoming more common in modern luxury cars.

The Velar arrived for the 2018 model year as a compact luxury crossover with a sleeker silhouette than the firm's usual offerings. Right from the get-go, it was offered with a fairly gutless 247-hp turbocharged 2.0-liter inline-4, and that option stayed, following on from a mild makeover for the 2024 model year Velar. It was also offered with a 180-hp turbodiesel inline-4, although that choice had fizzled out by 2020.

Regardless of the somewhat lackluster base engine options, the Velar is still every bit a luxury vehicle. Ever since a 2021 update, the model has boasted clever tech, such as active noise-cancellation technology and an enhanced air filtration system, plus all the typical goodies one might expect to find within a premium SUV. Even on the base models, heated and perforated leather seats are standard, as is a panoramic sunroof, while higher trim levels introduce larger 21-inch wheels, a Meridian sound system, plus cooled seats.

While opting for a 247-hp Range Rover might seem a little uninspiring, not every buyer is going to be chasing a higher-output engine in such a vehicle. Some will be happy to take the very nearly $10,000 discount in choosing a P250 instead of a six-cylinder P400, perhaps if it's to be predominantly used in cities or on school and grocery runs rather than highway commutes.

The four-cylinder C63 wasn't exactly a monumental excess, proving that downsizing can go too far, and upsetting purists and AMG enthusiasts by swapping out the thunderous V8s on which Mercedes' tuning arm built its reputation. As such, Mercedes-AMG is now killing the four-cylinder C63 in favor of an inline-6 C53, but on paper, the old four-cylinder car still got so much right.

Taking care of motivation is a boosted 2.0-liter inline-4, which works alongside an electric motor to produce a combined 671 hp — enough grunt to see it take the claim as the quickest production C-Class ever built. According to Car and Driver's testing, it can reach 60 mph in 2.9 seconds, 100 mph in 7.1 seconds, and cover the quarter-mile in just 11.2 seconds on its way to a 174-mph top speed. It does all this while still not ignoring the usual smattering of luxurious extras and options the tri-pointed-star brand is famed for.

Heated and optionally ventilated nappa leather seats accompany carbon-fiber trim, while a 15-speaker Burmester sound system is the standard affair. Buyers enjoy a panoramic sunroof as standard, along with wireless Apple CarPlay and Android Auto connectivity options. There's very little the C63 doesn't boast, in honesty, but that's surely what any buyer of a near-$90,000 C-Class should expect.

Lexus is the luxury arm of Toyota, and its models all boast plenty of premium features while also packing famous Toyota reliability — a trait often misplaced by its German competitors. The two brands share much with one another, including platforms and powertrains, and the 2027 NX 350h is no exception. The crossover shares its platform with the Toyota RAV4, and is offered in both front- and all-wheel drive. The former sports a naturally aspirated 2.5-liter inline-4 in addition to a pair of electric motors, while the latter boasts a trio of motors. In both instances, the combined output is a healthy 244 horsepower, and that oomph is channeled toward the wheels via a CVT.

It's no performance car, nor is it trying to be one, but with 0 to 60 mph times below seven seconds and a top speed of 124 mph, it's certainly peppy enough for most applications. Those with a hankering for more could always opt for the plug-in hybrid variant, which too boasts an inline-4 and a number of electric motors, but with a combined output of 324 hp. Details such as perforated leather seating, open-pore wood trim, 20-inch alloy wheels, and ambient lighting ensure the NX delivers a premium experience and help to differentiate it from its humbler Toyota roots. The NX Hybrid says the quiet luxury part out loud, but in our testing, it proved to be a genuinely enjoyable and comfortable luxury companion — inline-4 included.

#5742. Toyota & Waymo Advance Autonomous Driving Collaboration for Safer Streets and Freedom of Movement for All

Toyota Motor Corporation (“Toyota”) and Waymo today announced the next phase of their autonomous driving collaboration, in pursuit of a world with safer streets and freedom of movement for all.

Building on the preliminary agreement announced in April 2025, Toyota and Waymo will begin initial operations to test and validate an autonomous Toyota bZ4X fleet in Japan in 2027, with deployment to the United Kingdom and other Waymo-serviced markets planned for the following years. This partnership marks a major milestone in the expansion of Japan’s first fully autonomous ride-hailing service using high-volume, domestically manufactured vehicles.

Toyota will build the vehicles at its Takaoka plant in Toyota City, Aichi Prefecture, Japan. The entire process will take place under one roof, from assembling the base vehicle to integrating Waymo’s AV hardware.

By pairing Toyota’s global manufacturing and vehicle development expertise with Waymo’s fully autonomous technology, the companies will accelerate scaling safety-validated Level 4 autonomous vehicles globally — helping provide people with transit that reduces human-error accidents, and opening up freedom of movement for those who can’t easily get around.

Beyond safety, the custom-outfitted bZ4X is designed for comfort and greater accessibility. Generous rear legroom and features such as automatic doors, a power liftgate, and ambient lighting will provide seamless entry and exit for riders.

Toyota and Waymo will continue to explore the future of autonomous driving technology for personally owned Toyota vehicles. Alongside Woven by Toyota, they will also explore additional initiatives that support their shared goal of fewer road safety incidents, including how insights from Waymo’s real-world operations, data-driven intelligence, and technology could holistically improve safety for other road users.

“Toyota’s commitment to zero traffic accidents resonates deeply with us at Waymo, and we’re proud to work together toward safer roads for all,” said Dmitri Dolgov, co-CEO of Waymo. “In areas where Waymo operates, we’ve demonstrated that we are up to 20x safer than human drivers when it comes to serious crash injuries, and our partnership with Toyota will help us bring the safety and convenience of autonomous driving to more people around the world, including key international markets like Japan. We chose Toyota because of our respect for the legendary safety, reliability, and quality of their vehicles, and we look forward to deepening our collaboration as we scale our fully autonomous Waymo Driver technology globally.”

“We are deeply grateful that Waymo, which helped pioneer fully autonomous driving technology, has chosen Toyota as its partner,” said Hiroki Nakajima, member of the board of directors and executive vice president of Toyota. “Safety technology makes the biggest difference when it reaches as many people as possible. This collaboration will help scale that technology and bring it to customers in Japan and beyond through Waymo’s ride-hailing service, and in parallel, Toyota will continue to explore how integrating vehicles with traffic infrastructure can make those roads safer still. No single company can achieve the goal of zero traffic accidents alone, but with partners like Waymo, we will get there one step at a time.”

#5741. How Did History's Deadliest Pathogen Infect a Private High School?

— Anatomy of a tuberculosis outbreak in the Bay Area

We heard about the outbreak by accident. In December 2025, I (Meyer) was chatting with a friend who has a teenager in a rival San Francisco high school. Our conversation turned to football first, then to prom.

"It's going great," the friend reported. "Well, except for the tuberculosis."

Tuberculosis (TB), caused by the slender, rod-shaped Mycobacterium tuberculosis, is one of humanity's great scourges. It's ancient, contagious, highly lethal, and thrives in impoverished settings. A well-funded private school is just about the last place we would expect to find a TB outbreak.

But my friend was right. About a month earlier, the San Francisco Department of Public Health (SFDPH) issued a report describing a case of TB at Archbishop Riordan High School (ARHS). By March, that one case would balloon into hundreds more.

As a physician (Meyer) and a journalist (Hahm), we spent months wondering how history's deadliest infection overwhelmed a prestigious Bay Area school. On the outbreak's 1-year anniversary, we sat down with several parents and a student to find out.

The story of TB is one of the U.S.'s great triumphs. Until the 1800s, tuberculosis was the single greatest cause of American mortality, responsible for a quarter of all deaths. But by the early 1900s, public health initiatives that improved living and work conditions precipitated a drop in TB. After streptomycin was developed in the 1940s, case rates plunged. Between the 1950s and 1980s, annual TB mortality dropped from 20,000 to under 2,000. However, TB remains a leading cause of death globally due to overcrowding, malnutrition, limited healthcare, and, more recently, cuts to foreign aid.

Today, 77% of cases in the U.S. occur in foreign-born individuals, in whom TB incidence is 20 times greater than in U.S.-born individuals. In San Francisco, TB epidemiology mirrors national trends.

This, according to the parents we interviewed, is how the ARHS outbreak began -- with a student who had recently immigrated from a continent with high TB prevalence (Africa).

In September 2025, ARHS's index case of TB (patient zero) was evaluated by a medical provider, reporting 2 weeks of cough. As you might imagine, a cough is a fairly common complaint amongst high school students during flu season. The student's symptoms were not attributed to TB at that initial visit.

TB is a slow burning disease. Infection occurs when someone inhales its bacilli and, in about 5% of infected individuals, this results in the development of active TB with classic consumptive symptoms (cough, bloody sputum, night sweats). But most people don't develop symptoms immediately. Their immune system halts the infection and they remain both asymptomatic and non-contagious -- a state known as latent TB. But there's always a risk those dormant bacilli will reactivate into active TB -- about a 10% risk over a person's lifetime. In the U.S., 80% of active TB cases are due to reactivation, and among foreign-born individuals, 93% are. The good news is there's treatment for latent TB, and it's 90% effective at preventing progression to active disease.

However, there are still an estimated 13 million people with latent TB in the U.S. who, left untreated, represent a reservoir for ongoing transmission -- a sort of tuberculous tinderbox. And treatment of latent TB often falls through the cracks of the American health system: in one study, only half (48%) of foreign-born adults who tested positive for latent TB were offered treatment; in another, only 47% of patients who started treatment completed it.

In addition, in 2021, for the first time in 30 years, TB began quietly staging a comeback. Cuts to public health infrastructure, pandemic-related healthcare disruptions, and increased immigration all contributed to a recent and ongoing TB resurgence in the U.S.

Once someone has active TB, that person is contagious, but only moderately so -- in developed countries the R0 (the number of people typically infected by an individual with active TB) is generally well below 1. But transmission can increase -- a lot -- if there's extended, indoor contact between the infected and uninfected. Especially during the winter.

ARHS offers a boarding option, which is convenient for international students. This, according to the parents we interviewed, was where patient zero was living between the onset of cough in September and November -- when the student was finally referred to SFDPH for evaluation of active TB.

SFDPH began by screening patient zero's close contacts. This revealed an ominously high prevalence of TB infection. In December, mandatory testing of all ARHS students and staff was announced. Two additional cases of active and 50 cases of latent TB were confirmed in January. By late February, over 1,300 TB tests had been administered. And by March, a startling 18% of those tested -- 244 students and staff -- were positive for latent TB infection, in addition to eight cases of active TB.

All told, the fallout was massive: extensive, repeated, months-long testing; medical treatment for hundreds of students and staff; widespread school disruption, including cancellation of in-person classes; and considerable frustration and confusion amongst students and parents, who largely shouldered the cost of all that testing. By April, 96% of the latent and 100% of the active cases had started treatment, and the outbreak was considered contained.

The goal of any outbreak investigation is, first and foremost, to stop the outbreak. And then to learn from it. It's tempting to view the ARHS outbreak as an isolated anomaly -- but this would be learning the wrong lesson.

In April, the CDC published a review of 50 large TB outbreaks in the U.S. About a quarter were associated with congregate living settings, such as correctional facilities. In the remaining three quarters, the outbreak spread primarily via social networks. In all of them, 80% of cases occurred in U.S.-born individuals. The study is a bleak reminder that no one is completely safe from TB.

Could the San Francisco outbreak have been prevented? It's not clear. We don't know if patient zero was offered, initiated, or completed treatment for latent TB. We do know the initial diagnosis was missed and the student spent months with active TB in a congregate living setting before starting treatment. From there, the high-density, highly social environment of high school appears to have been perfect fuel for transmission.

Perhaps the most important takeaway is that tuberculosis is a contemporary threat, not a historical relic. In an era of rising TB rates, the ARHS outbreak is a warning. Its breathtaking escalation from a single case to hundreds of infections in just a few months underscores why the great white plague was once the most feared disease in the world. It's a reminder against complacency.

Tuberculosis was a death sentence just a century ago -- it would be arrogant to assume we've conquered it.

This perspective is the authors' alone and does not necessarily reflect that of any institutions or companies with which they are affiliated.

#5740. These 4 Gas Discounts Can Fill The Void Kroger Left In Your Heart

Saving money on gas and groceries is an everyday struggle, but if you're lucky enough to get both at the same location, hurrah! One less place on that list, though, is Kroger, whose store-branded credit card lost its automatic discounts at the pump. This stemmed from Kroger and U.S. Bank parting ways.

To quote an old proverb: "When elephants fight, it is the grass that suffers." In this case, the stomped-on grass is credit card holders. As for the elephants, U.S. Bank, the now-former issuer of its Kroger Rewards World Elite Mastercard, switched to a cash-back Visa, and Kroger is now bedfellows with First Bank & Trust, yet confusingly did not file a post-divorce name change for its rewards credit card.

Cardholders of the U.S. Bank version previously received 2% cash back when shopping at Kroger, 1% elsewhere, and 5% on their first $3,000 spent through mobile wallet purchases. The biggest prize, however, was the automatic 5-cent-per-gallon discount at Kroger gas stations. First-year cardholders, and those who spent $6,000 a year on the card, got 25 cents off instead when redeeming fuel points. But poof, that's gone now. No more real-time card discounts. Granted, the 5 cents off was a credit card-only bonus, and although Kroger's store program also changed, fuel discounts at Kroger aren't completely off the table. You just have to be a bit more clever to earn them.

Because U.S. Bank-Kroger Mastercard holders need to apply for the new First Bank & Trust-Kroger ones, they risk carrying two credit cards. But there are other ways to save money on gas so you don't need either one.

The Costco Anywhere Visa by Citi will snag you 5% cash back at Costco-branded gas stations, and a respectable 4% back at other eligible fuel centers and EV chargers. But once you spend $7,000 in combined fill-ups in a year, the returns diminish to only 1%. The Sam's Club Mastercard is more flexible with 5% cash back on fuel and EV charging purchases, regardless of brand. But its threshold maxes out at a lower $6,000 per year. Then, like Costco, the discount is 1%.

Walmart has a OnePay CashRewards card that pays Walmart+ members 5% back at Walmart. Walmart+ membership itself gets you 10 cents off a gallon at participating store gas stations and partner brands like CITGO and Exxon, plus the wholesaler's member gas price at Sam's Club. Non-Walmart+ cardholders don't receive an at-the-pump discount but still get 3% cash back at Walmart, including its gas stations. Go elsewhere and the discount is 1.5%. Target fans get fuel savings, too. Despite Target not having gas stations or fuel partners, the Target Circle Mastercard still offers 2% cash back on all gas purchases.

Regional supercenter chain Meijer also has branded gas stations as well as a store credit card and co-branded Mastercard. Cardholders of either don't receive a flat rate cash-back percentage. Instead, you get $10 back every $750 spent, which is a middling 1%, but do snag a 10-cent-per-gallon Meijer-only gas discount. Some of Albertsons Companies' regional chains, like Albertsons and Safeway, have branded gas stations alongside their grocery stores. But all Albertsons Cos. stores offer a co-branded American Express card with an everyday 3% gas cash back, no matter where you fuel up.

Want to cut ties with Kroger accounts altogether but not lose out on cheaper fuel prices there? Simply use another cash-back credit card, one that you probably already have in your wallet.

Banks, from nationwide behemoths to regional chains, offer cash-back credit cards, and with no annual fee to boot. Bank of America has a few, including one with a sign-up bonus of 6% cash back in the first year, up to $2,500 per quarter. This applies to one category of your choice, such as gas and EV charging. Wells Fargo's deal is 2% back but with no categories to monitor.

And U.S. Bank's Smartly Visa, which replaced the grocer's branded card, offers a 2% return on all purchases, but the cash-back percentage rises with the balance you keep in deposit accounts, up to 4% on qualified balances of $100,000 or more. With that, you can probably tap to pay for a new Toyota 4Runner, but your credit score and interest rate will be a separate matter.

Even non-cash back credit cards offer gas savings, too. Banks and retailers are polyamorous. They both want to be involved in your spending, including how and where you buy. So, check your credit and debit cards for any incentives or bonus offers. They usually need to be formally activated, but chances are you'll find handsome rewards for fueling up at specific brands and locations, including Kroger.

Don't want more credit cards or to sign up for yet another customer loyalty program? There's an app for that! GasBuddy is a car-related app you can't live without. It offers plenty of money-saving tools online like gas price heat maps, but its app is where the sweet savings are. Its integrated GasBack marketplace lets you buy everything from apparel to parking, and qualified purchases earn either a lump sum credit or up to 10% back. When your balance reaches $5, you can cash out or exchange your earnings for gift cards. Wanna save more, like up to 33 cents off a gallon? You'll have to sign up for the Pay with GasBuddy+ card.

However, never mind its Mastercard logo; the Pay with GasBuddy+ card is not a credit card. Instead, it's connected to your bank account with payments made via ACH. The card includes a 3- or 5-cent-per-gallon discount, depending on the station, and adds up to 25 cents off through bonuses. Plus, every $25 spent on non-fuel purchases in convenience stores will net an additional 3-cent discount. When stacked, the per-gallon savings tops out at 33 cents.

Other popular shopping apps that offer money back for fuel stops are Checkout 51, Fetch Rewards, and Upside. Checkout 51 requires users to upload receipts, which earn cash back that can be cashed out via third-party payment methods like PayPal. Fetch also uses a receipt-based system but redemptions are only via gift cards.

Less complicated is Upside. No receipts needed. Just select a discount offer in the app, make a purchase, and the cash back is deposited into your account. Your earnings don't expire as long as your account stays active.

Of course, the gas brands themselves offer fuel discounts, too. Sure, we'd like Big Oil to just set affordable prices no matter what, but sometimes gas costs have more to do with what the feds are doing than the fuel companies.

Anyway, while cash back offers are nice, you still have to wait for the reward. More satisfying is paying a lower price from the get-go. And pretty much every gas brand has a loyalty program, from bigwigs like Chevron Texaco, Shell, and ExxonMobil to road trip familiars Love's Travel Stop and Pilot Flying J.

Chevron members earn 5 points per gallon toward future discounts, while Exxon rewards by fuel type. Premium earns 6 cents a gallon in points while all other gas octanes and diesel net only 3 cents. Shell has a tiered system as well but it's based on fill-ups, with Platinum members receiving 10 cents off per gallon.

Popular convenience stores that also happen to have gas stations, such as 7-Eleven, Circle K, and Casey's, offer savings as well. Along with a free Slurpee every July 11, 7-Eleven gives members a minimum 5-cent-per-gallon discount. There are special promotions, too. Circle K has similar bonus days that cut the per-gallon price by up to 40 cents. Keep in mind that despite the freebies and fuel, these famous chains don't always sell additive-rich Top Tier-rated fuel, which is said to be cleaner gas for your engine.

If you'd rather stay loyal to Kroger, here's some good news: shortly before parting ways with U.S. Bank, Kroger updated its Fuel Points perks. The loyalty program is now simply called Points, reflecting the plan's flexibility to reward customers with more than just cheaper fuel. With a Kroger Points account, every $1 spent earns one point, but qualifying purchases and special bonus events can double or quadruple that earnings rate. Additionally, Kroger Boost or Boost Essential members can earn twice the points on most purchases all the time.

A minimum of 100 points is required for redemption, but values differ between store buys and gas purchases. For fuel, 100 points converts to 10 cents off per gallon, with a maximum discount of $1 per gallon. For food, that same 100 points will save you $1 at checkout in-store or online, up to $10 per day.

Kroger Points can be redeemed at any of the grocer's 1,232 stores located in 16 states, and then some. Parent company, The Kroger Co., operates more than 2,700 stores nationwide, including regional grocery chains like Ralphs, Smith's, Harris Teeter, and Pick 'n Save, to name a few. Among its brands, The Kroger Co. manages more than 1,700 gas stations. Fuel partners, such as Shell, will also accept redemption of Kroger Points. Yet, not all Shell locations participate in Kroger's program, and partnering fuel brands do vary by region. Check the app or website's store locator feature first, which can be filtered to show fuel-only locations.

All things considered, what Kroger taketh away, you can easily get back. Stick with Points, bring your own savings hack to Kroger gas stations, or simply fuel up somewhere else.

#5739. Hyundai IONIQ 3 Named European Car of the Year 2027 Finalist

SEOUL/OFFENBACH — Hyundai Motor’s all-electric IONIQ 3 has been named one of seven finalists for the European Car of the Year 2027 award. Selected by an independent jury of automotive journalists from across Europe, the nomination places IONIQ 3 among the contenders for one of Europe’s most established automotive awards. The winner will be announced on 8 January 2027 at the Brussels Motor Show.

Designed in Europe and produced at Hyundai Motor Türkiye’s İzmit plant, IONIQ 3 has been developed specifically for European customers. The fully electric compact hatchback combines Hyundai’s distinctive ‘Art of Steel’ design language, a spacious interior and intuitive technology with a driving range of up to 497 km (WLTP) in the Long Range version. With a starting price below €30,000 across European markets, IONIQ 3 aims to make electric mobility accessible to a broader range of customers.

“Being named a finalist for European Car of the Year is important recognition for IONIQ 3 and for the work that has gone into its development. We set out to create an electric car designed around the needs of European customers, combining distinctive design with class-leading aerodynamics, strong driving range and exceptional interior space, together with advanced technology at an accessible price. The response from customers has been very encouraging, with orders already exceeding our initial expectations. This nomination reflects the strength of that overall package and our ambition to make electric mobility both desirable and practical for more people.” —Christopher McKinnon, Vice President, Head of Product and Product Director Office for Europe at Hyundai Motor Europe.

IONIQ 3 combines several class-leading features that address key requirements of European customers. Its aerodynamic design contributes to driving efficiency, while its spacious interior offers the practicality of a larger vehicle despite its compact exterior dimensions. Intuitive technology supports everyday usability, and accessible pricing makes electric mobility available to a broader range of customers.

The European Car of the Year award is an independent automotive competition established in 1964. Its international jury consists of 65 automotive journalists from across Europe, who evaluate eligible new vehicles based on criteria including design, innovation, performance, value for money and market positioning.

Seven finalists are selected from the initial list of candidates. Following further evaluation and voting, the overall winner of the 2027 award will be announced on 8 January 2027 at the Brussels Motor Show.
