#5738. Car Design Has Gone Downhill Since The Plymouth Prowler

There must be something in the water, because there hasn't been a new car design in nearly 30 years that can even remotely be considered cool or fun. Most barely even scratch the surface of interesting these days. The most recent example I can think of when it comes to production cars that actually do something different at play with the visual form was the Plymouth Prowler of 1997. There wasn't anything like this on the road when it debuted in the late 1990s, and there still isn't today. The Prowler was a completely different way of looking at cars. Blending an element of retrofuturism and old-school hot-rod aesthetics, the Prowler was as much a baby boomer's fantasy as it was the car millennials drove in their adolescent dreams.

Then-Chrysler Corporation lead designer Tom Gale was responsible for so many great designs of the era, including a trio of the most unusual concept cars of all time: Chrysler's Thunderbolt, Atlantic, and Phaeton. I don't know if Gale just saw the world differently than other designers, or was just capable of more than they were — his work on the sublime Lamborghini Diablo indicates this may be true — but in either case the Prowler was immediately a stunning piece of automotive history.

Earlier in the 1990s, the Chrysler Corporation had launched the Dodge Viper, a study in function over form. That car was built exclusively to deliver hot nasty speed to its passengers. The Prowler was perhaps its foil, bringing the most style possible to the fight, but without any substance to back it up. For the purposes of this conversation, the substance doesn't matter. Prowler has style icon status and nothing has matched it, let alone beaten it, since. There are definitely cooler cars, but cooler looking? Fat chance.

I'm going to fess up right here and say that the Prowler was the first car I was obsessed with. When it hit dealerships in 1997, exclusively in purple metallic, I was 10 years old, and extremely impressionable. I fell head over heels in love with it. I had dozens of scale models, T-shirts, dealer posters, magazine article clippings, and advertisements for the strange car scattered around my room for its entire production run. You've all met those kids who are fixated on a single subject — well, for me it was the Prowler. I wished it was faster, I wished it drove better, and I wished it wasn't powered by a minivan-like V6. Most of all, I wished I owned one. I'm an adult now and I still think this was among the wildest swings a car designer could have made. Nobody's this brave anymore.

I can admit that there's more to making a good car than making it look cool, but why aren't designers trying? The Prowler couldn't exist today for myriad reasons, but its spirit can soldier on to bring a bit more levity and flair to car design. Apparently everything has to be egg-shaped, with thin slitty LED headlights, thick A-pillars, and sharp, angular minimalism. And you can forget about a convertible at all, really, because allegedly nobody buys them.

Even car interior design has become little more than a minimalist dashboard with a large touchscreen in the middle. I wouldn't say the Prowler's interior looks nice by any stretch of the imagination, but at least it has a little fun with the genre. The color-matched binnacle and center-high-mounted gauges are absolutely ace. You can't beat a column-mounted tach, I mean, come on.

The Prowler was an attempt to do something completely different. Plymouth was on the ropes at the time anyway, so, to use a mixed sports metaphor, why not swing for the fences? What good is it to keep producing boring rebadged models until you fade away?

Maybe this was the beginning of the end for car design, as it brought about a wave of retro-inspired ripoff designs that brought nothing new to the table and suddenly became de rigueur. Or maybe it was so good a first shot that it never should have been tried again.

We've since seen 30 years of Mustang/Mini/Challenger/ID.Buzz-style returns to the same well for diminishing result. Plymouth isn't absolved of this, as it quickly pushed out the PT Cruiser in an effort to stave off death. The brand died anyway and its replica-retro-for-the-everyman car was stolen for Chrysler. Result? Nothing feels stylistically special anymore. When even Ferrari is farming its own history for retro-modern designs, it's time to hang up and try again. The trend has been taken too far, and we need to look for the next thing. Please. I'm begging you.

I'm no design history scholar, but it seems that aesthetics tend toward bland aggression when there's a pessimistic societal weight. In the face of technofascism, uneasy economics, and a troubling climate outlook, it's difficult to be optimistic about anything, and design is seemingly suffering as a result. Practice radical optimism: In the face of everything, be joyful for a future better than the present. Even if it never comes, the infective nature of optimism can always make someone's day better. If there are any designers reading this, stop trying to emulate the Prowler's retro-coded look to the past. Please aim for its future-inspired optimism.

#5737. Incretin Drug May Improve Menstrual Regularity

— Dual GLP-1/GIP receptor agonist tied to shorter cycles, less variability

BALTIMORE -- Use of a combined GLP-1/GIP receptor agonist was associated with improved menstrual regularity, an analysis of a prospective cohort showed.

Women taking the combination incretin drug had a reduction in cycle length of 2.5 days compared with a reduction of 0.4 days for those on a GLP-1 drug and 0.8 days for never-users, reported Shruthi Mahalingaiah, MD, MS, of the Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health in Boston, and colleagues.

The combined GLP-1/GIP receptor agonist also was associated with a more pronounced decrease in mean individual cycle variability, at 6.93 days compared with 7.45 days for GLP-1 drugs and 7.46 days for never-users, they reported during a presentation at the American Society for Reproductive Medicine (ASRM) annual meeting.

Furthermore, a subgroup analysis of patients with polyendocrine metabolic ovarian syndrome (PMOS; formerly polycystic ovary syndrome [PCOS]) showed even more marked effects. For this group, mean individual cycle variability was 5.80 days for combined GLP-1/GIP receptor agonist users, 7.34 days for GLP-1 receptor agonist users, and 9.45 days for never users, they reported.

"This more pronounced reduction among PMOS individuals might suggest a separate pathway that might focus on ovulatory health in addition to those metabolic parameters that support ovulation, but further research is necessary," Mahalingaiah said.

Rachel Weinerman, MD, an ob/gyn and reproductive endocrinologist at Case Western Reserve University in Cleveland who wasn't involved in the study, told MedPage Today that this study's findings about PMOS were particularly interesting.

"We know that because PMOS is a metabolic issue, weight loss can actually help patients achieve a more regular cycle," she said. Thus, when a PMOS patient is on a weight-loss medication, it makes sense that they would both lose weight and have more regular periods, she noted.

Weinerman added that irregular periods aren't only problematic for people trying to get pregnant; they can signal underlying health issues. This study is a good reminder that PMOS patients are a distinct group who may respond to medications differently than others with similar characteristics, she said.

More than 10 million women in the U.S. use either a GLP-1 or combined GLP-1/GIP receptor agonist, which work through different mechanisms of action (the only GLP-1/GIP drug approved by the FDA is tirzepatide [Zepbound]). These therapies may impact ovulatory health through systemic and metabolic health improvements or direct ovarian impacts, Mahalingaiah noted.

While some retrospective studies have suggested an association with improved menstrual regularity, no prospective studies have looked at it as a primary endpoint. So this research sought to determine if these agents were associated with changes in mean cycle length and cycle variability in tracked menstrual cycles.

"We wanted to show, with a little more granularity, the extent to which we might see impact," Mahalingaiah told MedPage Today.

Researchers utilized the Apple Women's Health Study (AWHS), which is a longitudinal digital cohort of about 147,000 U.S. women and their prospectively logged menstrual cycles. Patients are 18 and older, have an iPhone, and have menstruated at least once. Participants in this analysis were younger than 50, could not be using hormones or be pregnant or lactating, and had a cycle length between 10 and 90 days. They had to have logged at least 3 cycles.

Mahalingaiah and team ultimately analyzed 1,945 participants with 70,134 logged cycles; most (1,564) were never-users while 258 used a GLP-1 medication and 123 used a combined GLP-1/GIP medication in the past year.

This cohort responded to surveys with questions about weight-loss medications, demographics, and medical history. They also consented to share their logged menstrual information recorded on HealthKit, though authors did not use any sensor-based data.

Researchers stratified groups by those who indicated using a GLP-1 or combined GLP-1/GIP receptor agonist in the past year and those who hadn't, as well as those with and without PMOS. Baseline characteristics were similar between groups, though medication users veered older (age 38 to 39 vs 35) and had higher parity than never-users. They also had a higher proportion of PMOS, infertility, type 2 diabetes, and hypertension.

The primary reason for being prescribed a GLP-1 or GLP-1/GIP receptor agonist in the past year was weight loss for health, followed by weight loss for appearance and PMOS.

Mahalingaiah noted that future manuscripts will break down this data by particular agents.

"There is such little innovation in women's health pharmaceuticals that maybe data like this could help people in pharma think about the additional mechanisms that might promote ovulation," Mahalingaiah said.

#5736. Can Silicon Valley Build A Safe Class 8 Truck? Early EV Semi Fires Expose The Experience Gap

If you drove through the East Bay area of California on Tuesday night, you probably got caught in a nightmare. A Tesla Semi traveling down Interstate 880 in Hayward caught fire during the evening commute. There was no crash, no multi-vehicle pileup, and no obvious collision. The driver noticed something was wrong, pulled over near Highway 92, and got out unharmed.

Then the lithium-ion pack did what high-energy battery chemistry does when it gets pushed past its thermal limits… By the time crews finished foaming down the wreckage, all that remained of the tractor was its rear axles and a pile of ash.

The California Highway Patrol shut down both directions of the freeway out of concern over toxic chemical smoke. Northbound lanes eventually opened back up, but southbound lanes stayed closed for nearly ten hours while fire crews managed the blaze and let the pack burn itself out. When the sun came up, all that remained of the tractor was its rear axles and a pile of ash.

This is the second time in just over two years that a single Tesla Semi has shut down a major California highway for half a day. Back in August 2024, a Tesla-operated Semi drifted off Interstate 80 near Emigrant Gap, hit a tree, and caught fire. It took 50,000 gallons of water, air drops from Cal Fire, and a 15-hour closure to clear the road.

Whenever this happens, the anti-EV crowd immediately pounces and claims electric trucks are rolling time bombs. That’s a lie, as we’ve repeatedly explained here, but the knee-jerk reaction from tech enthusiasts, which claims this is just normal trucking business, is equally disconnected from reality.

Something deeper is going on here, and it highlights an important cultural divide between Silicon Valley and the century-old world of heavy-duty commercial trucking. Instead of trashing people and pointing fingers, let’s look at a deeper lesson the industry can learn from this.

First, let’s look at the numbers before anyone starts cherry-picking data.

According to the National Fire Protection Association, US fire departments respond to roughly 15,000 large truck fires every single year. That is about 40 diesel rigs catching fire every day across the country. Blown turbochargers spray oil on hot exhaust manifolds, wheel-end bearings seize at 70 mph, and dragging drum brakes ignite recap tires. Diesel trucks burn all the time, so fire is not a phenomenon unique to electric trucks.

The difference is how they burn. When a diesel tractor catches fire, the local volunteer fire department can usually knock it down with an engine tank and some foam in 45 minutes. A heavy tow truck drags the scorched chassis off the road, sweepers clear the debris, and traffic starts moving again before the morning rush hour is ruined.

When an 800-plus kilowatt-hour commercial battery pack enters thermal runaway, the playbook goes out the window. The cells generate their own oxygen as they break down, turning the battery into a self-sustaining furnace. First responders cannot just drown it and walk away. Their only choice is to pump tens of thousands of gallons of water on it for hours or stand back in defensive mode to keep it from spreading while half a county gets gridlocked. At best, all they can do is hopefully cool it down enough to get it off the road before it might catch fire again.

Then there is the sample size problem. There are roughly 4 million Class 8 commercial diesel trucks on American roads. By contrast, the active Tesla Semi fleet is estimated somewhere between 150 and a few hundred early-production and pilot tractors.

Mathematically speaking, you cannot calculate a clean failure rate on an N of two. A single flat tire that catches fire and spreads to a battery pack can swing the statistical safety record of a 100-truck pilot fleet by 50%. But you also cannot ignore it when two trucks in a tiny pilot fleet cause catastrophic, 10-to-15-hour interstate shutdowns.

So, instead of being lazy and relying on just the numbers, we need to look for more context.

This is not uniquely a Tesla problem. It is an electric startup problem.

Remember Nikola? In the summer of 2023, several of their Tre battery-electric trucks caught fire, including one at their Phoenix headquarters and another in customer testing. Nikola initially hinted at foul play, but third-party investigators quickly found the real culprit: a coolant leak inside the battery packs supplied by Romeo Power had caused an internal electrical short. Nikola had to issue a voluntary recall on all 209 trucks they had built, halting sales and dragging every pack back to the shop to fix the design.

Startups approach trucking like consumer electronics. They design from a clean sheet, prioritize maximum aerodynamic efficiency, integrate components into custom monolithic structures, and move fast.

But, when you take the “move fast and break things” approach, the stakes are a lot higher in the trucking world.

Class 8 trucking is a brutal environment that eats clever ideas for breakfast. A big rig does not live the gentle life of a passenger car in sunny California. It flexes under 80,000 pounds of gross weight, takes violent torsional twisting over uneven loading docks, endures constant low-frequency road battering, and rattles across millions of frost heaves.

If there is a slightly loose coolant quick-connect, an undersized wire harness, or a busbar that flexes half a millimeter under torsional load, commercial duty cycles are like Liam Neeson in Taken. If you’re a problem of any kind, the duty cycle will find you, and it will kill you.

This brings us to the traditional truck builders. Why aren’t we seeing Freightliner eCascadias or Volvo VNR Electrics burning down to the rims on I-80? They’re electric, too, right?

It’s not because traditional truckmakers possess magical, fireproof battery chemistry. They buy lithium-ion cells from the same tier-one suppliers as everyone else. In fact, if you dig into public regulatory filings, other manufacturers have run into the exact same terrifying failure modes that bit Nikola.

In 2023, Volvo and Mack filed safety recall reports with the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) covering hundreds of electric trucks. What was the defect? Their battery supplier had two separate manufacturing errors: coolant lines that were not fully locked into place, creating the risk of conductive fluid leaking across cells, and over-torqued internal fasteners that could snap off and rattle around inside the pack as loose metallic debris. Both defects created direct short-circuit and fire risks.

Why didn’t you read about those trucks burning down on the highway? Because the so-called legacy manufacturers have spent a century building institutional safety systems.

They caught this problem when a small fire actually broke out in a battery pack in the factory. The fire stayed contained within the pack, but Volvo didn’t try to hide it or explain it away. Instead, they did what the old school companies do, even if it slowed them down. Within a week, there was a stop-ship order on the packs and trucks already on the road were called in for a swap.

Traditional truckmakers move at a glacial pace compared to Silicon Valley, and tech fans love to criticize them for it. But, that slow pace comes from decades of scar tissue earned in battle. When an old school builder sees an internal assembly error or gets a strange sensor reading in fleet monitoring, their reflex is not to push an over-the-air firmware patch and hope for the best. They trigger stop-ship orders, use their huge dealer networks to ground the fleet, file Part 573 defect notices with the feds, and tear the battery packs open in a service bay.

Their systems caught the defect in the shop before it had the chance to catch fire on the freeway.

There is also an important philosophical divide in how these trucks are packaged.

Tesla chose the moonshot approach. To hit a 500-mile range target while hauling full freight, they needed maximum energy density and minimal tare weight. That led them to build a massive, integrated, structural battery pack that sits low between the frame rails. It is an engineering marvel in packaging efficiency, but it is also a monolithic block of energy. If one section suffers an internal failure, the entire pack is at risk.

Traditional builders like Volvo and Freightliner (Daimler Truck) took a far more conservative, regional-haul route. Instead of packing nearly a megawatt-hour of energy into a single tightly integrated structural assembly under the cab, they use smaller, lower-capacity modular packs—with Volvo literally hanging separate battery enclosures off the outside of the frame rails where diesel saddle tanks normally sit.

Hanging heavy battery boxes on the side of the chassis is terrible for maximum range and adds unnecessary weight, and may leave them more vulnerable to collisions. But, from a safety and service standpoint, it is brilliant. The packs are physically isolated from each other. If one module suffers a mechanical puncture, a coolant leak, or a thermal event, the damage is far easier to contain, inspect, and unbolt.

Silicon Valley brought the heavy trucking industry kicking and screaming into the electric era. Without Tesla pushing the envelope on megawatt charging and long-range electric haulers, diesel truckmakers would still be dragging their feet with compliance pilot programs, if even that.

Building an electric truck that can pull 82,000 pounds is not just a software problem, and it’s not just a battery chemistry problem. It is a mechanical durability problem that has to survive millions of miles of rough roads, rough drivers, and unpredictable operating conditions.

Experienced truck builders know how to build a chassis that survives that abuse, but they were slow to embrace the battery. Silicon Valley knows how to build the battery, but they are still learning how punishing the road really is.

Until those two worlds finally learn from each other, fire crews are going to keep having very long nights on the interstate. Hopefully more cross-pollination and experience eventually lead to improvements for all sides of the industry.

Jennifer Sensiba is a long time efficient vehicle enthusiast, writer, and photographer. She grew up around a transmission shop, and has been experimenting with vehicle efficiency since she was 16 and drove a Pontiac Fiero.

#5735. Why Checking CarMax Makes Sense (Even If You End Up Buying Elsewhere)

You don't need me to tell you this, but I'm still going to say it: Cars are more expensive now than ever before. If you're a member of the 1%, then you probably couldn't care less about this, but for the remaining 99% of the people living in the United States, it makes the already costly prospect of car buying an even spendier endeavor. Unfortunately, even the old trick of buying used cars to save money is less effective than it once was, since used car prices are higher than ever, too, and used cars don't always come with the peace of mind that a new, warrantied car does. That's where CarMax comes in, as its business model relies on selling used cars that SAE-certified technicians have inspected over 125 components on, and if that's not enough, you can pay for an extended warranty.

The downside of CarMax is that its used cars tend to be more expensive than used cars from other dealerships or private sellers, and there's no haggling room in its prices (which is either a pro or a con, depending on your persuasion). Even if you're not ready to pay CarMax prices for a used car, though, it still pays to check the retailer's inventory, since not only don't you have to negotiate, they're picky about what they sell.

If finding the cheapest car around is your top priority, then CarMax isn't going to be the best place to start your search. If, however, you're sick of getting the runaround from other dealerships or you're burnt out from the dreaded act of haggling with private sellers, then the benefits of buying from CarMax may outweigh the drawbacks.

Every last vehicle in CarMax's national inventory has never experienced flood or frame damage, never has a salvage history, and never has odometer discrepancies. Every CarMax purchase includes a 10-day money-back guarantee, as well as at least a 30-day limited warranty, which may be longer depending on where you live. And they're all offered with CarMax's MaxCare warranty, which, for an extra cost, can extend up to five years after the purchase date on most cars.

I bought my 2017 Mini Cooper S four-door from CarMax in 2020, and I ended up buying the five-year extended warranty because I did not want to be on the hook for European car repair prices. Yes, the initial purchase price was higher than I would've paid at a normal dealership, or from a private party, but I really wanted the peace of mind provided by a warranty, and I have no regrets.

I ended up using the warranty several times over the course of my five-year contract, and I never had any trouble getting repairs covered, from replacing an engine mount to replacing a water pump to replacing a wobbly sunroof switch and a few other, smaller issues. It all depends on your priorities, but it never hurts to look at all options in front of you.

#5734. Antwerp’s Electric Cranes Can Handle More Than Today’s Cargo

Katoen Natie has put two all-electric mobile harbour cranes into service at the Port of Antwerp, with four more joining them across three deep-sea multipurpose terminals. The completed fleet will include four 200-tonne cranes and two 150-tonne machines, with paired lifting capacity reaching 400 tonnes. Katoen Natie expects the six cranes to displace as much as 500,000 litres of diesel annually once they’re operating.

The October commissioning announcement is worth attention because these are heavy, mobile machines working in a commercial port. They aren’t small demonstrations, and the engineering addresses the conditions that historically made mobile harbour cranes difficult to electrify.

Fixed container cranes were straightforward candidates for electrification. They stay in one location, have repetitive operating patterns and can be connected to substantial electrical supplies. Mobile harbour cranes travel along the quay, move between assignments and handle cargo that can vary considerably from one vessel to the next. An onboard diesel generator provided that mobility without requiring the terminal to build electrical connections everywhere the crane might work.

Konecranes’ solution separates the energy needed for lifting from the energy needed for travel. External grid electricity supplies the heavy working loads, while rechargeable batteries allow the cranes to move between operating positions. This avoids carrying an enormous battery for work that can be powered directly from the grid. It also eliminates diesel exhaust at the equipment and reduces local noise.

The bigger advantage is that the investment isn’t tied to one type of cargo. A mobile harbour crane can handle steel, industrial equipment, large transformers, wind components, scrap, project cargo, conventional bulk and containers. Port operators can change their handling arrangements as customers and cargo flows change without replacing the basic lifting machinery.

That matters as the energy transition changes maritime trade. In an earlier CleanTechnica analysis, I examined how ports face rising capital requirements even as fossil-fuel cargoes begin their long-term decline. Coal, oil and gas have supported major investments in berths, terminals, channels and inland connections. Their declining role creates commercial risks for infrastructure designed around individual commodities.

Ports are looking for new business in containers, renewable-energy equipment, industrial products and alternative fuels. Some will succeed, but they cannot all capture the same growth in container traffic, and low-carbon molecules will not replace fossil-fuel cargo tonne for tonne. Equipment investments have to make sense under more than one plausible freight forecast.

Electric mobile cranes are well suited to that uncertainty. A machine capable of lifting a transformer today can handle scrap steel or construction machinery tomorrow. Its electrical supply can also be part of a wider investment supporting yard equipment, trucks, shore power and harbour craft.

That doesn’t make every new crane or grid connection worthwhile. A terminal still needs sufficient durable activity, suitable electrical infrastructure and a sound operating case. But investing in flexible electric equipment is less dependent on predicting which replacement cargo wins than building another dedicated fossil-fuel or specialized alternative-fuel facility.

Katoen Natie’s investment demonstrates how ports can reduce diesel consumption today while retaining more options for the freight they handle tomorrow.

My TFIE Strategy Briefing article connects Antwerp’s electric mobile harbour cranes with the larger challenge of investing in ports as fossil bulk declines, new industrial cargoes emerge and electrical infrastructure serves more of the waterfront.

#5733. At $7,500, Will This 2003 Jeep Wrangler Sahara Get Its Just Dessert?

Introduced in the 1940s, the Willys Jeep has long been hailed as the "truck that won the war." Let's see whether any of that DNA remains in today's Nice Price or No Dice Wrangler and, if so, whether its price wins our admiration.

It's almost unheard of for an automobile model to switch from one end driving to the other, but that has happened on at least two occasions. The first time came in 1970 when Triumph released the Toledo, a model visually identical to the preceding FWD 1300, but featuring RWD under its twinsie bodywork. The Toledo went on to sire the legendary Dolomite Sprint, making that initially backward-seeming move a laudable decision. Another notable example of an existing model switching driving ends is the Polestar 2, which, with its 2024 refresh, changed its base single-motor layout from front- to rear-wheel-drive.

The 2024 Polestar 2 Long Range we looked at last Friday proved that point moot as the model offers dual motors driving both ends. It also had the model's biggest battery, a desirable driving assistance package, and, at $22,995, seemingly unfathomable depreciation. That latter affliction might be because Polestar sales are currently being banned in the U.S. due to the model's China origins, with owners possibly wanting out before the parts situation becomes dire. Regardless of threats of impending doom, our Polestar's condition and price had a number of you willing to take the chance—hypothetically, of course—earning the EV a respectable 66% 'Nice Price' win.

The name Sahara comes from Arabic, where it means "desert." Of course, that means we shouldn't call the world's largest non-polar desert the "Sahara Desert," since that is redundant. It's the same thing as calling Los Angeles' ancient fossil graveyard the "La Brea Tar Pits," since that is literally "The Tar Tar Pits."

Jeep chose the Sahara name for one of the myriad trim levels and special editions applied to the TJ Wrangler over its 10-year model run. The Sahara package included body-color fender extenders, full doors, cloth upholstery, and five-spoke alloy wheels.

This 2003 Jeep Wrangler Sahara is painted in Sienna Pearl matched to a gray hardtop and interior trim. Its running boards and outer bumper caps were likely once black but, due to the sun's daily attacks, have also faded to a similar gray hue. Other indicators of this Jeep's age include a cracked and dented right-front fender and rust bubbling through on both front wings. Fortunately, those are bolt-ons, and replacements are readily available from places like Amazon and eBay, making these aesthetic imperfections easy to address.

The rest of the Jeep appears to be in good condition, with the seller noting the frame and the rest of the bodywork are free of rust. That's a big deal, considering this TJ is offered in Pennsylvania, arguably the crusty buckle on the Rust Belt.

Another attractive aspect of this TJ is its removable hard top. Yes, removing or replacing the top is a job that'll take two (or three) people to complete, but it really makes this a four-season ride. The seller does warn, however, that while the A/C works fine, the heater is non-functional, so all passengers should be forewarned to bundle up in inclement weather.

Other notable info from the ad includes a new set of tires, a refreshed front axle driveshaft and U-joints, and a new battery and radiator. So far, this Jeep has done 144,370 miles and comes with a clean title and a current state safety inspection sticker.

It should be noted that the TJ edition of the Wrangler was the last to express any of its American Motors Corporation heritage. After this model, the legendary AMC straight six would be retired, exorcising the last vestiges of its former parent in favor of fully Daimler-Chrysler parts.

As such, it's good to see the AMC 4.0-six under this TJ's hood. Not much can go wrong with these cast-iron throwbacks, and they still make a reasonable 190 horsepower and 225 pound-feet of torque. Also in the plus column, this TJ mates that engine with a five-speed manual and part-time 4WD with Dana axles at both ends.

Things this Jeep might need? Well, there are those fenders and a bumper cap in the front that 'could' be repaired. Also, time should be set aside to bring the heater back up to snuff. Then there's the Phillies sticker on the rear door. That's gotta go, or at least be replaced with a new decal. The current one, faded from red to pink, looks a bit too limply phallic.

Okay, so this TJ is a bit of a mixed bag. It's far from perfect, but it's also perfectly usable as-is. Its issues are somewhat mitigated by its specs and the work already done (new tires, radiator, etc.). Could the Jeep's $7,500 asking price tip the balance? As we are all probably aware, Wranglers—except for the four-cylinder editions of the weird rectangular-headlight YJ—have a pretty big fanbase, making them a bit more dear than one might expect.

What do you think? Is this TJ Sahara a good deal at that price point? Or is that just too much to wrangle given the Jeep's inherent flaws?

Philadelphia, Pennsylvania, Craigslist, or go here if the ad disappears.

#5732. First Guidelines for Cancer Caregivers Address Key Information Void in Care

— Information and advice about how to be a caregiver and attend to caregiver needs

First-ever guidelines for cancer caregivers offer information and advice on topics ranging from medical management and emotional support to finances and legal issues.

Published by the National Comprehensive Cancer Network (NCCN), the "NCCN Guidelines for Patients: Caregiving for People with Cancer" helps fill an information void created by the growing number of new cancer diagnoses and cancer survivors.

"When a loved one is diagnosed with cancer, naturally, most people want to show up for them, however they can," said Anthea A. Gray, PsyD, of the Ohio State University Comprehensive Cancer Center in Columbus, in an NCCN announcement. "But pretty quickly, many realize it's not just about cancer. They're also juggling everything else in their lives. In moments when things feel scary or uncertain, or when everything starts to feel too much, having trusted, easy-to-find information matters a lot."

The timing of the guideline development and publication reflects the evolving landscape of oncology care.

"More and more people are living longer with cancer and many of them are older," Gray told MedPage Today. "There are more needs that come with that. As people age, comorbidities start to increase and so do the needs. Another factor is that more younger people are getting cancers than in the past, and people who are younger have different challenges. They might have young children or they might be earlier in their careers. The need for caregiving support has just become more evident."

According to the guideline, at least 70% of caregivers have jobs and other personal responsibilities, and 60% live separate from the patient. Each section of the guideline includes "Caregiver's Corner," which includes suggestions and advice about caregiver self-care. Additionally, the guideline has a dedicated chapter on caregiver well-being.

Individual chapters in the guideline cover definitions and explanations related to caregiving, basic tenets of caregiving (including medical and cancer-specific terminology), being part of a patient's cancer care team, and providing patient care and support (including non-medical issues). A separate chapter provides information on resources for caregiving and caregivers. The booklet also includes a glossary of key terms for caregivers.

Gray offered a couple of examples of issues that caregivers commonly encounter but might not have an adequate understanding of how to deal with.

"Feeling prepared for appointments is a big issue for many people," she said. "They have the best of intentions and want to help, but they don't know the questions to ask. The guidelines do an excellent job of articulating some of the questions for a patient's care team. Understanding the side effects of cancer treatment is another common issue, and the guidelines offer some particularly useful information."

The information on financial aspects of cancer care can help patients and caregivers avoid being blindsided by the cost.

"A person could work for 20 or 30 years and have a very prudent reserve for rainy days or health issues, and very quickly, they can see their savings just go out the door," she said. "It's heartbreaking because with all the advancements in healthcare and cancer treatment, people often have a long life to live [after cancer diagnosis and treatment] but now they have minimal or strained resources because of the treatment."

Caregivers and patients alike will find the information about survivorship useful and perhaps thought provoking.

"Survivorship is an understudied or misunderstood area, particularly as it pertains to caregivers," said Gray. "I think a lot of people have the expectation that once somebody finishes treatment, they ring the bell and think, 'OK, it's back to life as usual.' For many people, that's not how it goes. Maybe they go on to long-term hormonal treatment or have ongoing physical and emotional effects. That's really hard to understand, so this resource really helps coach them through that."

Though the guidelines address issues specific to cancer care, some of the topics and information could readily apply to caregivers for patients with other types of chronic illness.

"Issues related to fatigue or nausea, for example, could be helpful for caregivers for patients with other conditions," said Gray.

The guidelines can be downloaded at no cost from NCCN or from an associated mobile app.

#5731. Elon Musk’s Attack Ads Are Backfiring As Midterms Near

US Federal Election Commission data revealed this week that Elon Musk has donated at least $445 million into Republican-aligned political action committees and groups since last year. Interestingly, it seems that Musk’s deep influence isn’t enough, and he is worried.

If Democrats win big on Tuesday in midterm elections, Musk insists US democracy will come to an “end.”

“This would result in America becoming a permanent one-party state and the end of democracy in any meaningful sense of the word,” Musk wrote Friday on X. “All that is needed to ensure this doesn’t happen is 51 Republican senators in 2029.”

The Tesla and SpaceX CEO has a long track record of engaging in political quid pro quo. His companies receive billions in federal contracts. The once climate-is-all advocate now weighs heavily on the side of oil/fossil fuels. His DOGE actions may have forced labor shortages in regulatory agencies that were scrutinizing his businesses.

But now Musk seems distraught that newfound Democratic Congressional power could make him a target of investigations after the midterms.

And his fears may have merit. Musk’s blatant attempts to influence election results are getting a lot of recent analysis and condemnation.

“I worry about what Musk’s impulsive bullying is doing and has done to others more vulnerable than I am,” former Secretary of Labor Robert Reich wrote earlier this month after Musk exclaimed, “Prison for Reich.” Such extreme comments are part of Musk lifelong pattern, Reich continued, in which “he’s threatened and racially denigrated and pushed around (people) his whole life.”

Reich added that he is worried for that voters whom Musk has been “deluging with TV ads for Republican candidates” as well as for “federal workers who have been hurt by his so-called Department of Government Efficiency, Americans who have been harmed by his wanton destruction of public services, and people around the globe who are worse off because of cuts to USAID pushed by Elon’s DOGE.”

Musk’s America PAC has contributed to 43 US House and Senate races, and that influx of cash has funded vast amounts of television commercials, digital ads, text messages, and mailers. It’s almost easy to forget that Musk became a persona non grata with Trump and the Republican Party last year.

America PAC stands for “secure borders, safe cities, free speech, sensible spending, fair justice system, and self-protection,” according to its website.

These objectives might sound fair and reasonable to many voters, yet a majority of America PAC money goes to attack ads. Musk typically cross-posts America PAC’s damaging content to his more than 200 million followers on X, the social media site he owns and whose social commentary he controls.

Ezra Klein, co-author of Abundance, recently interviewed Quinn Slobodian, professor of international history at Boston University. Slobodian interprets Musk’s midterm campaign finance moves as “his flexing of his own power to make or break candidates, as he wishes,” which, in turn, is a way “fine-tune that control over the media and messaging ecosystem and get out ahead of what he worries would be a regulatory wave that would accompany a Democratic majority.”

Why are such media messages — even those that are based on false premises — so compelling during the run-up to the 2026 US midterm elections? Voters frequently base their own political decisions on fake information and images rather than on reality. Social historian Heather Cox Richardson explains that many such techniques draw scenarios of a chaotic society and destroy individuals’ “ability to make sense of the world.” Some media consumers “turn to a strongman who promises to create order.” Others will get so exhausted they simply refuse to fight back. The path of least resistance is succumbing to the manipulation and “giving up control of their government to an authoritarian.”

In Texas alone, America PAC has provided more than $33 million toward the Ken Paxton Senate campaign. Additionally, Musk donated $168 million contribution to the Senate Leadership Fund, which routes money back to the Paxton campaign.

“Elon Musk wants our land. He wants our water. He wants our tax dollars,” James Talarico, the Texas Democratic nominee for Senate, said at a recent rally. “He wants his very own personal United States senator.” Talarico’s comments were supported by loud boos from the crowd.

At a rally at the University of Texas, Talarico said Musk was trying to “stop the movement that we are building in our state.” In contrast, he offered that his own campaign is “funded by 1.3 million individuals who have donated their hard-earned money.” Instead of corporate PAC money, Talarico has depended on small-dollar donations.

The day before that, Talarico’s campaign released a digital advertisement accusing Paxton of being a mere pawn to Musk. “Let’s remind Elon: Texas is not for sale.”

Interestingly, Democratic candidates in Texas and elsewhere across the US are holding their own — many electoral races are too close to call, even with Musk’s influence and campaign finance imbalances helping out Republicans.

#5730. 1969 Corvette Stingray For Sale With 70,881 Miles Shown Kept Its OG 350 V8

The third-generation Corvette, in my mind, will always be the last generation of Corvette before it got a bit ... sweaty. The C4 moved the model forward from a technical standpoint, but when you tell somebody to picture a "classic" Corvette, the C3 is the newest one that arguably qualifies. Good news, fellow C3 enjoyers, because there's a particularly clean one for sale right now that's said to still use the same engine it came with when it rolled off the assembly line almost six decades ago.

Spotted on Hemmings is a 1969 Chevy Corvette Stingray with its original 350-cubic-inch V8 that made 300 horsepower when new. Power from the original V8 goes to the rear wheels through a three-speed automatic transmission. There are a reasonable 70,881 miles on the odometer, and its current owner, who has had it for the past 34 years, says it's a turnkey car with no mechanical gremlins that's never seen rain.

That said, this owner has only had it for about three-fifths of the car's life, so I wouldn't put too much stock in the "never rained on" claim. Still, judging from the pictures, it definitely looks like a clean, running, decent example of a C3, though there are some caveats the seller is upfront about. The radio is missing, the air-conditioning doesn't work, and there's a "very small blemish" on the driver's side front fender. Also, its engine may be said to be original, but the paint is not. This car was originally white, but was later painted black sometime prior to the current owner's acquisition in 1992. And the steering wheel is an aftermarket part.

If you aren't super picky or looking for a museum piece, this could be a great C3 to buy. Original V8, no huge issues, and those pop-up headlights? C'mon. It's being sold out of Fairfield, New Jersey, in case you're nearby and want to check it out.

#5729. Record Demand Catches (Some) OEMs with Their Pants Down — Europe September 2026

BEVs remain in high demand in Europe, with pure electrics reaching a record 31% share last month thanks to a record 422,000 fully electric passenger cars being registered in Europe, which is not only a 63%(!) increase year over year (YoY), but is also the fastest growth rate in over three years!

Overall, plugin vehicles were up 49% YoY, dragged down by plugin hybrids, which grew only 23%, to 167,000 units, which was also a new record high for plugin hybrids in Europe, thus placing September’s total plugin volume at a best ever 590,000 units and its share at 43% (31% for BEVs only).

With BEVs pulling the market upwards, the year-to-date share for BEVs is now at 25% (36% for PHEVs and BEVs combined), which is already higher than the 2025 final result (20% BEV share, 29% adding PHEVs). This is an encouraging sign if we want to be close to 100% PEV share by the mid-2030s.

Looking at other powertrains, while petrol (18% share in September, down 21% YoY) and diesel (4%, down 28% YoY) continue their downward trend into irrelevance, the main news is the fact that we could have already passed the point of peak HEV — in September, plugless hybrids still grew, albeit less (+5% YoY) than the overall market (+10%, to 1.4 million units).

More important than that, if we compare the 34% share earned by HEVs in September 2026 with the share they had 12 months ago (36% then), we might be witnessing the beginning of plugless hybrids’ downward slope. So … if 2026 is the year of peak HEVs, how far are we from peak PHEVs?

Looking at the best sellers in several size categories, the EV push is starting to get visible. While we are far from the EV domination we see in China, things are indeed evolving in Europe.

Before, the only EVs that we saw on the overall podiums were Teslas. Now, besides those two usual suspects, the 3rd placed model in the midsize category, the Mercedes GLC, is also heavily electrified, with 59% of all its sales coming from either its new BEV model (representing 44% of its total sales) or the PHEV versions (15% of sales), leaving the ICE versions representing just 41% of total sales.

The A-segment has the Renault Twingo as the 2nd placed model. Like in China before, expect this category to be quickly electrified. Firstly, the ICE competition is shrinking. Secondly, not only do we have the Renault Twingo already on the podium, but the new Dacia Spring is set to become a success, so expect it to join the Renault sibling on the podium. As a third musketeer, the VW ID.Up is set to land sometime next year, which is another strong bet from the German brand — and nothing shorter than a podium position would do.

And even if all these competitors cannot remove the eternal Fiat Panda from the podium … then the 2028 Fiat Koala and Citroen 2CV surely will. And let’s not forget the Chinese competition, namely the future second-generation Leapmotor city car, said to be developed together with the Koala and 2CV. Not counting others, by 2028, we will have half a dozen serious candidates for podium positions in the city car category.

Looking at the other segments, the B-segment is still ICE-heavy, with none of the top 3 models having a plugin version, the C-segment is getting increasingly electrified (#1 VW Golf is 31% PHEV, #2 VW Tiguan is 32% PHEV, and #3 Jaecoo 7 is 47% PHEV).

In the full size category, electrification is also significant, with the #1 BMW 5 Series (including the BMW i5) having 74% of sales coming from either the BEV or the PHEV version; the #2 BMW X5 being 28% electrified (either with the BEV i5 or the PHEV version); and the #3 Range Rover Sport having 33% of its sales coming from the PHEV versions.

So, electrification in the D-segment is quite advanced, while the A, C, and E segments are showing good potential, with real progress being made. But the all-important B Segment is showing that it is in the laggard category.

(A note on the full size category and its importance on the European market: the best selling E-segment model was just #109 overall … which says a lot about the (in)significance of it in the larger scheme of things. True, margins are attractive, but it’s a faaar smaller market than its counterpart categories in the USA or China. The full size category represents 18% of the market in both China and the USA, while in Europe, it represents only 6%.)

Looking at September’s best selling EV models, the big news this month was Tesla winning 1st and 2nd place, and by a large margin — so much so that the #1 plus #2 win extended into the overall market! Yes, you have read that right — for only the second time ever, the other time being in December 2022, Tesla placed the Model Y in 1st and the Model 3 in 2nd on the overall market! Notice, also, BYD placing two models in the top 5. Here’s a more detailed analysis of the top 5 EVs in September:

#1 Tesla Model Y — Tesla’s midsize crossover is back in the driver’s seat, thanks to an amazing 41,987 registrations in September, a 52% increase YoY, and its best result in four and a half years! The Berlin-made crossover was the overall #1 in Germany, France, Iceland, Ireland, Sweden, the Netherlands, Norway, Switzerland, Denmark, Finland, and … maybe a few more I might be forgetting. This was its second best performance ever in Europe, only behind the 46,512-unit score of March 2023. With Model Y prices starting at €40,000, and even after six years on the market, the crossover offers an appealing package and, together with its Model 3 sibling and a few Chinese models, is reviving a lost category on the European automotive market — the midsize mainstream market. Remember a time when the Ford Mondeo, Opel Vectra, and others sold at least as much as the models of the premium Germans? Well, it seems that with their prices now starting above 50,000 euros, that has created a hole in the market, where between 40,000 to 50,000 euros you can get a lot of great midsize models (both Teslas, the Xpeng G6, the Mazda 6e/CX-6e, the Deepal S07, the BYD Seal/Seal U, the Leapmotor C10, the Zeekr 7 GT, etc.), for a fair bit less money than the German brands. Currently, you buy one of these with your wallet/head, not your heart. And that’s exactly where the market is headed. People don’t care as much about badge, they care about value. That is exactly the kind of buyer that is now searching for EVs. These people don’t want a badge, they simply want to lower their costs by switching from ICEVs to BEVs.

#2 Tesla Model 3 — Tesla’s sedan was 2nd in September thanks to 26,770 registrations, an awesome surge of 120% YoY that allowed it to be 2nd in the overall European market. Benefiting from the same tailwinds as the Model Y (mass surge in EV demand, competitive pricing, and rapid production ramp-up), the 10-year-old sedan is racing to meet soaring demand, as proven by the current 3- to 4-month waiting list. Its demand surged because price-conscious buyers are in the market and its prices, starting around €35,000, place it, a midsize sedan, in competition with models one segment below, compact hatchbacks. As of this moment, it seems Tesla’s sedan will recover its podium position in 2026, and maybe even provide a gold plus silver win for Tesla at the end of the year … which is no mean feat for a 10-year-old model wearing a body type (sedan) not popular in Europe.

#3 BYD Seal U (BEV+PHEV) — The Chinese SUV remained in the top 5 and hit a record 12,779 registrations last month, meaning that while the BYD’s star is recovering in China, thanks to its new-generation Ultra body, the old generation is still able to win medals in Europe all by itself (a bit like when veteran football/soccer players leave top leagues and semi-retire in less competitive ones…). September’s 3rd place finish was much thanks to generous discounts, but still, with the model’s development costs now well behind it, BYD can afford to go into hard discount territory with this one.

#4 Skoda Elroq — The Elroq continues surfing the BEV wave, with the 12,592 registrations representing a record result. Looking ahead, the Czech crossover will have to face fierce internal competition, as the new, smaller, and more affordable Skoda Epiq crossover is about to land. Being simultaneously cheaper and more modern than the Elroq, will the Epiq become Skoda’s new best seller at the cost of the Elroq?

#5 BYD Atto 2 (BEV+PHEV) — The Chinese make has another hit on its hands, with the small crossover scoring 11,111 registrations, mostly thanks to the new PHEV version. Why so popular in Europe? Besides not having real competition in the small PHEV category, the top range version has a 18 kWh battery, providing a usable range (90 km/56 mi). Additionally, it has increasingly important vehicle-to-load (V2L) capability. With the recent Dolphin G PHEV small hatchback using the same powertrain, expect the Shenzhen make to have another representative on the European table soon, especially considering that the new hatchback will be made in Hungary.

Outside the top 5, there were a number of record scores to mention, as would be expected in a record month.

Starting with Mercedes, we have both its representatives, the CLA EV (15th place with 7,206 registrations) and the new Mercedes GLC EV (16th place, 7,099 units) scoring record results.

Regardless of what might happen to the GLC, I am now curious to see whether the Mercedes GLC will be able to surpass to the BMW iX3’s volumes. I mean, the three-pointed-star model’s specs are a degree below the Bimmer’s, but on the other hand, it looks like Mercedes is ramping up production faster than BMW, as one can see from the waiting lists of both models (5 to 6 months for the Mercedes, around 9 months for the BMW). An example of that is that Mercedes is working its Bremen factory 24/6 to make the GLC, while BMW only now added a second shift to its Debrecen plant.

Good news also in the Kia stable: Its star player, the EV3, scored a record high, 7,385 registrations, ending the month in 11th. Even better than that, the new EV2 small crossover (it’s basically a hatchback on stilts, but anyway … marketing oblige) continues to ramp up, now reaching a record 6,458 registrations. With production still in ramp-up mode and a sizeable waiting list, the EV2 looks destined to become the automaker’s best seller in Europe.

Renault’s new baby also shined, with the Twingo rising to 10th thanks to a record 7,776 registrations. How high will the cutesy French EV go? Will 10,000 units in a month be possible?

Despite Volvo’s headaches due to battery issues that are hurting the production ramp-up of the Volvo EX60, its smaller sibling, the EX30, has given the brand something to smile about — the small crossover had its best result in two years thanks to 7,245 registrations.

Finally, a reference has to be given to the Chinese models. Besides the record performance of the #3 BYD Seal U, three other made-in-China models hit top 20 positions and record scores — the Jaecoo 7 PHEV was 13th, with 7,262 units; the Geely Starray plugin hybrid crossover was 17th, with 6,888 units; and the Leapmotor B10 was 19th, with 6,262 units. All three models are compact crossovers. And together with the BYD models, they add up to five Chinese representatives in the top 20. Something to think about….

Outside the top 20, there were records galore, so I won’t mention all of them. Instead, I will mention this:

Looking at the 2026 ranking, the big surprise was the four-position jump of the Tesla Model 3, into 2nd place.

It was to be expected that the Tesla sedan could climb onto the podium, thanks to September being a peak month for Tesla, but that it could surpass a 13,000-unit disadvantage over the (then) 2nd placed Skoda Elroq … now that was a surprise. And all of this is despite the Skoda Elroq posting a record month.

A podium position is now guaranteed for the veteran sedan, and we can even say that it is now the favorite for the 2026 runner-up spot, something few could have foreseen at the start of this year.

Just below the podium, another question is arising — will the Renault 5 be able to resist the advances of the BYD Seal U and Atto 2? As for the latter, the current distance allows Renault some peace of mind. When it comes to the former, … it will be difficult for the Renault 5 to stay ahead of the up and coming model, especially considering that the R5 has to share its factory line with several other models, like the R4, Nissan Micra, Renault Scenic, and Renault Megane, preventing it from ramping its production up further.

As for the remaining changes, the Skoda Enyaq was up one spot, to 8th, surpassing the soon-to-be-retiring VW ID.4. Will the new VW ID.Tiguan improve on the ID.4’s performance? The high point of the outgoing crossover was two 3rd place finishes, in 2022 and 2023. That’s a tall order for the new contender….

In the second half of the table, the Kia EV3 profited from its record score to jump two positions, into 14th, while the Audi Q6 e-tron and Volvo EX30 profited from their Volvo XC60 PHEV and Audi Q4 e-tron siblings’ horrible September performances to surpass them and reach the 17th and 18th positions, respectively.

Finally, a reference must be given to the deliveries of the #11 Mercedes CLA EV. It might not sound like much of a highlight, but the point here is that, historically, the CLA was a niche model for Mercedes, sandwiched between the poor man’s Mercedes A-Class and the more properly statutary Mercedes, the C-Class. As a consequence, the CLA’s overall sales in Europe were no more than 65,000 to 70,000 units per year, in a good year. With the BEV version of the CLA now above 52,000 units, and still three months to go, it won’t be hard for the CLA to beat the upper limit of the sales target this year.

Sure, neither the A-Class nor the C-Class (yet) have BEV versions to cast a shadow over the CLA, but it is a significant performance nevertheless, and with the CLA having a BEV station wagon version, that neither the A or C-classes will have, it sure looks like the CLA will continue to sell in significant numbers. Adding to its fortunes is the fact that it’s one of the most energy efficient EVs around, which allows it to post above-average range despite having average-sized batteries. Go, Mercedes CLA!

Having a quick look at the overall brand ranking, the highlights were the contrasting fortunes of Tesla (7th, up 71% YoY) and Ford (16th, down 18% YoY), as well as the usual rise and rise of the Chinese brands.

Two comments regarding the US automakers:

BYD, also known as the Chinese Toyota, ended the month in 11th, with 49,340 units, up 102% YoY. That’s ahead of mainstream staples like Dacia (12th), but, above all, MG (13th). Yep, despite having a few years of advance and also selling ICE models, SAIC’s MG is now behind BYD in Europe.

But the strength of the Chinese automotive industry is the fact that it doesn’t rely on one major OEM, but rather the diversity it offers to European buyers — China has 15 brands in Europe’s top 50! Of them, the highlights are surging Jaecoo (#22, +157%), Omoda (#27, +111%), Leapmotor (#28, +324%), and Chery (#29, 688%).

(One could say that the overall European market is growing — +10% YoY — thanks to the rise of Chinese OEMs….)

As for the plugin auto brand ranking, the leader, Volkswagen, remained in the lead, but again lost share (8.7%, down from 8.9% in August and down from 9.3% in May). Sure, it still holds a sizeable advantage over rising BYD (8.2%, up 0.2%), and its new EVs (VW ID.Polo, ID.Cross, ID.Tiguan, etc.) should help it recover some sales, but … the German brand cannot allow itself to slow down. If it does, the company will get BYD on its back real fast … in its home market. And if you can’t win at home.…

#3 BMW (6.9%, down 0.3%) is stable in the last place on the podium, but it ended the month just 100 units ahead of Tesla (6.9% share, up from 6% in August). Tesla did benefit from a higher than usual end of quarter high tide, though. This looks to be an entertaining race in the last months of the year, as BMW currently lacks models that can add significant volume that might counterweight the surging demand that Tesla is now experiencing. I mean, the iX3 alone is not enough, the new i3 sports sedan will need to ramp up fast and deliver significant volume in Q4 if BMW wants to have any chance of keeping the bronze medal in 2026.

In 5th we have Mercedes (5.8%). It is holding steady in the last position of the top 5 and even gained a little bit more distance over #6 Audi (5.3%) and #7 Skoda (5.2%).

Arranging things by automotive group, everybody lost share except BYD.

Leader Volkswagen Group lost a significant amount of share, going from 23.9% share in August to its current 23.3%. Sure, the title is more than secured, but, for 2027, the fresh metal coming soon needs to correspond with expectations if the German OEM wants to stay comfortable in the lead.

#2 BMW Group (8.4%, down 0.3%) lost a worrying amount of share, especially considering that the new 3rd placed model is a rising BYD (8.2%, up 0.2%), just 0.2% behind it. At this point, it seems all but inevitable that the Chinese OEM will end the year in the runner-up spot, as its lineup is so vast and the volume deliveries increasing at such speed that it seems BYD’s true target is not BMW’s #2 spot but … VW’s throne.

As for now-4th-placed Stellantis … the never ending slide continues (7.9%, down 0.2%). The multinational OEM’s drops seem to have no end in sight. Worst of all, in a record month when every top OEM had several models scoring record results, Stellantis had … none. The multinational conglomerate lacks a star player. It has plenty of models selling okay — some 2,000–3,000 units per month — but the company lacks a frontrunner. Will that be the next Peugeot 208? One thing is for certain: that new generation has a lot of weight on its shoulders. If it fails….

Outside the top 5, #6 Geely (6.9%, up from 6.8% in August) gained ground on #5 Hyundai–Kia (7.5%). And with 7th placed Tesla (5.9%) now showing up on the radar as well, there could be an interesting race for 6th place.

2026 has been a wild ride in the EV world.

At the beginning of the year, legacy OEMs were happy with the growth of EV share in Europe — 20% to 30% BEV growth rates were good enough to keep investments in EVs steady, investments that would be funded by their still significant ICE business.

2025 ended with only one Chinese model in the European top 20, and Tesla ended the year in 4th, behind VW, BMW, and Mercedes. For the status quo to be perfect, you only need to convince those pesky eurocrats to soften CO2 targets….

Only, on the 28th of February 2026, with US and Israeli attacks on Iran, oil prices surged and the world has changed, and with it, so has the EV world.

In many markets, fuel prices escalated and people saw their wallets grow a hole. So many people who could buy a new car went and bought a BEV, preferably one that was readily available and cheap. It was not an ego-related buy, but a necessity-related one. And that changes the game.

Added to the Chinese auto market slump that was already happening towards the end of 2025, it was perfect timing for local OEMs to launch into export markets — sending production there that was originally meant for demand at home which had gone missing.

Now focusing on Europe, the increased EV demand quickly bought whatever existing stock was available, and waiting lists quickly started to grow.

Only, many people didn’t wanted to wait several months for their new BEV, they wanted to start saving money immediately, so they turned to whoever allowed them to receive a new electric vehicle the next day.

That was the perfect timing for Chinese OEMs and Tesla, which had been seeing its sales slowly erode in the previous couple of years. Because Tesla had competitively priced BEVs and an easily scalable production structure, it managed to quickly profit from the demand surge.

So, a win for Tesla and the Chinese EV makers.

Here’s what most numbers available do not show — how their sales are now compared to a year ago. Are they surfing the EV demand wave? Here’s a sample of European results in September:

Surfing the wave — Kia (+110% YoY), Toyota (+94%), Renault (+73%).

Trying to keep up — Stellantis (+53% YoY).

With their pants down — Ford (+3% YoY), Volvo (+4%), Hyundai (0%), Volkswagen Group (+19% YoY), BMW (+12%), Mercedes (+15%).

Always interested in the auto industry, particularly in electric cars, Jose has pioneered on documenting the plug-in sales evolution through the EV Sales blog, allowing him to gain an expert view on where EVs are right now and where they are headed in the future. Extending that work and expertise, Jose was co-founder of EV-Volumes and currently works with the European Alternative Fuels Observatory on EV sales matters.

#5728. Kimi Antonelli Becomes Youngest-Ever F1 World Champion

For the first time since 1953, an Italian is the F1 World Drivers' Champion. Kimi Antonelli finished third at the Mexico City Grand Prix on Sunday, clinching the championship. The 20-year-old Mercedes driver also became F1's youngest-ever champion, surpassing the mark set by a 23-year-old Sebastian Vettel in 2010. With Antonelli never crossing the finish line lower than fifth so far this season, the ninth-place finish he needed to secure his maiden title seemed like a cakewalk. However, nothing is ever guaranteed in Formula 1.

Antonelli was never in contention for the win at Autódromo Hermanos Rodríguez, but that didn't matter. His main goal was getting home in one piece. The young Italian qualified fourth and stayed there before cycling to the lead during the pit stop. He would eventually have to yield the position to make his own stop on Lap 43. He could've held the lead if he stayed out on lap longer. Valtteri Bottas' Cadillac suffered a catastrophic left-front suspension failure while heading into Turn 1. The machine became a sled, slid through the run-off area and slammed into the barrier. It took a while for Bottas to get out of the cockpit, but he walked away. Race control was forced to throw a red flag to recover his car.

Back in fourth for the standing restart, Antonelli took advantage of Charles Leclerc locking up into Turn 1 to snag the final podium spot. He may have finished third behind Max Verstappen and Lewis Hamilton, but the celebration under a blizzard of confetti might be the most memorable of the Italian's entire career. After the trophies were handed out and the champagne sprayed, Hamilton and Verstappen hoisted the new champion up onto their shoulders in front of the roaring crowd. It was the Hollywood freeze-frame ending to Antonelli's meteoric ascent to racing's highest summit.

It was a disastrous day on the other side of the garage. George Russell started 2nd, but had a power unit deployment issue on the opening run to Turn 1 and sank down the order. He then made contact with Isack Hadjar in Turn 6 and received a ten-second penalty for his trouble. Russell salvaged a sixth-place finish. While the British driver didn't hesitate to congratulate Antonelli after they climbed out of their cars, it was clear he was devastated to lose the championship to his teammate. Russell skipped the mandatory post-race media interviews. In Russell's absence, Mercedes team principal Toto Wolff heaped praise on Antonelli and the team's patience with the sophomore champion, saying:

"People said he shouldn't even be in F1, he should be at a smaller team, 10 races and no points, the tears in Spa, and then this. I'm just so proud for the team, all of them continued to trust him and he delivered."

The tumult at Scuderia Ferrari reached a new low. Ferrari seemed poised to take the victory with Lewis Hamilton taking his first pole since 2023. With how difficult it is to pass in Mexico City, the seven-time champion just needed to survive the start's lengthy run to Turn 1. His biggest threat turned out to be his own teammate. Leclerc slipstreamed from third to Hamilton, then tried to pass on the outside. The Monégasque driver took another shot at the lead heading into Turn 4.

Neither Ferrari driver kept their car on the track through Turn 4. While Hamilton re-entered the racing surface to make Turn 5, Leclerc cut the course across the grass to take the lead. Ferrari would ask the pair to swap positions to avoid a penalty, but this time was different. Max Verstappen had gotten past Hamilton. Ferrari and Leclerc opted to put their fates in the stewards' hands rather than yield the lead by choice. The decision: a five-second time penalty.

The penalty was enough for Verstappen to inherit the lead after pit stops, but not enough for Hamilton to take second. The decision's light-handedness was something Hamilton noted after the race, adding that he received a ten-second penalty for a similar infraction last year. He was far less calm during the red flag, telling his team over the radio, "We've got big problems. Big, big problems." After Hamilton returned to pit lane, he stormed off the back of the garage

As bad as things are at Ferrari, neither driver has been fired. Haas abruptly benched Esteban Ocon on Saturday. The American team had reserve driver Ryo Hirakawa serve as a substitute for the rest of the weekend. Relationships between Ocon and Haas were already strained as the French driver had already been axed from the team's 2027 lineup. The benching was allegedly the result of a heated argument on Friday between Ocon and team boss Ayao Komatsu that became physical, according to French newspaper L'Equipe. Ocon took to social media to deny the report that he resorted to violence.

Following the recent decision from Haas F1 Team confirming that I will not be participating in this weekend's Mexico City Grand Prix, I wish to formally address some media reports and speculation surrounding the situation.

Last night, a difference in viewpoints led to a...

— Esteban Ocon (@OconEsteban) October 31, 2026

It's unclear who will be in the empty Haas seat next weekend in Brazil. Hirakawa has a respectable debut outing, finishing 13th on the track. The 32-year-old Le Mans winner was promoted to tenth, scoring a point, after other drivers received post-race penalties. Unfortunately, Hirakawa is unavailable for Interlagos as he's racing for his third World Endurance Championship with Toyota in Monza. Komatsu implied that Ocon could return. Antonelli may have clinched the championship and made history, but this season's drama is far from over.

#5727. Here's How Many Patients Fudge the Facts to Get Antibiotic Scripts

— Caregivers also reported misrepresenting kids' symptoms for antibiotics

More than one in four adults said they misrepresented symptoms to try to get antibiotics for themselves or their children, survey results showed.

Among 959 adults, 27.9% said they had misrepresented symptoms to their clinicians to get antibiotics for themselves, with 26% reporting that they had exaggerated their existing symptoms and 16.7% reporting that they had made up symptoms that didn't exist.

Notably, 76.9% of those who misrepresented symptoms at least once said they succeeded in getting antibiotics, noted Alistair Thorpe, PhD, of the University of Utah in Salt Lake City, and colleagues in JAMA Network Open.

Among 236 caregiver respondents, 26.7% reported that they had misrepresented a child's symptoms to get clinicians to give them an antibiotic: 24.2% said they had exaggerated a child's symptoms, 23.6% said they had fabricated symptoms, and 19.1% reported doing both. Of note, 87.3% said they'd successfully received antibiotics, reported Thorpe and colleagues in a separate research letter in JAMA Network Open.

"Together, these findings suggest concerning patient-clinician dynamics characterized by patients not feeling taken seriously, unclear and inconsistent messaging about antibiotics and antimicrobial resistance, conspiracy-like beliefs about antimicrobial resistance, and symptom misrepresentation to obtain antibiotics," Thorpe and team wrote.

These results "signal a significant issue for health professionals making antibiotic prescribing decisions and for the fields of antimicrobial stewardship and public health," they concluded.

Blame for unnecessary antibiotic prescriptions extends beyond dishonest patients, cautioned Jeffrey Linder, MD, MPH, of Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine in Chicago, in an accompanying commentary.

"It does not track that approximately 80% of respondents who reported misrepresenting their symptoms obtained a prescription unless prescribers were being willfully deceived or, more likely, just getting worn down with what they perceive as patient demand, regardless of actual clinical appropriateness," Linder wrote.

For diagnoses for which antibiotics aren't appropriate, "prescribing antibiotics is medically incorrect and could itself be considered a form of dishonesty or lying mainly to assuage the patient," he noted. Beyond the potential danger to patients and the rise of antimicrobial resistance, "the more concerning negative externality of inappropriate antibiotic prescribing is, like lying, an erosion of trust."

Prescribing antibiotics that clinicians know "are more likely to hurt than help ... is an affront to clinicians' professional responsibility to our patients," Linder concluded. "Clinicians' obligation is to act in patients' best interest, even if patients are hoping we do something different, even if patients do not like it, or even if patients lie about their symptoms."

Conducted in early 2026, the online survey asked U.S. adults if they had ever exaggerated or fabricated symptoms to obtain antibiotics. Respondents were also surveyed about their reasons for their decisions.

Among adults who misrepresented symptoms for themselves, cold-like symptoms were most often exaggerated or faked (65.7% of respondents), followed by stomach symptoms (49.3%), joint or muscle pain (46.3%), and nothing specific (36.9%). Of the eight symptom categories, only one -- urinary symptoms -- "clearly justified antibiotic prescribing," Linder noted.

Among adults, reasons for misrepresenting their own symptoms included:

Factors associated with higher odds of reporting symptom misrepresentation to obtain antibiotics included often feeling that clinicians do not take their symptoms seriously (OR 2.19, 95% CI 1.49-3.22), believing that antibiotic resistance is a hoax (OR 1.98, 95% CI 1.34-2.93), reporting more comorbidities (OR 1.16, 95% CI 1.05-1.28), having higher lying acceptability scores (OR 1.49, 95% CI 1.23-1.80), and needing help reading health materials (OR 2.40, 95% CI 1.62-3.54). In contrast, dislike of taking antibiotics (OR 0.89, 95% CI 0.81-0.99) and higher antibiotic knowledge (OR 0.73, 95% CI 0.58-0.91) were associated with lower odds of misrepresentation.

Few patients had regrets about bending the truth to get antibiotics. Among adults, fewer than 30% said they regretted their action, and those who did were more likely to do so because of adverse effects or ineffectiveness than concerns about dishonesty.

Among caregivers who misrepresented a child's symptoms, 63.5% who reported that they lied about symptoms said they did so because they felt clinicians didn't take their child's symptoms seriously. That group was significantly more likely to misrepresent symptoms (adjusted OR 3.34, 95% CI 1.44-7.99), as were caregivers whose child had comorbidities (aOR 1.24, 95% CI 1.02-1.54), those with higher lying acceptability scores (aOR 2.06, 95% CI 1.33-3.31), and those with lower health literacy (aOR 2.50, 95% CI 1.06-6.05).

Caregivers with higher antibiotic knowledge scores had lower odds of reporting symptom misrepresentation (aOR 0.55, 95% CI 0.31-0.93).

Study limitations included the small number of survey respondents, who may not represent the U.S. population. Inaccurate reporting also may have led to underreporting.

#5726. AI Balloon in China Deflates by Half a Trillion Dollars

There’s been a lot of talk this year about a humongous AI bubble. We’ve written about that many times here on CleanTechnica, but focused on the US market. AI software companies have massive valuations/market caps based on hype and growth in AI use but without demonstrating anything close to profitability or a clear plan for profitability. AI hardware company growth is dependent on continued software growth, of course. Well, if it’s a bubble or a balloon, it’s still fully blown up in the US, but there’s reporting now that the AI balloon has been deflating (a lot) in China.

“A report from Reuters suggests that the AI bubble in China ‘has already burst.’ Or it might be more apt to say that it shriveled like a rapidly deflating balloon,” Futurism writes. “Over the past four months, Reuters notes, the country’s STAR50 index of AI and semiconductor companies collapsed by 40 percent, wiping out $480 billion in market value. That is not a typo.”

It seems the same thing has been happening in China with AI hype as we’ve seen in the US, and there have thus also been people over there warning of it being a big bubble. “The stock market later evolved into a speculative crazy bull,” Dong Baozhen, a fund manager in Beijing, told Reuters. “And now we see the bubble bursting.”

Of course, the robotics craze and the AI craze have been going hand in hand for the past year or two. This reporting seems to link the two.

“Shares of Unitree, China’s buzzy robotics company, have halved since the company went public in a much-hyped IPO in August,” Futurism adds. “The chipmaker CXMT, meanwhile, has lost more than a quarter of its value over the past month.”

Is this just the beginning of a much more massively deflation or popping of AI bubbles? We will see.
