#5725. Which Automaker Has The Coolest Model Names?

Many car enthusiasts tend to view their car more as a family member than an appliance. Cars take us to some of the most important events in our lives, to some of the most memorable places in our lives, and hell, for a lot of us, they're the second place we ever see right after we're thrust into human existence and we leave the hospitals where we were born. To me, family members have names, not alphanumeric codes, so I've always preferred when automakers give their cars proper names.

Thankfully there have been scores of cool, memorable car names over the years, but we want to know which automaker you, dear Jalopnik reader, think has the coolest model names. There's no cutoff date for this one, mostly because I think a lot of the coolest model names have been discontinued or replaced with alphanumeric nonsense, so feel free to unearth some obscure carmaker that hasn't made a car in a century or something, as long as they had cool model names. Sound off in the comments section below!

In a rare change of pace, Lincoln went from using lame alphanumeric model names like MKZ and MKX to what I believe are the coolest model names in the industry. To clarify, the company started off with proper names for most of its models throughout the 20th century, but for some reason Lincoln decided to ditch those in the 2000s, which I lamented, even then as a child.

With current names like Corsair, Nautilus, Aviator, and Navigator, the modern lineup is stacked with heavy hitters, but Lincoln's classic names are really good, too. Of course there's also the classic Continental, and my beloved Zephyr. I don't know that I could honestly choose a favorite name from the brand — it would be a tie between Corsair, Nautilus, and Zephyr, which all evoke distinct pictures in my imagination.

Corsair makes me think of the Vought F4U Corsair warbird. Nautilus makes me think of the prehistoric undersea chambered mollusk that has barely evolved over the past couple million years, and Zephyr evokes images of Art Deco-era pie-in-the-sky inventions like early flying machines and trains. All of this imagery comes from the exciting and fascinating names given to Lincoln's cars, which is something that alphanumeric names just don't quite achieve in my brain. Okay, that's enough of my yapping, we're here for you! What automaker do you think has the coolest model names?

#5724. What Is a Great Google Quality Real Estate Score: A Comprehensive Guide

Navigating the world of Google Ads can be challenging, especially in an industry as competitive as real estate. One term you've probably heard in relation to Google Ads is "Quality Score." This score is crucial to how your ads perform and how much they cost. But what does it mean for realtors, and what constitutes a great Google Quality Real Estate Score? Let's dive in.

Google Ads Quality Score is essentially a measurement of the quality and relevance of both your keywords and PPC ads. It is a dynamic metric, assigned on a scale of 1 to 10, with 10 being the best possible score. It is used by Google to determine your cost per click (CPC) and your ad rank in the ad auction process.

Quality Score is calculated based on three primary components: click-through rate (CTR), ad relevance, and landing page experience.

For any industry, including real estate, a good Quality Score is generally around 7 or above. However, in the competitive world of real estate, striving for an even higher Quality Score could give you an advantage. So, for realtors, a great Quality Score might be considered 8 or above.

That said, keep in mind that a high-Quality Score is not an end in itself. It's a sign that your ads are relevant, and your landing pages provide a good user experience, but it's still just one factor in the overall success of your Google Ads campaigns.

A high-Quality Score is beneficial for several reasons:

Improving your Quality Score is all about relevance and user experience. Here are some strategies:

A high Google Ads Quality Score can be a strong asset for realtors. It not only helps you rank higher and pay less for clicks but also ensures your ads are shown to the most relevant audiences. However, remember that Quality Score is just one piece of the puzzle. You should also consider other factors like your bidding strategy and overall account performance to create a successful Google Ads campaign.

With continuous effort and strategic planning, you can optimize your Quality Score and ultimately generate more leads for your real estate business.

#5723. Cranberry Bogs Were Unprofitable, Then Solar Saved The Day

One of the things many people still don’t realize is how much solar power and wind power help farmers to be more profitable or to become profitable. Agriculture has never been the easiest industry, but it has been getting especially challenging to make ends meet in some parts of the world and with some types of farming. Extra income from solar farms and wind farms can make all the difference.

I don’t think I’ve ever learned anything about cranberry farming, but, apparently, they are grown in bogs in the northern parts of the US — Wisconsin, Massachusetts, and New Jersey are the three largest producers in the country, in that order, while Oregon and Washington also contribute to the market. We now have news of a couple of cranberry bogs in Massachusetts that were out of service because of financial collapse but have now been saved by solar.

“Distributed Energy Infrastructure (DEI), a leading EPC provider for utility-scale solar and storage projects, announces the harvest of two groundbreaking cranberry agrivoltaics projects: the Black Cat Agrivoltaics Project in Plymouth, Massachusetts, and the Ring Road Agrivoltaics Project in Kingston, Massachusetts. Together, these projects have produced the first-ever agrivoltaic cranberry harvests — a milestone for expanding agrivoltaic applications into challenging wetland environments,” DEI shares.

“The projects are owned by developer Greenbacker Renewables were installed by Distributed. Cranberry farmer Iain Ward, who manages the bogs, was closely involved throughout design and construction to ensure that the sensitive wetland environment remained intact.”

“Building solar projects over active perennial wetland crops was an exciting challenge, and we’re thrilled to see the success of these harvests,” says Prateek Tare, Executive Vice President and Co-founder of DEI. “Iain’s collaboration and input at every stage was essential for us to ensure a reliable, cost-effective build while safeguarding the productivity of these heritage bogs.”

The Black Cat and Ring Road cranberry bogs had been out of service for years because they were no longer financially viable. However, the solar projects brought them back to life! “The solar projects now provide the landowner with additional income through land leasing, making the bogs’ cultivation economically viable again. Iain Warde is paid for agricultural management, in addition to his income from selling the cranberry crops.”

To close, here are some details of the project:

“Both projects were finalists for the North American Agrivoltaics Awards, and both Ward and DEI are founding members of the Solar & Farming Association (SAFA). SAFA is a coalition of organizations representing the interests of the agrivoltaics industry, including developers, agricultural producers, livestock producers, and affiliated organizations.”

Agrivoltaics wins again. Farming and solar power is one of the best combos we’ve seen since someone decided to spread peanut butter and jelly together inside some slices of bread.

#5722. These Are The Most Haunted Cars You've Ever Bought

Halloween has come and gone. You might have a bucket full of candy you're trying to keep your kids from binging in an hour. If we're being completely honest, you might be considering candy as your own dinner tonight. While most haunted houses will shutter until next, there's a year-round show sitting in your garage just waiting for another surprise performance when you're sitting behind the wheel.

We asked our readers last week about the most haunted cars they've ever bought. Responders filled the comments section with technical issues that I've honestly never seen before, like pop-up headlights that would erratically go up and down only in the rain. Speaking of water, there was even a boat that seemed to want to destroy itself out on the water. These are the kinds of problems that would haunt anyone's dreams. Without further ado, here are the vehicles most likely to have a connection to the supernatural:

My 2017 Chevrolet Volt was pretty much one thing after the other. AC only blew cold whenever it wanted to. Nagging "shift to park" messages even though I already put the car in park. A misfire. But the worst was when the car suddenly stopped and wouldn't move again, leaving me stranded in a parking garage at midnight. When I got it towed to the dealer, they traced it to a panel of wires that mysteriously burnt. The employees literally told me they had "never seen that in their life". The car was in the shop for three months because the dealer also said GM doesn't make the problem part anymore so they had to build one from scratch. I decided I was done with the car after that and got rid of it.

More cursed than haunted. My former car was a 2020 S560. Amazing car. Loved it. But I got hit in 3 separate minor accidents in 15 months. Never my fault – one while parked for gods sake. Each time requiring all the effort of insurance adjusters, body shops, etc. Prior to that car, I had been in one accident in about 32 years of driving (also not my fault). Traded for a 2025 S580e and love it even more – hopefully the curse didn't transfer.

Ps. How much you think a S-class with 3 accidents on the carfax is worth? Not much is correct :(

I owned an '88 Prelude Si back in college (think 1997) had been in a collision somewhere near the fusebox, and when it rained the popup headlights would alternate up and down (one up, one down, back and forth). I got really good at jumping out in the rain and pulling the exact fuse necessary to stop it, and then twist the headlights into the "up" position. I could have just left them up all the time, but the car looked SO much better with them down.

I had a real Christine situation with my first car.

My dad and I rebuilt a 1960 Chevy El Camino for me to drive. I loved that car and was directly involved in everything we did to get it back on the road. Installing a new engine, replacing the clutch, sculpting the bondo to cover all the rust, and laying down the Corvette red paint.

She never let me down. Always got me where I was going.

My girlfriend HATED that car. No AC, loud, and I spent probably as much time and money on it as I did her.

The car hated her right back. The starter would occasionally require a jump across the poles with a screwdriver to work, and it always seemed to be when she was with me.

Like I said, it didn't have AC, and I lived in West Texas then. It did have kick panel vents you could open for some relief, and one time, my girlfriend grabbed the knob to pull it open on her side. Somehow, and I don't even know how it was possible, she got, I dunno, pinched or something in a way THAT DREW BLOOD.

Not a car, but how about a haunted, murderous boat story?

I had a sport fisherman. An evil one. The first bad day was when the twin outboards both died while crossing the intercoastal waterway, with tankers coming at me from both directions. Since both outboards were fed by separate gas tanks and has separate electrical systems, this shouldn't be possible, but it there I was. The next month it caught fire, but, unfortunately, I reacted and put it out. I never knew how, but drain plug could pop out all on its own, on the ocean once and at dock. Another time, while trying to assess some damage to the bottom gel coat, I climbed under the boat resting on its trailer. I absolutely did not touch the trailer or the boat, but the boat somehow knew anyway and made the trailer jack suddenly collapse, pinning me. Finally, I was trying to fix one of the outboard lower units. I was hanging backward off the transom by my legs, body wedged between the two motors. The other engine then popped loose its gas line which proceeded to douse my face with gasoline. I couldn't see, breathe and the gas filled both nostrils. I managed to extricate myself but later discovered the gas had burned away my sense of smell for the next few months. That's how I learned adult have stem cells in their nose – the "nose buds" regrow eventually.

Today, when I hear an unexpected knock at the door, I sometimes fear the boat has found me and that I'll open the door to two screaming propellers coming toward me....

2011 Dodge Avenger SXT. Bought it used for my daughter. It wasn't pretty, but it ran great on a LONG test ride. She takes it to work the next day and she calls me that it's out in the parking lot with all the lights blinking and horn blaring away. I figured it was the alarm but it wasn't. Put a new battery in it that day (nightmare) and all seems fine. A few weeks later the battery light comes on and I test the alternator which is bad. I replaced it and good for a few more weeks. She comes home one day and tells me the horn starts blaring randomly along with the hazards. I couldn't find anything obvious so I drop it off at my mechanic and he changes whatever Dodge calls its fuse box. Runs great for a few more weeks and then it just starts randomly shutting off but restarts after sitting for a few minutes. I just gave up and sold it dirt cheap but told the buyer about all the issues. He didn't care because of the price. He actually called me back complaining about the car and how I ripped him off even though I explained in detail what a POS the car was. He backed down and admitted that he thought he could fix it himself. Absolutely the worst car I have ever owned. Every time my daughter and I see an Avenger we laugh.

When I first bought my 1982 Mercedes 300td about four years ago, it seemed haunted by mysterious mechanical failures, the oil pressure gauge started shooting oil all over the cabin of the car, and I had to replace so many stubborn parts that refused to be removed without drawing blood. Some may say that these are the normal consequences of buying a poorly maintained 40-year-old car, but I think we all know the real answer is ghosts.

Not a car, but anyone who grew up in Chicago knows about Resurrection Mary, the ghostly hitchhiker who wandered Archer Ave looking for a ride home. A favorite thing to do around Halloween was to try and find her... 😳

My 2003 Dodge Ram Van 1500. It has reliably run poorly the whole time I've owned it. I got it with supposedly 60,000 miles about 8 years ago and it ran fine, but by the time I got around to taking it to emissions, the CEL came on. The wife was supposed to take it across the country pretty quickly after we got it and on that trip she learned that it eats oil and coolant. I figured out most of the oil consumption by replacing a manifold gasket. The coolant still goes somewhere, but I never see a drop on the ground. I fixed the CEL by replacing the alternator, but it still comes back on periodically. It's at 140,000 miles now and still does what it needs to do, but it's always unhappy about it.

My Miata wasn't haunted when I bought it... But driving home on a dark and stormy night I hit a cat. The next time I went to shift gears I heard a "meow" when I pressed the clutch pedal. I stopped and looked under the car – nothing! The clutch kept meowing for about 10 years. Then it stopped – I guess the cat's ghost got bored.

When I got out of Navy boot camp, I bought a 1969 Volvo 144 up in the Chicago area for $300. To put it mildly, it needed work to pass inspection. It had a damaged headlight assembly so to aim it, we used window caulking and glued it in place. The automatic column shift lever bracket broke and to put it in gear it took two hands to wrestle it into drive. The driver's side window was held in place with a notched broom handle or it sat in the bottom of the door. It had a barely functional radio. It drove well for me for about a year and I slowly fixed all the issues. Used replacement fender, core support, headlight bucket, steering column, window regulator and handle, I even painted it in the base hobby shop back to its original red (first paint job I ever did). No sooner than I got it road worthy-ish, on the way out of the Orlando navy base, it decided to destroy itself internally by dropping the guts out of the torque converter and blow transmission fluid on the road next to a gas station. It chugged its way into a parking spot next to the tow truck as if gasping its last breaths and then all by itself thew the transmission into park and blew parts everywhere then threw a rod through the engine block as if to say "I'M DONE! NO MORE TO GIVE!" The hand of the Swedish car gods took any spirit it had left. I signed the title, put it under the wiper, grabbed my stuff, and walked away. I saw it parked it next to the donor car when looking for parts for my next $300 car.

My mom's 1998 Mustang GT convertible with the manual (she's awesome!). It's had an electrical gremlin the entire time she's owned it. (20+ years) No one has ever been able to isolate the specific issue. The issue has prevented the convertible top from going up/down, headlights wouldn't turn on, radio issues, cluster issues. Only one issue at a time. She brings it to the shop, they can't isolate the issue. Then, sometime later, the issue goes away. She's even managed to drive it to the shop when these issues have been actively happening and the techs still couldn't find a reason. Battery has been replaced, grounds checked, alternator replaced. Every few years it's something. It sounds bad, but 99% of the time it runs without issue.

#5721. AI, Digital Pathology in Early Breast Cancer Among Highlights at SABCS

— Virginia Kaklamani also previews studies on antibody-drug conjugates, endocrine therapy, and more

The use of artificial intelligence (AI) and digital pathology in early breast cancer will be a major theme at the upcoming San Antonio Breast Cancer Symposium (SABCS), with new research exploring whether image-based tools can help determine which patients need chemotherapy or extended endocrine therapy.

In this exclusive MedPage Today video, Virginia Kaklamani, MD, DSc, of the University of Texas at San Antonio Health Science Center, who is co-director of SABCS, previews some of the early breast cancer research to watch at this year's meeting, including studies on novel antibody-drug conjugates and endocrine therapy, genomic studies, and a debate on the use of trastuzumab deruxtecan (T-DXd; Enhertu) in the neoadjuvant versus adjuvant setting for HER2-positive disease.

Following is a transcript of her remarks:

Hello, my name is Virginia Kaklamani from UT Health San Antonio, and I'm one of the co-directors of the San Antonio Breast Cancer Symposium.

So for this year's symposium, our 49th year, we're going to have a lot of really interesting abstracts, especially in the early-stage setting, looking at AI. So looking at digital pathology and how this digital pathology can actually help us determine who needs chemotherapy and who doesn't need chemotherapy, who needs extended endocrine therapy, and so forth. This is really a new era where we have a lot of tools available so that instead of taking a specimen, sending it to a laboratory thousands of miles away, and analyzing it, now with digital pathology, all we need to do is just take a picture and send that picture over and then that picture gets analyzed, and really the information that we get may actually be as good, if not better, than the information we get from doing genomic assays on specific tumor specimens.

So this is going to help, I think, in the future because it'll allow us to do these tests quicker and also cheaper. And we can do them from anywhere in the world, whereas right now these laboratories sometimes in certain countries have specific regulations.

So I think this is kind of the biggest news out of SABCS this year, but there's so many other things. We have new novel antibody-drug conjugates. We're going to be looking at endocrine therapy in the early-stage setting and so forth, and a lot of other genomic presentations that'll really help us treat our patients better.

This year we expect more than 11,000 people to come to San Antonio. We always have many medical oncologists, surgical oncologists, radiation oncologists, advocates. I think the strength of SABCS is the presence of our patient advocates and the inclusion of our patient advocates through the program and the different sessions that we have.

We have several special sessions that are looking at lobular carcinoma, looking at obesity and breast cancer, GLP-1 agonists, really the talk of the past -- what, 5, 10 years -- and how GLP-1s can help improve health in our patients with breast cancer, potentially also improve outcomes in our breast cancer patients.

So a lot of things happening in the symposium, from educational sessions, state-of-the-art sessions, debate. The big debate this year is going to be whether to give T-DXd in the neoadjuvant or adjuvant setting in patients with early-stage HER2-positive disease. So the 5 days that the meeting is going on is really going to be crazy, but it's going to be a lot of fun data to discuss.

#5720. Westward Ho! First Trip Cross Country with Our New 2026 Tesla Model Y & Full Self Driving V14

We were headed west again for the 6th time, driving the ~1500 miles from our lake house in Northern Wisconsin to our winter home near the grandchildren in Northern Utah. We had totaled our 2019 Tesla Model 3 in an accident in May; therefore, this was our first road trip in our new 2026 Model Y running Full Self Driving V14. As usual, we were carrying two big ebikes on the back. We plan to pause in Utah at our home for a few days and then on to Portland, Oregon, and the Pacific Ocean.

The experience was quite different this time because of the new car and the significant upgrade to Tesla’s driving automation: Full Self Driving Supervised V14.3.12.

As we approached our first Supercharger in Wausau, Wisconsin, it looked like the navigation was taking us the wrong way. However, once we got to our normal exit, it was closed. We had to drive a few miles beyond it, do a U-turn, and come back. It cost us roughly an extra 5 miles. I’m going to have to learn to trust the navigation. The Nav knows about road closures like this and even traffic jams. Unfortunately, it doesn’t explain ahead of time why it is suggesting an alternate route.

Our first stop was at the Wausau, Wisconsin Supercharger next to the Hilton Gardens Inn on highway NN. In 7 years, we’ve never seen more than a few cars charging there. For the first time, the charging station was full (see photo below). It was Sunday after a big fall-colors week in Northern Wisconsin and many tourists were headed home. On our screen, it gave a charger wait time of five minutes. As we drove the last 100 miles to Wausau, our computer screen alternated between showing an unspecified wait time and as many as two stalls open.

When we arrived, it listed two stalls as open, but, in fact, for nearly the first time across the last seven years that we’ve been Supercharging, all eight stalls were full. However, here’s the good part: within less than five minutes, one of the cars pulled out and we were able to back into the empty stall. Then, the bad part: it was the slowest charge we’ve ever experienced. It was an older V2 hardware Supercharger where you need to share charging with the adjacent car. That meant that we were charging at only a 50-kilowatt speed for the whole charge. The total charge took about 40 minutes. However, after the bathroom break and walking our small rescue dog, Zuni, we only needed to kill about 15 minutes before setting off again.

The next stop was Menomonee, Wisconsin, halfway to Minneapolis and the charger was showing three empty stalls. Since, as I mentioned, we were traveling on a Sunday, a beautiful day, with full fall colors in the Northwoods, we found another Supercharger that appeared to be full (see below), but there was one stall empty and we pulled in. When we came east in mid-May, there were seldom more than one or two cars in any of the 12 Superchargers en route.

The Menomonee Supercharger has a newer Hardware 4 charger, so we were charging faster, at almost 100 kW during our whole charge. As we pulled out, there were about three cars waiting, but the line moved quickly. No one had to wait more than a few minutes. Then we were on our way to Minneapolis.

About 20 miles east of Minneapolis, crossing the Saint Croix River on the border between Wisconsin and Minnesota, half the lanes of the bridge were shut down. The navigation took us on a town of Henderson detour going on highways U and UU, then back onto the I-90 route across the bridge. It probably saved time avoiding the worst of the traffic backup because of the closure on the bridge. Then we were on an east–west route right through the center of Saint Paul–Minneapolis. Full Self Driving V14 with Tesla navigation found its way through the big city freeways perfectly.

As we left the Minneapolis Supercharger, we couldn’t get Full Self Driving to work! It said “FSD Temporarily Unavailable.” However, temporarily lasted for a very long time. We went through a long troubleshooting process with Ara (the Grok bot) and with Tesla Customer Service on the phone. Not only did FSD fail to work, but it was reporting a speed limit of 15 mph when the actual speed limit was 65 mph. First, we stopped, put the car in park, and did the two-scroll button reboot. No luck. Then we were instructed to use the menu to reboot the car. No luck. Then we tried to recalibrate the cameras as suggested, but it was nighttime and the calibration wouldn’t finish. Not only was FSD not working, but smart cruise control also failed to operate. However, after partial re-calibration of the cameras, it did restore smart cruise control.

On day two of our cross-country trip, we left Worthington in the southwest corner of Minnesota on I-90, passing through a huge wind turbine field (see below) with hundreds of turbines on the north side of the road just as we got into South Dakota.

A short time later, we saw a wind turbine tower factory with hundreds of turbine tower sections laid down as you see in the photo.

As we began our drive through South Dakota we had smart cruise control, but no FSD.

The first Supercharger stop in South Dakota was at Murdo. I went into the pharmacy in the adjacent supermarket and bought some medical tape and taped over the back camera. I did this because we found that, with our older car, strangely, taping over the back camera was the only way to get full FSD to work with bikes on the back.

We restarted the camera celebration, which had failed the night before in the dark. This time we observed the little gray steering wheel icon in the upper-left-hand corner of the screen. A blue circle starts at the bottom around the icon, and it progresses all the way around to indicate that the calibration is in progress. However, when it was almost complete, it started over at the halfway point. It oscillated back and forth a few times for almost an hour before it finally completed the calibration and we got FSD back! We had lost FSD for the better part of two days of our 1500-mile trip. Since we pay $99/month for FSD, will Tesla give us a refund?????

Our stop in the middle of South Dakota was at the Supercharger in Wall, home of the famous Wall Drugstore. See my wife and our Tesla with the bikes at the Supercharger in the photo below.

We charged in Wall, South Dakota, and drove another 80 miles to the wildlife loop at Custer State Park. There, “we ran with the bulls” — I mean, we ran with the bison (buffalo). We were driving right in the middle of a big bison herd, as you see in the photos below.

My wife spotted hundreds of pronghorns on our trip west, and the visit to Custer State Park would not be complete without seeing one, as well as feeding and petting the donkeys.

We made it by 8:00 pm to the tiny town of Lusk, Wyoming, where a four-stall Supercharger is across the street from the Pioneer Best Western where we spent the night. The next morning, we were in eastern Wyoming, where we were following double railroad tracks used to ship coal from the huge, high-quality, low-sulfur coal deposits in northeast Wyoming to power plants farther east.

“Coal Area — The Powder River Basin spans over 1,000 square miles, making it one of the largest coal-producing regions in the US,” Google shares. “Depth of Coal Beds — Coal beds in the region can be as thick as 250 feet, with only 120 feet of soil overburden.”

The photos below show one of the unit coal trains bringing coal from the Powder River Basin as we passed. Clean power is on the rise, but coal is still an important part of electrical generation in the United States.

Powder Basin coal is also going to the Dave Johnson power plant in eastern Wyoming that we passed. It is an 817 MW plant that was scheduled to shut down next year (2027). However, the Trump administration has “rescued” it and it won’t shut down or be converted to gas for at least a couple more years.

On the third day of our trip, FSD was working and we stopped to charge at Evansville near Casper, Wyoming. Then we passed Devils Gap, Independence Rock, and the pioneer handcart company memorial on our way to Rawlins. At Rock Springs, I left my wife at the TJ Max while I charged at the Tesla Supercharger 100 yards away. The final charger was in Evanston, Wyoming, and then we crossed the Utah border and passed Park City, Heber, and drove down Provo Canyon to our winter home in Lindon just north of Provo (30 miles south of Salt Lake City). It took three 500-mile days to make the trip.

Summary: Our new 2026 Tesla Model Y seemed to charge a little slower than our old Tesla Model 3 Long Range. However, it appeared to have longer battery range. This meant that we could often travel as fast as 80 mph, whereas previously we never exceeded 75 mph and occasionally had to slow down as low as 60 mph to make the next charger — in our old Model 3 with the bikes on the back. FSD was a pleasure, keeping the car precisely in the middle of the lane, passing cars, and returning to the right-hand lane just as I would do myself … except when FSD failed to work for a big part of two days during the trip. I’m 86, and it was a strain keeping the car centered in the lane and keeping a foot on the accelerator when FSD wasn’t working.

If you find any of my articles helpful to you, please use my referral link when buying a new Tesla: https://ts.la/arthur73734 (Be sure to use it when you make your order). Tesla keeps changing the program, but here’s the latest: If you are buying a new Tesla Model 3 or Y and use my link, you will get two months of free Full Self Driving (a ~$200 value) on top of the one month that comes free with every new Model Y right now. This will get you a total of 3 months free Full Self Driving. It will be $99/month after that. For a Cybertruck, you will get $1000 off your purchase price. At this moment, Tesla is offering a year of free Supercharging with the purchase of a new Model 3 Premium or Performance.

Also: Research Meteorologist (Emeritus) at NASA GSFC, Adjunct Professor at Viterbo University On-Line Studies, PSIA L2 Certified Alpine Ski Instructor at Brighton Utah Ski School.

#5719. Off-Road Mazda Miata, De-Bodied Honda Civic Si, Jawa Sandcrawler: The Dopest Cars I Found For Sale Online

You made it! Happy, uh, whenever this runs. I've lost track at this point. Friday? Saturday? Tuesday? Either way, you made it through and for that I'm proud of you. Give yourself a little round of applause. The years start coming and they truly don't stop coming, and despite high gas prices and the looming economic collapse that threatens to cast us all out onto the streets, homeless and starving and desperate as AI CEOs rail ketamine on their yachts, you're still here. Despite everything, it's still you.

So anyway, how about them cars? May as well get one now while you still can. Party like it's 1999, in a sense — not with the optimism of a new millennium to come, riding a bull market into infinity, but with the terror of a looming technological apocalypse and an economic bubble on your mind. Plenty of people bought Supras before Y2K and the Dot Com crash. You can get something fun now, while the U.S. dollar is still a piece of currency that people accept. Here are this week's Dopest Cars.

The seller describes this Miata as being "one of one," but let's be honest — we've all seen offroad Miatas before. A jacked-up NB with a Hard Dog roll bar and all-terrain tires wrapped around 15-inch wheels is nothing new. I would argue, though, on the side of the seller. There are two things that make this particular offroad Miata neat. First, it seems to be genuinely well-built, with an eye for usability rather than getting a job done in the most comical manner for hte least possible money. Second, it seems to actually get used.

There are shots of this Miata in the dirt that don't look like the fire road photoshoots that every other dirt Miata gets. The seller claims to have put 40,000 miles on the car since it was lifted, so the build is well tested — if anything were going to go wrong with its design, its suspension setup, or its weight balance, it likely would have by now.

Facebook Marketplace sellers, I am begging you to take better photos. You see how much I had to rotate this one to make it halfway usable as a slide image. This sucks! Every photo is at some absurd angle, and they all hide the true gem: This club-style Dyna.

I have little love for Harley-Davidson as a brand, but even I have to admit that the club style Dyna just hits. I watched "Sons of Anarchy" at a formative age, and some part of my brain still yearns for this kind of fairing and seat. I don't think I'd want this kind of satin paint job, especially not mixed with the high gloss of the fairing, but maybe you do. Maybe that's your thing. Listen, I'm not here to judge, just to educate you that this thing will show every smudge or speck of dirt forever. Though maybe, as a propsective Harley owner, you don't care.

See, now this is a photo taken by someone who wants their vehicle to sell. Not to go all "Wes Anderson is when something is in the middle of the frame" here, but there's something Wes Andersony going on here. The warm tones of the car against the cool yet vibrant water and sky behind, the comical proportions of the vehicle. If anything, the just-off-center framing is the least Wes Anderson thing here.

The seller of this Vanagon seems eager to get it off their hands, having just reduced the price by more than half — from $12,000 down to $5,500. They say it'll need "some repairs," with frustratingly little detail on what that might mean beyond California DMV paperwork and smog testing, but if that's the extent of the issues then anyone from any other state can get a half-price camper easily. Go take it to the Grand Budapest Hotel or something.

You thought I'd let the Halloween-ish Dopest go by without a spooky skeleton? This Civic Si coupe has been stripped down to bare metal and further, an incredibly in-depth process that requires comprehensive fabrication and modification knowledge. If you're hoping to find any of that in the description, though, you won't. The ad text, in full:

2007 Honda civic si made it into a EXO Car six speed manual with a new clutch kit and coil over suspension with new wheels

Hey, seller, quick question: Can you give any amount of detail on anything happening here? What kind of parts are the clutch, coilovers, and wheels? What're those seats? Are the harnesses in date? Why is there only one windshield wiper? This isn't some fungible stock Corolla, this is an incredibly heavily modified vehicle. You're going to need to give us some details here.

I love these anniversary liveries, with the yellow and black and the little dashes. I'm not sure that paint meshes harmoniously with the shape of the tank on this first-generation XSR900, but c'est la vie — the second generation, which I think would wear this paint better, hadn't yet been designed.

What the first generation lacks in style over its younger brother, though, it more than makes up for in outright rowdiness. This is a bike that CP3 riders said was too rowdy — the same kinds of riders who spend their days at 12 o' clock on their MT09s considered this bike's wheelbase too short and its roll-on power too abrupt. This bike wants nothing more than to have fun, to buck and wind out and split through lanes, and the yellow color just makes it all the more lively. I want one of these so badly. Buy one in my stead.

Yee and, I cannot stress this enough, haw. This is the platonic ideal of a truck, and all others fail to measure up to this C20's level of quietly confident, unassuming utility. Maybe the Slate will get there once it's released and (eventually) considered to be normal, if the company manages to sell more than six of them before going under. Personally, I can't wait to get a Slate from the Chapter 11 auction when the company eats it.

Even the Slate, though, stands in the shadow of this C20. Simple round headlights, a simple square grille, a hood that covers the engine and a roof that covers your head. None of it is so painstakingly designed as every modern truck, no crease or angle on the C20 exists purely to project the image that its driver is a Rugged Flannel Man who Does Labor and Wears Carhartt But Not In An American Spirits Way.

You want to know what's worse than bad pictures of a car? No pictures of a car. This is the only photo in this ad, plus two screenshots of a notes app parts list. You're selling a non-running Mitsubishi Eclipse, and you can't even put in the effort to show us the condition of the body? What if there isn't one, like that Civic up earlier? How would we know?

Despite my griping about the ad, I do still like this car. I'm a sucker for this generation of the Eclipse, and this one claims to be totally rust free. Sure, we have to take the seller's word for it, but still. A shell that hasn't seen a thousand New England winters is worth an engine that doesn't work, I think. One of those is a lot easier to fix than the other, and you can probably just rip a block out of a rusted-out Eclipse and toss it in here.

This one's for Andy, who's always bragging about how he doesn't have to sell things to maintain his lavish lifestyle of owning multiple watches. Oh yeah, Andy? You're financially stable enough to have gone to an off-off-Broadway show the other day? Buy an NSX about it. I know you want one.

This seller didn't include a year in the ad, but I don't care. Much like the C20 we had before, this is simply a good truck. Does it matter what year it is? Does the mileage matter? You can get those things off the title when you buy it. All that matters is that this Hardbody runs, stops, and can haul your stuff around.

Honestly, what more could you really need? I wish the wheels were a little different — give me steelies on a truck like this, really — but anything else is gravy. This is just a good ol' truck for doing good ol' work, and I love it.

Yeah, I... I'm not sure either. The seller says this sandcrawler could be for Burning Man, or to live in. Good luck.

#5718. “Everything Trump Touches Dies” — A Warning For America

In August 2018, Rick Wilson, a long-time GOP media consultant, published a book entitled Everything Trump Touches Dies. In it, he argued that MAGA and Trump will ruin the reputations, careers, and stability of the people and institutions that choose to align with him. Recently, Paul Krugman and Heather Cox Richardson published separate posts on Substack that bring Wilson’s warning forward to the present.

Krugman based his article on the writings of Robert Pape, author of the Substack Escalation Trap, who argues that, as the loss of control of the House and possibly the Senate becomes more likely, it is making Trump more dangerous than ever. Krugman wrote:

“It’s a well-understood proposition that the owners of businesses that are on the verge of going bust often engage in destructive behavior. And Trump is about to go bankrupt — not financially but politically. So he, like a failing businessman, has strong incentives to wreak havoc.

“Pape’s argument is basically a version of the phenomenon often called ‘gambling for resurrection.’ When a company is on the edge of failure, it makes sense for the owner to make highly speculative, even crazy investments that are highly likely to fail but might, just might, make enough money to pull the company back from the brink. After all, for the owner it’s heads he wins, tails his creditors lose.

“In Trump’s case, all indications are that his party is heading for defeat in the midterms. And while a normal president might be able to live with a Congress controlled by the opposing party, there has been so much corruption and malfeasance over the past two years that Democratic control of Congress, and the subpoena and investigative power that will bring, will have catastrophic consequences for Trump and everyone around him.

“So Trump has an incentive to do something — anything — that might shake up this election. Attacking Iran would probably lead to an even bigger Republican defeat, but it might, just might, rally Trump’s base enough to change the outcome. True, a president who cared at all about what is good for America wouldn’t pursue disastrous policies for the slim chance of saving his own political skin. But this is, after all, Trump we’re talking about.”

According to Richardson, the Wall Street Journal has reported that Vladimir Putin has proposed a meeting next month with Trump and China President Xi Jinping to decide the fate of Ukraine.

The report explained that “a three-way summit with China and the U.S. would cement Russia’s status as one of the world’s three great powers, a status Putin has sought since 2000 when he entered the Kremlin. Such a summit would formally acknowledge that the U.S. had broken from Europe and allied with Russia, a goal for Putin as he tries to take control of Ukraine.”

Richardson wrote, “After ten years of Trump insisting that reports of his cooperation with Russia were a hoax, here we are, watching one profoundly unpopular man tear apart the global system under which we have lived for eighty years in order to climb into bed with Vladimir Putin.”

Writing in The Atlantic, national security scholar Tom Nichols said, “The American commander in chief has abandoned the Western order, the most successful — and powerful — security arrangement in human history.” Richardson observed:

“This decision appears to have had nothing to do with the national interest of the United States…. Instead, Trump appears to have thrown out the world order because of his own personal interests, his admiration for Putin, his resentment of Ukraine’s president Volodymyr Zelensky, and quite possibly other factors that so far have remained obscured from the American public.

“Outrage over Trump’s actions has come from across the political spectrum,” she wrote. “Nichols notes that former federal prosecutor turned conservative commenter Andrew McCarthy wrote that Trump is giving Putin ‘aid and comfort.’ Those words echo Article III, Section 3 of the Constitution, which defines treason against the United States, as ‘levying War against them, or in adhering to their Enemies, giving them Aid and Comfort.’

“McCarthy’s blistering article in the National Review on Saturday reminds readers that Putin is a murderous dictator who is actively supporting Iran’s military operations against the United States with both intelligence and drones and provoking U.S. allies in the North Atlantic Treaty Organization. And yet Trump is siding with him against Ukraine, a U.S. ally. McCarthy demanded Congress hold ‘immediate hearings about this week’s shocking escalation of the Trump-Putin alliance.’”

Krugman warned that a cornered Trump may resort to what he calls “policy looting” by “using his remaining power to take actions that are both deeply unpatriotic and deeply unpopular, but fit his own preferences and prejudices. With nothing left to lose, he will be free to be his worst self — a malignant narcissist who instinctively supports authoritarians and is preoccupied with revenge against anything and anyone that threatens his fragile grandiosity.” In other words, a raging lunatic with access to the nuclear codes.

“Anyone who believes Trump will become more cautious, more moderate in the aftermath of midterm defeat just doesn’t understand the new political reality. The truth is that political bankruptcy will free Trump to pursue his true desires. And those desires are horrifying,” he wrote.

Left to his own devices, Trump is an existential threat to the United States that came into being in 1776. Don’t overlook the fact that the US Supreme Court has enabled and emboldened him, based on a legal theory known as “the unitary executive,” a creation of conservative groups like the Federalist Society that has no basis in the Constitution.

Richardson concludes her essay with this observation:

“The Framers of the Constitution thought they had designed a system that would prevent the rise of a tyrant. They believed U.S. representatives would be so fearful of a leader’s abuse of power that they would impeach any president who overstepped his authority, and that U.S. senators would be so jealous of their own power they would convict, and tyranny would not be able to take root.

“The Framers especially did not anticipate that senators, to whom they deliberately gave longer terms than they gave the president, would put party over country and stand aside as a president and his appointees captured the prerogatives of the Senate.

“But even given the evidence of the past ten years, it is truly astonishing to see Republicans, who for more than forty years have championed President Ronald Reagan for his stance against the Soviet Union, stand aside as Trump trashes the post–World War II system of global security in order to ally the United States with a Russian dictator.”

In the end, the people who fashioned the Constitution simply failed to anticipate the overwhelmingly corrosive influence of power, nor did they provide a way to rein in a madman. RIP, America. The world may never see the likes of you again.

Paul Krugman likes to end his Substack posts with a song. Here’s one that seems pertinent, given the news reports that the US has warned the Gulf nations to expect renewed attacks on Iran shortly after the midterm elections.

Steve writes about the interface between technology and sustainability from his home in Florida or anywhere else The Force may lead him. He is proud to be "woke" and believes weak leaders push others down while strong leaders lift others up. #5717. Read Jim Farley's Lips: No Boring Fords

Happy Monday! It's November 2, 2026, and this is The Morning Shift — your daily roundup of the top automotive headlines from around the world, in one place. This is where you'll find the most important stories that are shaping the way Americans drive and get around.

In this morning's edition we're looking at Ford's desire to make its lineup more interesting, and the effects of our Canadian trade war on Michigan's election. We'll also look at companies getting ready for a midterm rout, and BMW's plans for autonomy.

If you'd like a recap of the latest auto news sent to your inbox each weekday, you can sign up for Jalopnik's free Morning Shift newsletter here.

Ford wants its sales back to pre-pandemic levels, and it has a new approach that CEO Jim Farley hopes will get the company there: Make cars that are actually interesting. Wild concept, I know, but perhaps it is just crazy enough to work. From the Wall Street Journal:

Ford Motor expects to sell 5 million cars a year by early next decade, Chief Executive Jim Farley told workers this week, a sales volume the company hasn't reached since before the pandemic.

The company's global strategy turns on Farley's emphasis to no longer make "boring" vehicles, which for now has left it without the budget-friendly small sedans and SUVs it used to give priority to. Farley has said that making affordable vehicles remains a priority, however, and Ford is planning to launch five new models priced below $40,000 by the end of the decade, including an all-electric pickup truck.

Ford once decided to kill off nearly all its interesting cars in favor of making crossover slop, so this will be a reversal of that. Of course, the crossover slop got Ford to the sales numbers it's now trying to return to, so who knows what's best.

Michigan's race for Senate is a closely-watched one, and it's made headlines recently as (former?) Democrat Haley Stevens turned away from supporting her (former?) party-mate Abdul El-Sayed to go back Republican Mike Rogers. But within Michigan, there's another factor playing in voters' minds: The trade war with Canada. From Automotive News:

The Michigan seat is crucial for Democrats to get a Senate majority. It's also a race where Trump's trade war with Canada has become a ballot box issue.

The economies of Michigan and Canada have long been integrated and Trump's tariffs have had a significant impact on the state. The auto industry on both sides of the border has been struggling with the 25 per cent auto tariffs for vehicles finished in Canada — which Trump has threatened to increase to 50 per cent next year.

The Detroit Regional Chamber has repeatedly said the tariff battle causes economic pain on both sides of the border. Multiple polls have shown most Michiganders — no matter their political affiliation — disapprove of Trump's tariff attack on Canada.

Midterm elections are often a judgment on the ongoing administration, and this midterm could shape up to be the biggest blue wave in recent memory. Pocketbook issues like the trade war with Canada are a major part of that, as people regardless of political inclination feel the economic sting of policy.

Corporations tend to invest heavily in U.S. elections, hoping to buy candidates that will be friendly to their means of doing business. As analysts predict a rebuke of the Trump administration, though, some industries such as AI, defense, and more are concerned that the winds may turn against them. From Reuters:

Nov 2 (Reuters) – As the US midterm elections approach, Corporate America is bracing for a potential shift in the balance of power in Washington.

In case of a strong Democratic showing, analysts and strategists say clean energy, housing and healthcare stocks could benefit, while AI, data center and defense names may draw greater scrutiny from lawmakers.

Of course, it's an open question how much a blue wave would really affect anything. Many Democrats have been doing their best to turn as far right as possible — Abdul El-Sayed and Zohran Mamdani stand as rare exceptions to the Tallaricos and Newsoms of the nation — so it's possible Dem leadership wouldn't rock the boat too hard.

BMW wants to include the necessary hardware for a Level 2 driver assist system on a huge range of its cars, starting once the company's software can match the hardware. The only problem? BMW only said this about the hardware, there's nothing about the software being included. From Automotive News:

BMW plans to launch a Level 2 driver-assistance system that can handle turns, lane changes and roundabouts in city traffic, with the required hardware becoming standard on several key model lines from 2027.

The automaker expects to launch the enhanced system in Germany, pending regulatory approval that is anticipated in early 2027, the automaker said in a statement on Nov. 2.

BMW plans to make the necessary hardware standard on the new iX3 and iX4, as well as all versions of the next-generation X5, 3 Series and 7 Series. Future vehicles based on the automaker's Neue Klasse architecture will also receive the equipment.

If this sounds familiar, every Tesla for years now has come with "all the hardware for full self-driving" despite that being patently untrue for many older vehicles. With automakers getting ever more interested in subscriptions, it's likely the hardware is all you'll get included in your purchase.

Prices are ever-dropping, it seems, though who knows if that rumor about nuking Iran will turn out to be true. Gotta nuke something.

#5716. Another Surgical Treatment for Back Pain Fails in Rigorous Analysis

— Norwegian registry data show no benefit from spine fusion as part of decompression surgery

Spinal fusion added to decompression surgery in patients with spinal stenosis did not provide any added benefit versus decompression alone, Norwegian national registry data indicated.

With outcomes compared for 1,446 patients undergoing decompression alone and 806 having spinal fusion as well, no significant difference in Oswestry Disability Index (ODI) scores was seen a year later, according to James H. McAuley, PhD, of Neuroscience Research Australia in Randwick, and colleagues.

This is not the first study to question the utility of spinal fusion for patients with degenerative stenosis, the group noted in their report appearing in Annals of Internal Medicine. Earlier, prospective trials in Sweden and Norway found no improvement in disability when compared with decompression surgery alone, as did a smaller U.S.-based study (although the latter did show better quality of life with fusion). Moreover, these trials all showed a higher burden of complications such as bleeding with spinal fusion, along with prolonged operative time and increased costs.

Nevertheless, McAuley and colleagues observed, including fusion in decompression procedures is now common practice in some countries, particularly the U.S. and Australia. One analysis in 2023 found that fully 90% of U.S. surgeries for degenerative spondylolisthesis in 2019 involved spine fusion -- a 23-point increase from just 3 years previously. McAuley's group had no explanation for this explosion in popularity, while the authors of the 2023 analysis pointed to "financial incentives, lack of guidelines and/or national regulations, and delayed evidence dissemination through medical education or professional societies."

Degenerative spondylolisthesis is a common feature of spinal stenosis; it involves a forward shift in a vertebra relative to those below it. Some surgeons have viewed this as indicative of instability that should be rectified via fusion, which seems plausible on its face but, as noted above, rigorous evidence for actual benefit is weak at best.

The current study sought to extend the findings from that Norwegian trial, called NORDSTEN-DS, by examining spinal stenosis patients' outcomes as recorded in the country's national health registry. They identified a total of some 3,900 patients undergoing surgery for degenerative spondylolisthesis from 2007 to 2021. NORDSTEN-DS participants' data were included in the registry and analyzed anew in the current study, which used a "target trial emulation" approach to compare patients undergoing versus not having fusion as part of decompression surgery. After excluding patients who, for example, were very elderly or had previous surgeries in the same spinal region, the investigators had 2,252 patients for detailed analysis, including 236 who were in NORDSTEN-DS.

Mean patient age was about 65 and roughly 70% were women. ODI scores at the time of surgery averaged 38.9 for those having decompression alone and 41.4 for the group undergoing fusion (higher scores indicate worse symptoms, and the maximum is 50).

At month 12 after surgery, mean ODI scores declined to 20.0 for the fusion group and 22.3 among those undergoing decompression alone. The mean difference of 2.2 points fell short of statistical significance (95% CI -1.6 to 6.1).

McAuley and colleagues also performed some subgroup analyses to see whether patients who would be more likely to benefit from fusion could be identified from baseline characteristics, such as age, sex, body mass index, or symptom severity. This effort came up empty, as no significant differences in ODI improvement emerged. However, it did show that men with obesity (BMI ≥30) had significantly worse outcomes versus men with lower BMI values.

And, as with earlier studies, fusion led to longer operative time (mean 85 extra minutes) and longer hospital stays (mean 1.9 extra days) compared with decompression alone. Although no particular complication stood out as more common with fusion, the risk for any type of complication was doubled overall. Risk for needing additional surgery because of complications was increased even more.

"This study calls into question the highly prevalent management of pain from lumbar degenerative spondylolisthesis with fusion," McAuley and colleagues wrote. "Our findings add to current evidence and should prompt reevaluation of current guidelines," which, they noted, are sparse and "offer conflicting recommendations."

Limitations to the study included the reliance on administrative records, the potential for confounding by unmeasured variables, and the 12-month window for examining outcomes, leaving open the possibility that fusion could have longer-term benefits.

#5715. What Do You Want To Know About The Electric Mercedes-AMG GT 4-Door Coupe?

Undoubtedly one of the most talked about new cars of this year is the 2027 Mercedes-AMG GT 4-Door Coupe, the first dedicated AMG EV. Upon its extravagant reveal in Los Angeles the new GT drummed up lots of chatter due to its divisive styling and advanced powertrain with an AMGFORCE S+ mode that turns on V8 noises and shifts. I've been eagerly awaiting getting behind the wheel, and last week I was in Spain to do just that. I got to drive the second-generation Mercedes-AMG GT 4-Door Coupe on track at Ascari and on the incredible roads between there and Málaga, and while I can't tell you what I think of it yet, I want to know all of your questions.

What do you want to know about the electric AMG GT? Are you curious about styling details and interior features, or do you want to learn more about its fancy axial-flux motors and new battery tech? I'm sure you have a lot of questions about those AMGFORCE noises, too. Head down into the comments below and I'll do my best to answer them, either in the replies for un-embargoed things, or in a few days when you're able to read my full review.

As a refresher, the new GT 4-Door Coupe is the first model to ride on Mercedes' AMG.EA platform, the second model being the recently revealed GT SUV. In GT55 form its tri-motor powertrain has a peak output of 805 horsepower and 1,372 pound-feet of torque, enough to send it from 0 to 60 mph in 2.4 seconds, while the GT63 ups that to 1,153 hp and 1,475 lb-ft, bringing the 0-60 time down to two seconds flat. (There will also be a dual-motor GT53, but I didn't get to drive it.) Both models can charge at 600 kW, which Mercedes demonstrated for us, able to charge the 106-kWh battery pack from 10% to 80% in 11 minutes.

Other standout features include an Active Ride Control suspension, optional aerodynamics like an underbody "venturi flow" and an extending diffuser, and Race Engineer dials that let you adjust throttle responsiveness, handling agility and traction, in case you want to get really sideways. Oh yeah, and it'll have an EPA range that gets close to 400 miles, maybe even exceeding that figure.

So, what do you want to know about the 2027 Mercedes-AMG GT 4-Door Coupe? Surely you all will have very normal, calm opinions about it.

#5714. Batteries Beat Norway’s Hydrogen Bulk Carriers on Cost in Two Modeled Routes

Norwegian shipping company GMI Group is moving ahead with four hydrogen-electric bulk carriers for coastal freight. In October 2026, PowerCell confirmed a follow-up order that brings the program to 24 marine fuel-cell units with 5.4 MW of combined installed capacity. The first two ships, rated at 4,000 deadweight tonnes, are planned for 2027, while two additional 3,000-tonne vessels are expected in 2029.

The project has real shipbuilding commitments and substantial public support. The question is whether hydrogen makes economic sense for the freight services these vessels will provide.

GMI says its design can deliver lifetime costs 9–10% below conventional ships. Its argument combines fuel cells, batteries, an efficient hull and wind assistance. Those are useful engineering features, but most are not exclusive to hydrogen propulsion. A diesel or battery-electric ship can have a similarly efficient hull and sails. The fuel and energy systems still need to be compared on equivalent terms.

I modeled two hypothetical 3,000-tonne coastal bulk services, covering 80 and 55 nautical miles, using comparable hull performance and operating requirements. Over fifteen years, hydrogen propulsion came out 50–58% more expensive than conventional diesel, even after assuming approximately €13.1 million in public vessel support was received upfront. Battery-electric alternatives were €11.64–14.13 million cheaper than the subsidized hydrogen vessels. These are scenario results, not estimates of GMI’s actual operating contracts.

The company has not published the route and commercial data needed for a direct assessment, and the model’s vessel acquisition prices remain important uncertainties. The battery alternatives were not the cheapest option under every assumption. In the central cases they cost somewhat more than diesel over fifteen years. Their advantage was the much smaller gap to conventional operation compared with hydrogen. Battery-electric propulsion also requires adequate grid connections, charging equipment and berth time, which cannot be assumed available at every Norwegian port.

Hydrogen has a different set of requirements. Renewable electricity must be converted into hydrogen, compressed, stored, delivered to the vessel and converted back to electricity in fuel cells. Those additional processes introduce energy losses and capital costs that efficient hulls and sails cannot erase. They matter particularly for short coastal voyages where a practical charging arrangement may be possible.

Norway is using substantial public funding to support the hydrogen ships. Norsk Hydrogenforum reports Enova awards totaling roughly €42.9 million for the four vessels. Additional public support has been awarded to GreenH hydrogen production projects. Announced grants are not necessarily fully paid, and a subsidy changes the allocation of costs rather than eliminating the equipment and energy required.

There may be coastal services where voyage length, available berth time or grid limitations favour hydrogen over batteries. That needs to be established through engineering and commercial comparisons, rather than inferred from the existence of a funded shipbuilding program.

Before further public commitments to compressed-hydrogen coastal freight, Norway should require credible battery-electric bids using vessel, charging and energy prices appropriate to the expected entry-into-service dates.

#5713. Rivian Is Doing The Damn Thing

There's never a guarantee a startup car company will actually make it. Automotive history is littered with companies that never made it more than a few years, and after some of Rivian's early financial troubles, it wasn't out of the question that the Irvine, California-based company could join that group. Well, following the introduction of the R2, those fears seem to be all but squashed.

Rivian just posted its third-quarter results for 2026, and the compact-crossover-sized R2 helped raise the automaker's deliveries by 46% year-over-year to 19,248 vehicles, according to Car and Driver. That means Rivian is increasing its yearly sales projection to between 67,000 and 70,000 vehicles — up from 65,000 to 70,000. It also just so happened to outsell the larger and more expensive R1S and R1T flagships combined, as well as the EDV delivery van. I should note that Rivian didn't explicitly say how many R2s it sold, though. This news comes even though the only R2 that Rivian currently sells is the most expensive Performance version of the electric SUV, and the idea that Rivian is still "early in the ramp" of R2 production, according to CEO RJ Scaringe, who spoke with Reuters.

Theoretically, things are only going to get better from here, sales-wise, with the only currently available R2 clocking in at $59,485, including destination. Eventually, a $55,485 Premium trim and $46,485 base model will join its ranks, but the only information we've got is "coming late 2026" and "coming spring 2027," respectively. Of course, even the most expensive R2 is a hell of a lot cheaper than the R1S and R1T, which both start well north of $80,000.

And good news keeps coming, because last week, Scaringe said he was "very confident" Rivian would hit an autonomy milestone this year that would unlock a quarter-billion-dollar tranche of investment money from Uber. That's part of an up-to $1.25 billion investment the rideshare company is making in the automaker through 2031, according to Reuters. It includes an initial $300 million and is contingent on several autonomy milestones. However, Rivian hasn't said exactly what those milestones are. Don't worry, though. Scaringe says Rivian is "right on time."

We do know that the partnership between Uber and Rivian targets the 2028 launch of a driverless R2 robotaxi on Uber's network — starting in a handful of cities and then expanding to about 25 over the next year and a half. Clearly, Rivian is aiming its R2 robotaxi directly at the Tesla Cybercab.

It needs to work, too. As Reuters points out, both the R2's success and the Uber partnership are imperative for making Rivian stick as a company. It's doing pretty well right now, don't get me wrong, but it's still burning through cash like the Bouvier Beale family, and it's looking for any way to narrow those losses.

No matter how you slice it, Rivian is truly showing it's a company that has the ability to stick around and be viable. Once R2 production is fully ramped up and the R3 is introduced, who knows where it could end up? All it needs to do is get that pesky cash burn problem under control.

#5712. Parents' Autoimmune Disease Linked to Kids' Type 1 Diabetes Risk

— Large Swedish study highlighted shared familial susceptibility

Parental autoimmune diseases were associated with an increased risk of type 1 diabetes in offspring, according to a Swedish registry cohort study.

In an analysis of nearly 1.5 million individuals, children born to mothers with autoimmune disease during pregnancy had a 57% higher risk of developing type 1 diabetes (adjusted HR 1.57, 95% CI 1.46-1.68), according to Xingbo Mo, PhD, of Karolinska Institutet in Stockholm, and colleagues. Paternal autoimmune disease before childbirth had a comparable association (adjusted HR 1.51, 95% CI 1.37-1.67).

When parents had type 1 diabetes, the magnitude of risk was higher. Maternal type 1 diabetes was linked with a more than fourfold risk increase (HR 4.51, 95% CI 3.72-5.48), whereas paternal type 1 diabetes was associated with an even higher hazard (HR 5.79, 95% CI 4.70-7.13), they reported in JAMA Pediatrics.

"A clear gradient in type 1 diabetes risk was observed according to parental type 1 diabetes status, with the highest risk among offspring with both parents affected," the authors wrote. "The largely comparable maternal and paternal associations suggest that shared familial susceptibility may play an important role in the intergenerational transmission of type 1 diabetes risk."

While maternal and paternal estimates showed no overall difference for composite autoimmune disease, researchers identified variations across specific disease categories. Paternal associations were stronger for endocrine conditions -- most notably autoimmune thyroiditis -- whereas maternal associations were stronger for ankylosing spondylitis and gastrointestinal autoimmune disorders.

"Stronger associations for endocrine autoimmune diseases, particularly autoimmune thyroiditis, are consistent with the established clustering of endocrine autoimmune disorders and their shared immune-genetic susceptibility," the researchers noted. Yet the "disease-specific heterogeneity suggests that selected conditions may also involve parent-specific or potential intrauterine influences," they added, which "support a multifactorial model of intergenerational autoimmune transmission."

In an accompanying editorial, Mikael Knip, MD, PhD, of Biomedicum Helsinki in Finland, emphasized that an "obvious" practical takeaway is that offspring of parents with autoimmune disease warrant monitoring for type 1 diabetes.

"Consensus guidelines have been published for screening and monitoring of individuals at risk for type 1 diabetes," Knip wrote, noting that while screening efforts "may be variable based on resources available," children with a familial history "represent a definite risk group for progression to clinical type 1 diabetes."

Benjamin Nwosu, MD, chief of endocrinology at Northwell Health Cohen Children's Medical Center in New York, who was not involved with the study, underscored the necessity of early detection.

"Currently, in our practice, we screen children with type 1 diabetes for autoimmune thyroid conditions and celiac disease, because it's been published that children with type 1 diabetes have higher risk for the development of celiac and autoimmune thyroid conditions," Nwosu said in a statement.

He pointed out that the authors "did not mention that 40 to 50% of the risk for type 1 diabetes is carried on what's called the major histocompatibility complex class 2 genes or loci."

"Once you know that, you know a parent who has type 1 diabetes has some possibility of passing this condition to the offspring, not in a Mendelian autosomal dominant fashion, because type 1 is caused by what's called a polygenic aggregation of genes. But the risk is higher than in unaffected parents," he said.

The researchers evaluated 1,455,645 children born in Sweden between 2001 and 2014, with follow-up extending through 2023 (median age was 15.3 years, and 51.4% were male). Overall, 6.1% of children were exposed to maternal autoimmune diseases before delivery.

After adjusting for unmeasured shared familial factors using cousin-comparison models, the maternal association remained statistically significant (adjusted HR 1.27, 95% CI 1.08-1.50), with higher risks specifically noted for maternal endocrine autoimmune disorders and maternal type 1 diabetes.

The study team noted several limitations, including the lack of register data regarding clinical disease severity, autoantibody levels, disease activity during pregnancy, or medication use. "Such factors may modify fetal immune programming and could not be evaluated in the present study," they acknowledged.

Nwosu also cautioned that the results might not fully translate globally, noting Sweden "is located in the northern latitudes, where type 1 diabetes is almost endemic."

#5711. A Referendum On Elon Musk And Transgender Rights — Who’d A Thunk It?

US voters rarely, if ever, get a chance to vote on someone whose name is not on the ballot, but this year is an exceptional one with Tesla CEO Elon Musk throwing millions at key races. In years past, Musk’s seal of approval would be a planet-saving badge of climate action honor for candidates seeking office. Now he’s just another highly privileged crank exercising his outsized financial power against transgender rights. Who’d a thunk it?

Coincidentally or not, Elon Musk’s highly personal vendetta against transgender rights has materialized against the backdrop of a corresponding focus in Republican Party campaign strategy, with one early hint being his “pronouns suck” message on Twitter in July of 2020.

Musk went on to buy Twitter and rename it “X” in 2022, and the rest is transphobic history.

Also coincidentally or not, the Republican Party has been hammering against human and civil rights for generations, but the razor-sharp focus on transgender persons did not bloom into full flower until 2021, after US President Donald Trump lost his first bid for re-election.

In the aftermath of Trump’s failed insurrection of January 6, the Party alighted upon transgender rights as a powerful tool in a coordinated campaign strategy aimed at ginning up the fevered emotions of voters already besotted with US President Trump’s “MAGA” propaganda during his first term in office.

In addition to working the conservative aisle of the US Supreme Court, the anti-transgender movement has focused on legislation introduced by state lawmakers (looking at you, Florida). Among the LGBTQ+ organizations tracking the bills is the organization is the Human Rights Campaign, which has identified a distinct shift in anti-transgender strategy during the 2020 campaign cycle.

All else being equal, transgender persons are an unlikely target for a national political campaign. According to one study, self-identified transgender persons over the age of 13 make up just 1% of the US population, with much of the self-reporting cohort under age 35. As for the much-ballyhooed danger of transgender-related surgery on minors, that is vanishingly rare to the point of not occurring at all according to a 2024 Harvard study. Somewhat ironically, the same study found a “substantially higher” rate of sex-affirming surgery among non-transgender minors and adults.

For that matter, Elon Musk is known to have availed himself of body-affirming treatments as an adult, but that’s a whole ‘nother can of worms.

Regardless of the numbers, the political payoff for anti-transgender activism is substantial. Prior to 2020, Republicans did not focus narrowly on transgender rights. Their attacks were a broad barrage against marriage equality for LGBTQ+ couples, as part of a relgion-centric campaign to eliminate legal and constitutional protections. Although this type of legislation continues to appear in statehouses, HRC identified a sharp rise in the number of bills specifically attacking transgender rights during the 2020 campaign cycle. The organization summarized its findings in a report last year, stating that “opponents of LGBTQ+ equality explicitly – and admittedly – began attacking transgender youth” (emphasis added).

In the report, HRC takes note of ADF (formerly Alliance Defending Freedom) and other anti-LGBTQ+ organizations serving as “bill mills,” that funnel pre-written legislation to state lawmakers in a coordinated campaign. So, if you’re wondering why gender-related health care for minors became such a hot button issue during the 2024 election cycle, alongside transgender participation in youth sports, there’s your answer.

Although some Democrats also contributed a share to the hate fest, the bulk of the coordinated anti-transgender campaign that poured out of state legislatures in 2021, and the following years, is credited to Republican lawmakers.

The anti-transgender strategy exceeded expectations in the 2024 election cycle, aided by a late-stage change in the Democratic ticket. Podcaster Joe Rogan, among other Trump-endorsing influencers, is also credited with swinging the Gen Z (ages 18-29) vote to the Republican Party, with spectacular results.

The public mood has soured since then, to say the least. Most people may or may not ever encounter a transgender person in their whole life, but they encounter Trump every time they go to fill up their car with gas.

Still, with the well known anti-transgender activist Elon Musk contributing almost half a billion dollars (and counting) to support Republican candidates in key races around the country, transgender rights are still on the ballot — and now the whole strategy is backfiring.

CleanTechnica’s Carolyn Fortuna took note of the Great Backfiring over the weekend, observing that “Musk’s blatant attempts to influence election results are getting a lot of recent analysis and condemnation.”

“US Federal Election Commission data revealed this week that Elon Musk has donated at least $445 million into Republican-aligned political action committees and groups since last year. Interestingly, it seems that Musk’s deep influence isn’t enough, and he is worried,” Fortuna explained.

“If Democrats win big on Tuesday in midterm elections, Musk insists US democracy will come to an ‘end,” she continued, citing this message on X last Friday, October 30, from Elon Musk:

“This would result in America becoming a permanent one-party state and the end of democracy in any meaningful sense of the word. All that is needed to ensure this doesn’t happen is 51 Republican senators in 2029.”

Despite Musk’s millions, the effort is having little effect in the run-up to Election Day. Take Texas, for example, where Musk has devoted his millions, and his amplified voice, working the anti-transgender message to support Ken Paxton for US Senate against the Democratic candidate James Talerico.

Don’t just take my word for it. The well known independent journalist Judd Legum is among those taking notice. “Musk is blanketing the internet with digital ads falsely accusing Democratic Senate candidates of supporting ‘taxpayer-funded sex changes for kids,’” Legum reported on September 23.

In Texas, the attack ads are just one prong of a transgender-centric attack strategy that includes raising “questions” about Talerico’s sexual identity. The Talerico campaign, for one, is having none of it. They are turning the anti-transgender strategy back on Musk. Nothing will be certain until the dust settles after election day, but so far Musk’s millions have failed to move the needle for Paxton in Texas, where according to late breaking polls Talerico leads by a slim margin.

As reported by Houston Public Media, Talerico has been regularly blasting Musk’s influence in the Texas midterms in campaign speeches, tying the world’s richest man to a Republican candidate who drags a considerable amount of scandal and corruption baggage.

As for Musk’s interest in Tesla, not to worry. Despite Elon Musk’s personal and political baggage, the iconic automotive startup continues to hold the #1 position in EV sales in the US by a wide margin. On the other hand, General Motors and Ford are rebooting their EV efforts in anticipation of a friendlier political environment next year and beyond. Other legacy automakers, including Toyota and Subaru, have also introduced new EV models to the US market this year. In addition, Rivian and other pure play EV startups are contributing to the rising competition against Tesla for EV sales in the US. Hold on to your hats…
