#5710. Driving A 43-Horsepower Geo Metro Convertible From New York To Los Angeles Looks Really Miserable

The Geo Metro Convertible is one of the silliest cars ever sold in America. The Metro hatchback was already a misfit on American roads, with its microscopic 1.0-liter three-cylinder engine, 1,600-pound curb weight, and $7,000-ish starting price. It was a General Motors rebadge of a Suzuki Swift, and it almost immediately became the butt of every automotive joke. There was no logical reason, then, for any engineer to lop off its roof and turn it into a convertible, but that didn't stop it from happening, and honestly I'm grateful GM sold them anyway. I doubt GM ever imagined that a YouTuber would end up driving one from New York to Los Angeles 34 years after it was built, but that's just what Mitchell Kohrmann did in his latest video.

When new, the Geo Metro convertible was fabled to have 55 horsepower and 58 pound-feet of torque, but Kohrmann was smart enough to dyno test his remarkably clean 1992 Metro convertible before setting off on his cross-continental journey. Before that, though, he gave it a tune-up and fixed the air conditioning — or at least he thought he did... After a 50-second pull, the dyno showed the Metro made 43 horsepower and 53 lb-ft of torque. That's more than enough to complete a 2,838-mile road trip that includes a 10,000-foot climb, right?

I had never seen any of Kohrmann's videos before watching this one, but looking at his other uploads, he's no stranger to long drives, including a 10,050-mile drive to the Arctic Ocean in a Touareg V10 diesel, and a 6,500-mile drive in a V10 Audi S8. Those are both leather-lined luxury cruisers, though, and going from that to a vinyl-topped three-cylinder economy car must feel like quite the downgrade. With just 43 horsepower, Kohrmann ends up keeping the little Metro's gas pedal in contact with the floor a lot more than I expected.

The calamities begin before he even starts his journey, since he realized the Geo's tires may have looked fine at first, but they were utterly ancient, so he had to source some 155/80R13 tires at the last minute. Fortunately he finds some that will fit, and sets off on the drive. Predictably, the drive into and out of Manhattan involves navigating excruciating traffic jams, but once he gets out away from New York, he starts covering more ground.

This video is about 1.5 hours long, and includes lots of POV shots as well as breakdown and repair content and tons of beautiful scenery. Kohrmann initially intended to attempt to beat some sub-1.0-liter engine Cannonball Run record of 48 hours, 9 minutes and 53 seconds that was set in 1964 by a Sunbeam Imp, but he gives up on that after a series of setbacks. Regardless, he still completes the journey in 49 hours, 11 minutes and 18 seconds, after a harrowing journey filled with twists, turns, breakdowns, and hellish traffic jams. I think it's worth a watch. Cheers to you, Mitchell, for following through on what looks like an utterly miserable journey, but a really cool video.

#5709. Jackery Launches Explorer 100D Portable Power Station. Maximum Capability For Carryon Compatibility

Jackery just launched the Explorer 100D portable power station. This is Jackery’s smallest battery, giving a new level of versatility to power many of the devices people rely on most. Meanwhile, it integrates  the durability and safety people expect from Jackery’s larger models into a compact case.

The Explorer 100D has a capacity of 96 Wh (30,000mAh) which puts it just below the carryon limit for domestic and international flights. Meanwhile, up to 158 W of output is more than enough to charge a laptop. The 100D is compatible with advanced protocols including AVS, PD 3.1 and PPS, providing precise charging, particularly for Apple products.

The Explorer 100D measures 4.6” X 3.4” X 3.4”(11.6 X  8.3 cm X  8.5 cm), or about the size of a softball. It weighs 1.87 lbs. (850 g). This is a bit larger than the typical power bank, but much smaller than the typical solar generator. It is also smaller and lighter than the Explorer 100 Plus that it replaces, while also increasing in power output.

On the test sample model, I found the form factor very convenient. It is not small enough to fit in your pants pocket, but the rectangular case with rounded corners was easy to fit in a backpack or carryon bag. A cloth handle makes it easy to hold, and a shoulder strap is available. I had a credentials lanyard from a previous media event on hand, and it worked fine as a strap for the lightweight battery.

On the front of the battery are two 140 W USB-C ports and an 18 W USB-A port. The left USB-C port can both charge the battery and discharge to another device. The battery can also be discharged through the other two ports while it charges. The battery does not come with a charger, although a USB-C cable is provided. I was able to charge the Explorer 100D on a wide range of chargers, although lower powered chargers take a while to charge the large capacity battery.

The front display default shows battery charge percentage as well as input and output power. However, you can switch the display to also show power output to each port or battery health, cycles and temperature.

Unlike some of Jackery’s larger batteries, the 100D does not have cooling fans or vents in the case. The LFP battery is rated for 3000 full charge cycles, good for 8 years of daily use. This is far more durable than many Li-ion power banks, which commonly only offer a few hundred charge cycles. It is also a 50% improvement over the previous Explorer 100 Plus model that it replaces. Of note, while this model does comply with China’s strict CCC (China Compulsory Certification), the logo was not printed on the test model I received, although that may change in the future. Without the logo present, it could be taken away if travelling in China. However, the battery did have FCC and SGS certification printed on it, which are more relevant to most US customers.

The portable battery can function as a solar generator by accepting direct solar input up to 100 W. This makes it a bit different than many power banks. Solar panels connect through the USB-C charge port. which may require the purchase of an adaptor, depending on the panel used. Jackery’s lightweight, folding SolarSaga 100 Air could charge the battery in less than two hours. The battery can also take 12 V or 24V input from a car, boat or RV. Using a traditional USB-C charger, the Explorer 100D can accept up to 140 W of input power.

The Explorer 100D  test model came with 30% charge and reached a full charge in about an hour. While the battery was charging, I was able to charge my phone and another power bank off the output ports while the battery was charging. I initially charged the battery off the HomePower 1000 Plus V2, which has a 140 W USB-C output. With similar styling, it looked like a baby of the larger battery when placed on top. Of course, the battery would take longer to charge on lower power. However, it could be used to accumulate charge over time and discharge more quickly for a device like a laptop that needs more power than what may be available from a charge jack at a hotel room or airport.

While the battery will often be used to charge phones, the Explorer 100D ran my T-Mobile Wi-Fi router all day, with capacity left to charge my phone and tablet. When used with a laptop with a USB-C charge port, the small battery could power a remote office.

I was also able to run a small personal fan off of the Explorer 100D. With peak power draw at 5 watts, it didn’t pose much of a challenge for the battery. This would come in handy at an outdoor event or while fishing on a hot day.

On Halloween, I used the battery to power an old Bluetooth speaker for hours of handing out treats, while keeping our phones charged. The black and orange design fits well with the holiday decorations. By the end of the night, the battery still had over 90% capacity.

I also used the battery on a trip to an apple harvest festival. While charging ports were in short supply in the car, the Explorer 100D kept phone and camera batteries topped up. The rubber feet kept the battery from shifting around when placed on the center console.

The Jackery Explorer 100D launches as a two-unit bundle for $179.99 at select Costco warehouse locations from the start of November 2026. Individual units will then become available at Walmart for $99 on November 15th, just in time for holiday travel.

Frost & Sullivan recently named Jackery the World’s No.1 Solar Generator Brand. Jackery leads in solar generator sales volume globally and has pioneered multiple innovations in portable power. The Explorer 100D is the latest example. The company has a focus on providing solutions for customer needs with durable products that have exceeded my expectations in testing. After trying the Explorer 100D, I also find it to be more useful than I originally expected, using it in a wide range of applications. While the Explorer 100D clearly is not going to solve all of the world’s energy problems, it provides another power option that meets the needs of many customers while integrating well with Jackery’s expanding range of solar and energy storage products. As Steven Wang, CEO of Jackery Americas puts it:

Power support is often most effective when it’s highly portable and backed by comprehensive compatibility and a long lifespan. Developed for peak productivity, the Explorer 100D represents years of honed design and feature implementation, while maintaining focus on the top-quality engineering and unmatched consistency that make Jackery portable power stations must-haves for work and travel. Thanks to that collection of refined expertise, it’s among the longest-lasting ultra-portable batteries ever.

I look forward to putting the Explorer 100D to the test during some upcoming travel over the next few months. I also look forward to seeing what Jackery comes up with next in versatile, portable power.

Larry lives in Queens, NY, with his wife and cats. While he has spent much of his professional career leading global marketing efforts, his passions focus on clean technology and the automotive industry. He believes in Green Free Trade and the potential for the application of engineering talent to create solutions to the world’s most challenging problems.

#5708. Yamaha Refuses To Race In Qatar Despite MotoGP's Penalty Threats

Despite concerns that President Donald Trump will restart hostilities in the U.S. war against Iran after the midterm elections, MotoGP must go on. But Yamaha announced Sunday that it will not travel to the Lusail International Circuit for next weekend's Qatar Grand Prix. The Japanese manufacturer cited a company-wide policy currently barring travel to the Middle East. In response, MotoGP threatened Yamaha with contractual and sporting implications.

Yamaha's absence means that its two factory motorcycles and Pramac's two satellite bikes will not compete in Qatar. The company stated, "This decision reflects Yamaha's assessment of the information currently available and is independent of the measures implemented by MotoGP Group and the event organizer." While the safety of the team's employees should be paramount, Yamaha didn't have much to lose competitively. The factory team and Pramac are in the last two spots in the teams' standings. Yamaha expects to return for the next round in Portugal, but the manufacturer might not be allowed to rejoin.

MotoGP's response offered few details about the implied financial and competitive consequences, but the championship's organizers stated that every other team committed to racing in Qatar. Don't be deceived by the release, though. The riders have been far from content with the situation they have been forced into.

Riders first held a secret meeting on the matter at Phillip Island ahead of the Australian Grand Prix. It's safe to say management didn't take it well. Last week, The Race reported that MotoGP refused to even give the riders a meeting room at Sepang last weekend. Instead, they reconvened inside Yamaha hospitality. MotoGP also threatened to ban any riders who boycott Qatar from the final two rounds in Portugal and Spain. According to Motorsport.com, outgoing Pramac rider Jack Miller revealed:

"I don't want to go. Like everybody. There's 19 of us that don't want to go there. I don't think we should be racing like that. We've made our opinion very clear on that. We'll see what the decision is.

As riders, we've united and done the best we can, and threatened the best we can with what ammunition we have. Not only for us as riders, but also other people in the championship. You speak to 50% of the paddock, nobody wants to go. I don't understand why we need to. I don't understand."

Some riders are finding their own ways out of racing in Qatar without consequences. Alex Marquez elected to undergo surgery to repair his right shoulder, which he injured in May. He aims to miss only Qatar. It wouldn't be surprising if other riders with lingering injuries also withdraw.

No matter what assurances MotoGP made to all 22 riders, there's no way it could guarantee everyone's safe passage out of Qatar should things escalate in the region further. There are hundreds of people who work in the paddock across MotoGP, Moto2 and Moto3. Organizers also can't prevent the worst-case scenario of Lusail Circuit itself being the target of an Iranian drone or missile strike, and the track sits 27 miles away from Al Udeid Air Base, the largest U.S. military base in the Middle East.

#5707. CTE in NFL Players: What a New Study Tells Us -- And What It Doesn't

— Does the new research mean 3 in 4 football players are at risk?

A statistical model that extrapolated data from deceased National Football League (NFL) brain donors to include all former NFL players who died from 2008 through 2023 estimated that nearly three in four had chronic traumatic encephalopathy (CTE).

Yorghos Tripodis, PhD, of the Boston University School of Public Health, and colleagues developed the model by quantifying cognitive symptoms of NFL brain donors who had CTE assessments. CTE can be identified only through autopsy.

After adjusting for bias associated with brain donation, the model estimated the prevalence of CTE at death was 74.5% (95% CI 69.6-78.3) for all former NFL players who died from 2008 through 2023, Tripodis and colleagues reported in JAMA Network Open.

The analysis incorporated a selection ratio to estimate the prevalence of CTE. The ratio assumed that brain donation was driven primarily by family members observing cognitive symptoms in former NFL players.

"This is an interesting attempt to statistically account for the problem of selective donation into a brain bank so that they can estimate the prevalence of CTE among all NFL players who died during the time interval they were looking at when they only could analyze a subset of them," observed Marc Weisskopf, PhD, ScD, of the Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health in Boston, who wasn't involved with the study.

The numbers don't necessarily apply to all NFL players, he pointed out. "The deaths they are working with are about 10% of all that could have occurred in that period, and there are reasons to suspect CTE would be less common among the still-living players when they eventually die," Weisskopf told MedPage Today.

"The statistical approach is sound, although it's important to realize that it is very dependent on some assumptions -- notably that brain donation is entirely driven by cognitive symptoms and that those are captured by dementia on the death certificate and their adjustment for additional mild cognitive impairment," he said.

CTE has four distinct stages, with higher CTE stages correlating with more severe cognitive symptoms. "Even if we were able to determine an unbiased estimate of the proportion of living and deceased players with CTE, we still don't have information on their probable CTE stage, which has the most meaning for their everyday lives and health," noted Rachel Grashow, PhD, MS, also of Harvard, who also wasn't involved with the study.

The study findings do not apply to other groups, observed Julie Stamm, PhD, of the University of Wisconsin-Madison, who wasn't part of the study. "It does not provide an estimate of CTE among athletes in other sports or at lower levels of play," Stamm told MedPage Today.

"Greater lifetime repetitive head impact exposure has been linked to an increased risk of developing CTE, so it is likely that the prevalence would be higher among NFL players than those who played at lower levels," she added. "However, we cannot estimate the prevalence among players at lower levels at this time."

Tripodis and colleagues used UNITE brain bank data and national death index records to evaluate former NFL players who died from 2008 through 2023. The mean duration of play was 5.1 years and 41.1% of deceased players were ages 80 or older when they died.

Of 2,362 deceased former NFL players, 396 were brain donors and 1,966 were nondonors. Of 387 donors, 362 (93.5%) had CTE at autopsy. The estimated prevalence of all-cause dementia was 27.2% (95% CI 22.6-31.6).

The median selection ratio was 4.97 (95% CI 4.72-5.26), meaning that former players with cognitive symptoms were five times more likely to have their brains donated by their family than those without these symptoms.

"The findings of this cross-sectional study suggest that selection bias materially inflates donor-based prevalence estimates but is unlikely to fully explain the high burden of CTE neuropathology among this population," Tripodis and co-authors stated.

Studies like this can refocus attention to the "20,000 living former NFL players, some of whom are suffering with cognitive symptoms or have been diagnosed with dementia," Grashow told MedPage Today. The Football Players Health Study at Harvard has more than 5,000 former players in its cohort, she noted.

"In our conversations with and ongoing study of these NFL players, we've found that head injury increases risk for treatable conditions that -- if left untreated -- increases risk for cognitive issues and dementia. These include sleep apnea, high blood pressure, low testosterone, depression, anxiety, tinnitus, and more," she added.

"So, if we are thinking about how these recent findings relate to the many thousands of former living players, the story shifts to one of surveilling and treating former players for treatable conditions so we can reduce their dementia and heart disease risk," Grashow said. "This, we believe, is one of the best ways we can tackle the potential symptoms, risks, and challenges of CTE right now -- before we turn to estimating prevalence, which is at the end of life."

#5706. Here's What The Original Tesla Roadster Cost In 2008 Vs. What It's Worth Today

The unveiling of the upcoming Tesla Roadster's new design has once again put the nameplate at the forefront of automotive discourse after the prototype stunned fans with its incredible ability to lift off the ground. So you'd imagine it's also made people take a closer look at its predecessor, the original Tesla Roadster that laid the groundwork so the current experiment could soar into the sky.

Revealed in 2006 and brought to market in 2008, the Tesla Roadster earned critical plaudits for being the first production EV to offer a usable driving range (of up to 244 miles). Performance was also noteworthy, as the Lotus Elise-based electric sports car could sprint to 60 mph in less than four seconds, thanks to its gobs of instant electric torque. With this, the Roadster not only won over buyers — a total of 2,450 during its lifetime — but also dispelled preconceived notions about electric cars being slow and impractical.

Then again, being a groundbreaker doesn't come cheap, as the 2008 Tesla Roadster's price shows. The luxury sports car went on sale for $98,000, which is equivalent to around $153,000 in 2026, and today, the amount you'll need to pay for a concours condition example will be close to the car's original MSRP. According to Hagerty, concours Roadsters carry eye-watering valuations of between $85,633 and $97,767, while examples in excellent condition start at a shade over $66,000 and top out at $79,567. If you're bargain hunting and would rather have something cheaper, those in good condition sell from $45,567 to $58,967, while fair-condition Roadsters are available for even less, at between $27,167 and $39,433.

Several factors contribute to the original Tesla Roadster's impressive value retention, with brand kudos, its reputation as the car that brought EVs into the mainstream, and the symbolic weight it carries as the car that launched Tesla being some of the biggest. Beyond its historical legacy, the original Roadster enjoys relative rarity, considering Tesla only made 2,450 units between the 2008 and 2012 model years. This means that used models are hard to come by, even on leading online marketplaces.

A quick glance at what's currently available on CarGurus shows a lone 19,000-mile example priced at $81,600, and it does look like prices are around this range at the moment. One recently went for $77,000 on Bring a Trailer. So, while they fetch good money for owners whenever they appear on the market, Tesla Roadsters usually sell significantly below their new price in 2008. This is not to say all Roadsters have depreciated. The limited-edition "Signature One Hundred" models, for example, have become astronomically valuable. We've already seen a Tesla Roadster with under 1,000 miles sell for $250,000, and plenty of other Roadsters like it have done well over the past few years, too, fetching well over $100,000 from buyers.

Heck, even the regular Roadsters have been pretty pricey in the past, too. According to Hagerty's data, these cars were at their high point back in 2023 when concours-perfect models fetched around $164,000, while examples in excellent condition floated around for $131,000. In good condition, Roadsters charged $95,300 then, with models in fair condition costing $61,300.
